## I / 一
===Original===

A Hostage

“It is a fair sight.”

“It may be a fair sight in a Roman’s eyes, Beric, but nought could be fouler to those of a Briton. To me every one of those blocks of brick and stone weighs down and helps to hold in bondage this land of ours; while that temple they have dared to rear to their gods, in celebration of their having conquered Britain, is an insult and a lie. We are not conquered yet, as they will some day know to their cost. We are silent, we wait, but we do not admit that we are conquered.”

“I agree with you there. We have never fairly tried our strength against them. These wretched divisions have always prevented our making an effort to gather; Cassivelaunus and some of the Kentish tribes alone opposed them at their first landing, and he was betrayed and abandoned by the tribes on the north of the Thames. It has been the same thing ever since. We fight piecemeal; and while the Romans hurl their whole strength against one tribe the others look on with folded hands. Who aided the Trinobantes when the Romans defeated them and established themselves on that hill? No one. They will eat Britain up bit by bit.”

“Then you like them no better for having lived among them, Beric?”

“I like them more, but I fear them more. One cannot be four years among them, as I was, without seeing that in many respects we might copy them with advantage. They are a great people. Compare their splendid mansions and their regular orderly life, their manners and their ways, with our rough huts, and our feasts, ending as often as not with quarrels and brawls. Look at their arts, their power of turning stone into lifelike figures, and above all, the way in which they can transfer their thoughts to white leaves, so that others, many many years hence, can read them and know all that was passing, and what men thought and did in the long bygone. Truly it is marvellous.”

“You are half Romanized, Beric,” his companion said roughly.

===Chinese===

人质

“这景象倒真壮观。”

“在罗马人眼里也许是壮观，贝里克，可在一个不列颠人眼里，再没有比它更丑恶的了。依我看，那一块块砖石，每一块都在往下压，帮着把咱们这片土地摁在枷锁里；他们还敢给他们的神祇起那座庙，庆贺他们征服了不列颠——那是一记侮辱，也是一个谎。咱们还没有被征服，这一点，他们总有一天会付出代价才知道。咱们沉默着，咱们等着，可咱们从不认这个‘被征服’的账。”

“这一点我同意你。咱们还从没有真正同他们较量过力气。倒霉的内讧，一直拦着咱们把力量聚起来：他们头一回登岸，只有卡西维劳努斯和肯特几支部落抵了敌，泰晤士河以北的部落还出卖了他、撇下了他。打那以后，回回都是这一套：咱们零敲碎打地打；罗马人把全部力气砸向一个部落，其余的袖着手看。罗马人打败特里诺班特人、在那座山上站住脚的时候，谁帮了他们？没有一个人。他们要把不列颠一口一口吃掉。”

“这么说，在他们中间住了这些年，你也没多喜欢他们一分，贝里克？”

“多喜欢了几分，可也更怕了几分。像我在他们中间那样住上四年，不可能看不见：许多方面，咱们学他们，只有好处。他们是个了不起的民族。把他们那些气派的宅邸、规矩有序的日子、他们的礼数和做派，同咱们的粗棚子比一比，同咱们那些十回有九回以吵骂斗殴收场的酒宴比一比。再看看他们的手艺，把石头变成活人一般模样的本事；尤其是，他们能把心思搬到白色的叶子上，好叫旁人——许多许多年以后的旁人——拿起来读，就全知道从前发生过什么、那些早已逝去的人想过什么、做过什么。说真的，这是神乎其技。”

“你半个身子叫罗马人化了，贝里克。”他的同伴粗声说。

===Original===

“I think not,” the other said quietly; “I should be worse than a fool had I lived, as I have done, a hostage among them for four years without seeing that there is much to admire, much that we could imitate with advantage, in their life and ways; but there is no reason because they are wiser and far more polished, and in many respects a greater people than we, that they should come here to be our masters. These things are desirable, but they are as nothing to freedom. I have said that I like them more for being among them. I like them more for many reasons. They are grave and courteous in their manner to each other; they obey their own laws; every man has his rights; and while all yield obedience to their superiors, the superiors respect the rights of those below them. The highest among them cannot touch the property or the life of the lowest in rank. All this seems to me excellent; but then, on the other hand, my blood boils in my veins at the contempt in which they hold us; at their greed, their rapacity, their brutality, their denial to us of all rights. In their eyes we are but savages, but wild men, who may be useful for tilling the ground for them, but who, if troublesome, should be hunted down and slain like wild beasts. I admire them for what they can do; I respect them for their power and learning; but I hate them as our oppressors.”

“That is better, Beric, much better. I had begun to fear that the grand houses and the splendour of these Romans might have sapped your patriotism. I hate them all; I hate changes; I would live as we have always lived.”

“But you forget, Boduoc, that we ourselves have not been standing still. Though our long past forefathers, when they crossed from Gaul wave after wave, were rude warriors, we have been learning ever since from Gaul as the Gauls have learned from the Romans, and the Romans themselves admit that we have advanced greatly since the days when, under their Caesar, they first landed here. Look at the town on the hill there. Though ’tis Roman now ’tis not changed so much from what it was under that great king Cunobeline, while his people had knowledge of many things of which we and the other tribes of the Iceni knew nothing.”

===Chinese===

“我看不是，”另一个平静地说，“我在他们那里当了四年人质，要是还看不见他们的生活做派里有许许多多可敬、可学的东西，那我还不如个傻子。可是，他们比咱们聪明些、文雅些，许多方面比咱们强大些，并不等于他们就该跑来当咱们的主子。这些东西固然好，可在自由面前，一文不值。我说过，在他们中间住过，我更喜欢他们了。喜欢他们，有许许多多缘故：他们彼此之间庄重有礼；他们遵自己的法；人人有各人的权利；下级服从上级，上级也尊重下级的权利。他们中间最高的，也动不得最低者的财产和性命。这一切，我看都好极了。可另一头呢，一想到他们怎样瞧不起咱们，我的血就在血管里滚；想到他们的贪、他们的抢、他们的凶，想到他们把一切权利都不给咱们。在他们眼里，咱们不过是野人、蛮子，有用的，可以给他们种地；捣乱的，就该当野兽一样围猎了杀掉。我佩服他们的本事；我敬重他们的力量和学问；可我恨他们——恨他们这帮压在咱们头上的人。”

“这还差不多，贝里克，好得多了。我都开始担心了，怕这些罗马人的华堂大宅，把你的爱国心磨没了。我恨他们全伙；我恨变动；我只想照咱们一向过的日子过下去。”

“可你忘了，波杜克，咱们自己也没有站着不动。虽说咱们的远祖，当年从高卢一波一波渡过来的时候，不过是些粗野的武人，打那以后，咱们一直在向高卢学，正如高卢人向罗马人学；连罗马人自己也承认，从他们的恺撒头一回登上这片土地到今天，咱们长进了老大一截。瞧瞧山上那座城。虽说如今归了罗马，同库诺贝林那位伟大君王治下相比，也还没换掉多少；他的人民懂得的东西，有许多是咱们和爱西尼别的部落闻所未闻的。”

===Original===

“What good did it do them?” the other asked scornfully; “they lie prostrate under the Roman yoke. It was easy to destroy their towns while we, who have few towns to destroy, live comparatively free. Look across at Camalodunum, Cunobeline’s capital. Where are the men who built the houses, who dressed in soft garments, who aped the Romans, and who regarded us as well nigh savage men? Gone every one of them; hewn down on their own hearthstones, or thrust out with their wives and families to wander homeless﻿—is there one left of them in yonder town? Their houses they were so proud of, their cultivated fields, their wealth of all kinds has been seized by the Romans. Did they fight any better for their Roman fashions? Not they; the kingdom of Cunobeline, from the Thames to the western sea, fell to pieces at a touch and it was only among the wild Silures that Caractacus was able to make any great resistance.”

“But we did no better, Boduoc; Ostorius crushed us as easily as Claudius crushed the Trinobantes. It is no use our setting ourselves against change. All that you urge against the Trinobantes and the tribes of Kent the Silures might urge with equal force against us. You must remember that we were like them not so many ages back. The intercourse of the Gauls with us on this eastern sea coast, and with the Kentish tribes, has changed us greatly. We are no longer, like the western tribes, mere hunters living in shelters of boughs and roaming the forests. Our dress, with our long mantles, our loose vests and trousers, differs as widely from that of these western tribes as it does from the Romans. We live in towns, and if our houses are rude they are solid. We no longer depend solely on the chase, but till the ground and have our herds of cattle. I daresay there were many of our ancestors who set themselves as much against the Gaulish customs as you do against those of the Romans; but we adopted them, and benefited by them, and though I would exult in seeing the last Roman driven from our land, I should like after their departure to see us adopt what is good and orderly and decent in their customs and laws.”

Beric’s companion growled a malediction upon everything Roman.

===Chinese===

“那给了他们什么好处？”另一个讥诮地问，“他们平趴在罗马的轭底下。毁了他们的城，倒还容易；咱们呢，没几座城好毁，日子过得相比之下倒自由。你望望卡马卢杜努姆，库诺贝林的都城。当年盖房子的那些人、穿软衣裳的那些人、学罗马人派头的那些人、拿咱们当半个野人看的那些人，都哪儿去了？一个不剩：剁倒在各家的灶台边，或者连同妻儿老小一起被撵出去，无家可归地流浪——那边的城里，可还剩下一个？他们引以为豪的房子、开熟了的田地、各样的家财，都叫罗马人占了。他们学了罗马的时髦，仗就多打胜了一场么？才不呢。库诺贝林的王国，从泰晤士河到西海，一戳就散了架；只有到了西尔人的荒山里，卡拉塔库斯才算顶出了一场硬仗。”

“可咱们也没高明多少，波杜克；奥斯托利乌斯碾咱们，同克劳狄乌斯碾特里诺班特人一样容易。同变动作对，没有用处。你数落特里诺班特人和肯特部落的那些话，西尔人也可以一字不差地数落咱们。你得记着，没多少代以前，咱们同他们一个样。高卢人同咱们这片东海滨、同肯特各部落的往来，把咱们改了许多。咱们不再像西边那些部落，单是猎人，住在树枝搭的窝棚里，在林子里转悠。咱们的衣裳——长斗篷、宽松的背心、裤腿——同西边部落差得远，同罗马人也差得远。咱们住在城镇里，房子虽说粗，却结实。咱们不再光指着打猎，也种地，也有成群的牛。我料想，当年咱们的祖先里，也大有把高卢风俗恨得像你恨罗马风俗一样的人；可咱们学了，学了占了便宜。所以，我固然巴望着最后一个罗马人被赶出咱们的土地那一天，我也巴望着他们走后，咱们能把他们风俗法度里那些好的、有秩序的、体面的学过来。”

贝里克的同伴低声咒了一句，把罗马的一切捎带了个遍。

===Original===

“There is one thing certain,” he said after a pause, “either they must go altogether, not only here but everywhere﻿—they must learn, as our ancestors taught them at their two first invasions, that it is hopeless to conquer Britain﻿—or they will end by being absolute masters of the island, and we shall be their servants and slaves.”

“That is true enough,” Beric agreed; “but to conquer we must be united, and not only united but steadfast. Of course I have learned much of them while I have been with them. I have come to speak their language, and have listened to their talk. It is not only the Romans who are here whom we have to defeat, it is those who will come after them. The power of Rome is great; how great we cannot tell, but it is wonderful and almost inconceivable. They have spread over vast countries, reducing peoples everywhere under their dominion. I have seen what they call maps showing the world as far as they know it, and well nigh all has been conquered by them; but the farther away from Rome the more difficulty have they in holding what they have conquered.

“That is our hope here; we are very far from Rome. They may send army after army against us, but in time they will get weary of the loss and expense when there is so little to gain, and as after their first invasions a long time elapsed before they again troubled us, so in the end they may abandon a useless enterprise. Even now the Romans grumble at what they call their exile, but they are obstinate and tenacious, and to rid our land of them for good it would be necessary for us not only to be united among ourselves when we rise against them, but to remain so, and to oppose with our whole force the fresh armies they will bring against us.

“You know how great the difficulties will be, Boduoc; we want one great leader whom all the tribes will follow, just as all the Roman legions obey one general; and what chance is there of such a man arising﻿—a man so great, so wise, so brave, that all the tribes of Britain will lay aside their enmities and jealousies, and submit themselves to his absolute guidance?”

===Chinese===

“有一桩是肯定的，”他顿了顿说，“要么他们干脆全滚——不光这里，是处处都滚——得像咱们的祖先在他们头两回入侵时教训他们的那样，叫他们明白，征服不列颠没有指望；要么，他们早晚当定这座岛的全权主子，咱们做他们的仆役和奴隶。”

“这话不假，”贝里克同意，“可要赢，咱们就得拧成一股绳；不光拧成一股绳，还得咬牙撑到底。我在他们那里，自然学了不少。我学会了他们的话，听惯了他们的谈论。咱们要打败的，不光是眼下在这里的这些罗马人，还有他们后头会来的。罗马的势力大；大到什么地步，咱们说不清，可它大得出奇，几乎叫人没法想。他们铺开了大大的疆土，处处把各民族压在脚下。我见过他们叫做‘地图’的东西，画着他们所知道的世界——几乎全叫他们征服了；不过离罗马越远，他们要按住征服到手的东西，就越吃力。

“这正是咱们在这里的指望：咱们离罗马远得很。他们可以一军接一军地派来打咱们，可日子久了，进项这样少、损耗那样大，他们会腻的；就像头几回入侵之后，隔了许久他们才又来招惹咱们，到头来，他们兴许会撂下这桩无用的买卖。就是现在，罗马人也在抱怨他们所谓的‘流放’了；可他们又倔又缠，要把他们连根撵走，咱们不光起事的时候要齐心，事后还得齐心，还得倾尽全力，顶住他们再开来的大军。

“你知道难处有多大，波杜克。咱们要的是一位大首领，一个众部落都肯跟的人，就像罗马的军团个个听一位将令一样。可这样的人，从哪儿冒出来？一个那样伟大、那样明智、那样勇敢的人，叫全不列颠的部落都撇开宿怨猜忌，把自己整个交给他调遣——有这样的指望么？”

===Original===

“If we wait for that, Beric, we may wait forever,” Boduoc said in a sombre tone, “at any rate it is not while we are tranquil under the Roman heel that such a man could show himself. If he is to come to the front it must be in the day of battle. Then, possibly, one chief may rise so high above his fellows that all may recognize his merits and agree to follow him.”

“That is so,” Beric agreed; “but is it possible that even the greatest hero should find support from all? Cassivelaunus was betrayed by the Trinobantes. Who could have united the tribes more than the sons of Cunobeline, who reigned over well nigh all Britain, and who was a great king ruling wisely and well, and doing all in his power to raise and advance the people; and yet, when the hour came, the kingdom broke up into pieces. Veric, the chief of the Cantii, went to Rome and invited the invader to aid him against his rivals at home, and not a man of the Iceni or the Brigantes marched to the aid of Caractacus and Togodamnus. What wonder, then, that these were defeated. Worse than all, when Caractacus was driven a fugitive to hide among the Brigantes, did not their queen, Cartismandua, hand him over to the Romans? Where can we hope to find a leader more fitted to unite us than was Caractacus, the son of the king whom we all, at least, recognized and paid tribute to; a prince who had learned wisdom from a wise father, a warrior enterprising, bold, and indomitable﻿—a true patriot?

“If Caractacus could not unite us, what hope is there of finding another who would do so? Moreover, our position is far worse now than it was ten years ago. The Belgae and Dumnonii in the southwest have been crushed after thirty battles; the Dobuni in the centre have been defeated and garrisoned; the Silures have set an example to us all, inflicting many defeats on the Romans; but their power has at last been broken. The Brigantes and ourselves have both been heavily struck, as we deserved, Boduoc, for standing aloof from Caractacus at first. Thus the task of shaking off the Roman bonds is far more difficult now than it was when Plautius landed here twenty years ago. Well, it is time for me to be going on. Won’t you come with me, Boduoc?”

===Chinese===

“要等这么个人，贝里克，只怕等到地老天荒，”波杜克声音发沉地说，“反正，咱们伏在罗马脚跟底下过安生日子的当口，这样的人冒不出头来。他要出头，必得在打仗的日子。到那时，兴许有一位酋长远远高出了众人，大伙儿都服他的功，肯跟他。”

“这话是不错，”贝里克同意，“可就算是顶天的豪杰，也未必撑得起全族。卡西维劳努斯叫特里诺班特人卖了。库诺贝林的儿子们，统着几乎整个不列颠，还有谁比他们更能把各部落拢起来？他们的父亲是个贤明的君王，治国有方，一心要抬举族人；可时辰一到，王国照样四分五裂。肯特人的酋长维里克，竟跑去罗马，请入侵者来帮他收拾国内的对手；爱西尼和布里甘特人，一个也没去救卡拉塔库斯和托戈达姆努斯。这样还打输了，有什么奇怪。更糟的还有呢：卡拉塔库斯兵败逃难，躲进布里甘特人那里，他们的王后卡蒂斯曼杜娅，竟把他捆了送交罗马人！咱们还能指望在哪儿找到一位比卡拉塔库斯更配统起咱们的首领？他是咱们人人至少都认过、纳过贡的那位君王之子；是一位从贤父那里学来智慧的王子；是一位敢闯、敢斗、打不垮的战士——一位真正的爱国者。

“连卡拉塔库斯都拢不住咱们，再找一位拢得住的，还有什么指望？何况咱们的处境，比十年前又坏了多少。西南的贝尔盖人和杜姆诺尼人，打了三十仗之后被碾平了；中部的多布尼人败了，叫人驻了军；西尔人给全族树了榜样，把罗马人打败了许多回，可他们的力气到底也叫耗尽了。布里甘特人和咱们自己，都重重挨过一下——那是咱们活该，波杜克，谁叫咱们起初坐视卡拉塔库斯不管。这样看来，今天要挣脱罗马的枷锁，比二十年前普劳提乌斯登岸那会儿，难得多。好了，我该进去了。你不跟我一起来么，波杜克？”

===Original===

“Not I, Beric; I never want to enter their town again save with a sword in one hand and a torch in the other. It enrages me to see the airs of superiority they give themselves. They scarce seem even to see us as we walk in their streets; and as to the soldiers as they stride along with helmet and shield, my fingers itch to meet them in the forest. No; I promised to walk so far with you, but I go no farther. How long will you be there?”

“Two hours at most, I should say.”

“The sun is halfway down, Beric; I will wait for you till it touches that hill over there. Till then you will find me sitting by the first tree at the spot where we left the forest.”

Beric nodded and walked on towards the town. The lad, for he was not yet sixteen, was the son of Parta, the chieftainess of one of the divisions of the great tribe of the Iceni, who occupied the tract of country now known as Suffolk, Norfolk, Cambridge, and Huntingdon. This tribe had yielded but a nominal allegiance to Cunobeline, and had held aloof during the struggle between Caractacus and the Romans, but when the latter had attempted to establish forts in their country they had taken up arms. Ostorius Scapula, the Roman proprietor, had marched against them and defeated them with great slaughter, and they had submitted to the Roman authority. The Sarci, the division of the tribe to which Beric belonged, had taken a leading part in the rising, and his father had fallen in the defence of their intrenchments.

Among the British tribes the women ranked with the men, and even when married the wife was often the acknowledged chief of the tribe. Parta had held an equal authority with her husband, and at his death remained sole head of the subtribe, and in order to ensure its obedience in the future, Ostorius had insisted that her only son Beric, at that time a boy of eleven, should be handed over to them as a hostage.

Had Parta consulted her own wishes she would have retired with a few followers to the swamps and fens of the country to the north rather than surrender her son, but the Brigantes, who inhabited Lincolnshire, and who ranged over the whole of the north of Britain as far as Northumberland, had also received a defeat at the hands of the Romans, and might not improbably hand her over upon their demand. She therefore resigned herself to let Beric go.

===Chinese===

“我才不呢，贝里克；除非一手剑、一手火把，我再也不想进他们的城。看他们端起那副高人一等的架子，我就来火。咱们走在他们街上，他们简直拿眼角都懒得扫咱们；至于那些顶着盔、端着盾、大摇大摆的大兵，我的手指头发痒，恨不能在林子里会一会他们。不；答应陪你走到这儿，我可不再挪窝了。你进去要多久？”

“顶多两个钟头吧。”

“太阳落了一半了，贝里克；我等你，等它挨着那边那座山。到时候，你出林子那个地方的头一棵树底下找我就是。”

贝里克点点头，朝城里走去。这少年——他还不到十六岁——是帕尔塔的儿子；帕尔塔是爱西尼大族一支的首领。爱西尼人占的那片地方，就是今天的萨福克、诺福克、剑桥和亨廷顿。这个部落对库诺贝林不过名义上服个管，卡拉塔库斯同罗马人苦斗的时候，他们一直置身事外；可等罗马人要在他们地界里设堡垒，他们就动了刀兵。罗马总督奥斯托利乌斯·斯卡普拉挥军来攻，把他们杀得大败，他们降了罗马。萨尔西——贝里克所属的这一支——在那场起事里挑过头，他父亲就阵亡在寨垒的守卫战里。

在不列颠的部落里，女人同男人平起平坐；便是嫁了人，妻子往往还是部落公认的酋长。帕尔塔同丈夫一向同掌权柄；丈夫死后，她便是这一支独一的首领。为着叫这一支往后服帖，奥斯托利乌斯执意要她把独子贝里克交出去当人质——那时贝里克才十一岁。

倘若依着帕尔塔自己的意思，她宁可带几个随从遁进北边乡野的沼泽洼地，也不交出儿子；可占据林肯那一带、地盘一直铺到诺森伯兰的布里甘特人，也刚在罗马人手里吃了败仗，罗马人要人，他们多半会把她也交出去。她只得认了，放贝里克去。

===Original===

“My son,” she said, “I need not tell you not to let them Romanize you. You have been brought up to hate them. Your father has fallen before their weapons, half your tribe have been slain, your country lies under their feet. I will not wrong you then by fearing for a moment that they can make a Roman of you.

“You have been brought up to lie upon the bare ground, to suffer fatigue and hardship, hunger and thirst, and the rich food and splendid houses and soft raiment of the Romans should have no attraction for you. I know not how long your imprisonment among them may last. For the present I have little hope of another rising; but should I see a prospect of anything like unity among our people, I will send Boduoc with a message to you to hold yourself in readiness to escape when you receive the signal that the time has come. Till then employ your mind in gaining what good you may by your residence among them; there must be some advantage in their methods of warfare which has enabled the people of one city to conquer the world.

“It is not their strength, for they are but pygmies to us. We stand a full head above them, and even we women are stronger than Roman soldiers, and yet they defeat us. Learn then their language, throw your whole mind into that at first, then study their military discipline and their laws. It must be the last as much as their discipline that has made them rulers over so vast an empire. Find out if you can the secret of their rule, and study the training by which their soldiers move and fight as if bound together by a cord, forming massive walls against which we break ourselves in vain. Heed not their arts, pay no attention to their luxuries, these did Cunobeline no good, and did not for a day delay the destruction that fell upon his kingdom. What we need is first a knowledge of their military tactics, so that we may drive them from the land; secondly, a knowledge of their laws, that we may rule ourselves wisely after they have gone. What there is good in the rest may come in time.

===Chinese===

“我的儿，”她说，“用不着我嘱咐你，别让他们把你化成个罗马人。你是恨他们长大的。你父亲死在他们的兵器底下，你部落半数的人叫他们杀了，你的国土伏在他们脚下。这样说，我倒不是小看你——我一点也不怕他们能把你变成罗马人。

“你是在光地上睡惯、吃苦受累惯、忍饥挨渴惯的人；罗马人的美食、华堂、软衣，迷不住你的眼。你蹲在他们中间的日子有多久，我说不上。眼下，我不指望再起一回大事；可我一旦看出咱们的人有了那么一点拧成一股绳的苗头，就打发波杜克捎信给你，叫你时刻预备着，一得信号就逃。在那之前，你只管拿这趟寄居，能学多少好东西学多少好东西。他们的打法里必有长处——不然，一座城的人，凭什么征服天下。

“不是凭力气，论力气，他们同咱们一比是侏儒。咱们比他们高出整整一头；连咱们女人都比罗马兵壮，可他们还是赢咱们。那就学他们的话，头一样，把全副心思都扑上去；再学他们的军纪，学他们的法度。他们能辖这么大一片帝国，法度同军纪必是一半对一半。你想法子找出他们治术的诀窍；用心看他们操练，看他们的兵怎样行动厮杀，像一根绳拴在一处，结成一面堵不死的大墙——咱们撞上去，白白撞碎自己。他们的手艺你别理会，他们的奢华你甭搭理；那些东西没给库诺贝林半分好处，也没叫罩在他王国头上的祸事迟来一天。咱们要的，头一样是他们兵法的门道，好把他们赶出这片土地；第二样是他们法度的门道，好等他们走后，咱们自己把自己治得像样。其余那些好的，来日方长。

===Original===

“However kind they may be to you, bear always in mind that you are but a prisoner among the oppressors of your country, and that though, for reasons of policy, they may treat you well, yet that they mercilessly despoil and ill treat your countrymen. Remember too, Beric, that the Britons, now that Caractacus has been sent a prisoner to Rome, need a leader, one who is not only brave and valiant in the fight, but who can teach the people how to march to victory, and can order and rule them well afterwards. We are part of one of our greatest tribes, and from among us, if anywhere, such a leader should come.

“I have great hopes of you, Beric. I know that you are brave, for single handed you slew with an arrow a great wolf the other day; but bravery is common to all, I do not think that there is a coward in the tribe. I believe you are intelligent. I consulted the old Druid in the forest last week, and he prophesied a high destiny for you; and when the messenger brought the Roman summons for me to deliver you up as a hostage, it seemed to me that this was of all things the one that would fit you best for future rule. I am not ambitious for you, Beric. It would be nought to me if you were king of all the Britons. It is of our country that I think. We need a great leader, and my prayer to the gods is that one may be found. If you should be the man so much the better; but if not, let it be another. Comport yourself among them independently, as one who will some day be chief of a British tribe, but be not sullen or obstinate. Mix freely with them, learn their language, gather what are the laws under which they live, see how they build those wonderful houses of theirs, watch the soldiers at their exercises, so that when you return among us you can train the Sarci to fight in a similar manner. Keep the one purpose always in your mind. Exercise your muscles daily, for among us no man can lead who is not as strong and as brave as the best who follow him. Bear yourself so that you shall be in good favour with all men.”

===Chinese===

“他们待你再和气，你也永远记着：你不过是关在压你国家的人手里的一个囚徒；他们出于算计可以待你好，转过身，就毫不留情地抢你的族人、糟蹋你的族人。还要记着，贝里克，如今卡拉塔库斯被押去罗马做了囚徒，不列颠人缺一位首领：不光要阵前勇猛，还要会领着众人走向胜利，事后再会安顿、会治理。咱们是顶大的部落里的一支，这样的人要出，就该出在咱们中间。

“我对你抱着大指望，贝里克。我知道你胆子大——前几天，你独自一人，一箭射死了一头大狼；可胆量人人都有，咱们部落里，我看没有一个懦夫。我相信你脑子灵。上礼拜我去林子里请教了老德鲁伊，他预言你命里有一场大前程。使者把罗马人要我交你为质的召令送来时，我心里就觉得：万事万物里，偏偏这一桩，最配把你磨成将来掌舵的料。我倒不是为你图什么，贝里克。你做全不列颠的王，于我值个什么？我想的是咱们的国。咱们需要一位大首领；我求告神明，求祂赐下一位来。倘若那个人是你，好上加好；不是你，是旁人也罢。在他们中间，你要立得直，像一位来日要做不列颠部落首领的人那样；可别摆脸色、别犯犟。同他们大大方方地往来，学他们的话，摸清他们活在底下的是些什么法，看他们怎样起那些神奇的房子，看大兵们操练——这样，你回到咱们中间，就能照那个样子，把萨尔西操练起来。这一个目的，须臾不可离你的心。每天练你的筋骨：在咱们这里，不比最壮、最勇的追随者更壮更勇的人，带不了头。做人行事，叫人人都说你一声好。”

===Original===

Beric had, to the best of his power, carried out the instructions of his mother. It was the object of the Romans always to win over their adversaries if possible, and the boy had no reason to complain of his treatment. He was placed in the charge of Caius Muro, commander of a legion, and a slave was at once appointed to teach him Latin. He took his meals with the scribe and steward of the household, for Caius was of noble family, of considerable wealth, and his house was one of the finest in Camalodunum. He was a kindly and just man, and much beloved by his troops. As soon as Beric had learned the language, Caius ordered the scribe to teach him the elements of Roman law, and a decurion was ordered to take him in hand and instruct him in arms.

As Beric was alike eager to study and to exercise in arms, he gained the approval of both his teachers. Julia, the wife of Caius, a kindly lady, took a great fancy to the boy. “He will make a fine man, Caius,” she said one day when the boy was fourteen years old. “See how handsome and strong he is; why, Scipio, the son of the centurion Metellus, is older by two years, and yet he is less strong than this young Briton.”

“They are a fine race, Julia, though in disposition as fierce as wild cats, and not to be trusted. But the lad is, as you say, strong and nimble. I marked him practising with the sword the other day against Lucinus, who is a stout soldier, and the man had as much as he could do to hold his own against him. I was surprised myself to see how well he wielded a sword of full weight, and how active he was. The contest reminded me of a dog and a wild cat, so nimble were the boy’s springs, and so fierce his attacks. Lucinus fairly lost his temper at last, and I stopped the fight, for although they fought with blunted weapons, he might well have injured the lad badly with a downright cut, and that would have meant trouble with the Iceni again.”

“He is intelligent, too,” Julia replied. “Sometimes I have him in while I am working with the two slave girls, and he will stand for hours asking me questions about Rome, and about our manners and customs.”

===Chinese===

贝里克已尽他所能，照母亲的话做了。罗马人的一贯路数，是能收买对头就收买，这少年没什么可抱怨他们的待遇。他被交给盖乌斯·穆罗——一位军团统领——照管，立刻派了奴隶教他拉丁语。他同家里的书吏和管家一桌用饭；盖乌斯出身贵族门第，家道殷实，宅邸是卡马卢杜努姆数得着的。他是个和善公正的人，深得军心。贝里克一学会语言，盖乌斯便叫书吏教他罗马法的大要，又派一名十夫长带他习武。

贝里克读书肯钻，练武也肯下力，两位师父都看他顺眼。盖乌斯的妻子尤利娅是位和气的夫人，打心眼里喜欢这孩子。“他会长成一条好汉，盖乌斯，”这孩子十四岁那年，她有一天说，“瞧他这副俊模样、这副筋骨；百夫长梅特卢斯的儿子西庇阿，比他大两岁呢，还没这个不列颠小子结实。”

“这一族人筋骨是好的，尤利娅——虽说性子凶得像野猫，信不得。可这孩子，也真像你说的，又结实又灵巧。前几天我看他同卢基努斯练剑，卢基努斯是条硬汉，招架他竟招架得吃力。他自己一柄十足分量的剑抡得那样好、身子那样活，连我都吃惊。那一场子看得我想起狗斗野猫：少年那几扑那样轻，那几攻那样狠。末了卢基努斯真动了火，我叫停了手——兵器虽是钝的，他一记实砍也够孩子受的，真伤了，同爱西尼又是一场麻烦。”

“他脑子也灵，”尤利娅说，“有时我做活，叫两个奴隶女孩陪着，把他唤进来，他一站几个钟头，问我罗马的事，问咱们的规矩风俗。”

===Original===

“One is never sure of these tamed wolves,” Caius said; “sometimes they turn out valuable allies and assistants, at other times they grow into formidable foes, all the more dangerous for what they have learned of us. However, do with him as you like, Julia; a woman has a lighter hand than a man, and you are more likely to tame him than we are. Cneius says that he is very eager to learn, and has ever a book in his hand when not practising in arms.”

“What I like most in him,” Julia said, “is that he is very fond of our little Berenice. The child has taken to him wonderfully, and of an afternoon, when he has finished with Cneius, she often goes out with him. Of course old Lucia goes with them. It is funny to hear them on a wet day, when they cannot go out, talking together﻿—she telling him stories of Rome and of our kings and consuls, and he telling her tales of hunting the wolf and wild boar, and legends of his people, who seem to have been always at war with someone.”

After Beric had resided for three years and a half at Camalodunum a great grief fell on the family of Caius Muro, for the damp airs from the valley had long affected Julia and she gradually faded and died. Beric felt the loss very keenly, for she had been uniformly kind to him. A year later Suetonius and the governor of the colony decided that as the Sarci had now been quiet for nearly five years, and as Caius reported that their young chief seemed to have become thoroughly Romanized, he was permitted to return to his tribe.

The present was his first visit to the colony since he had left it four months before. His companion, Boduoc, was one of the tribesmen, a young man six years his senior. He was related to his mother, and had been his companion in his childish days, teaching him woodcraft, and to throw the javelin and use the sword. Together, before Beric went as hostage, they had wandered through the forest and hunted the wolf and wild boar, and at that time Boduoc had stood in the relation of an elder brother to Beric. That relation had now much changed. Although Boduoc was a powerful young man and Beric but a sturdy stripling, the former was little better than an untutored savage, and he looked with great respect upon Beric both as his chief and as possessing knowledge that seemed to him to be amazing.

===Chinese===

“驯服的狼，说不准，”盖乌斯说，“有时调教出来是个得力的帮手，有时偏偏长成个厉害的对头——就因为他们把咱们的门道学了去。也罢，你看着办，尤利娅；女人的手比男人轻，你驯他，比咱们有指望。克内乌斯说他求学心切，不练武的时候，手里总捧着书。”

“我顶喜欢他的一点，”尤利娅说，“是他真心疼咱们的小贝伦妮刻。这孩子黏他黏得出奇；下午他跟克内乌斯完了课，她常同他一道出去，自然是老露琪娅跟着。落雨天出不了门，听他们俩说话才有趣呢——她给他讲罗马的故事，讲咱们的君王和执政官；他给她讲猎狼猎野猪，讲他族人的传说。他那族人，好像天 生就在同人打仗。”

贝里克在卡马卢杜努姆住了三年半，盖乌斯·穆罗家遭了一场大丧：谷地里的湿气早就侵蚀着尤利娅，她一日一日地衰下去，死了。贝里克很伤了些心，她待他始终是好的。一年后，苏埃托尼乌斯同殖民地的总督计议：萨尔西人既然安分了将近五年，盖乌斯又禀报说他们的小首领看去已彻头彻尾罗马化了，便准他回部落去。

眼下这次，是他走后四个月头一回再回殖民地来。他的同伴波杜克是部落里的人，比他年长六岁，是他母系的亲戚，小时候就同他作伴，教他林子里的本事，教他掷标枪、使剑。贝里克去当人质之前，两人一道穿林打猎，猎狼猎野猪；那时节，波杜克于他，是兄长的身份。如今这份名分变了许多：波杜克虽是条壮汉，贝里克不过是个结实的少年，可前者粗野得没开过蒙，对贝里克敬重得很——一头敬他是酋长，一头敬他那一肚子在他看来神妙无比的学问。

===Original===

Hating the Romans blindly he had trembled lest he should find Beric on his return completely Romanized. He had many times, during the lad’s stay at Camalodunum, carried messages to him there from his mother, and had sorrowfully shaken his head on his way back through the forest as he thought of his young chief’s surroundings. Beric had partially adopted the Roman costume, and to hear him talking and jesting in their own language to the occupants of the mansion, whose grandeur and appointments filled Boduoc with an almost superstitious fear, was terrible to him. However, his loyalty to Beric prevented him from breathing a word in the tribe as to his fears, and he was delighted to find the young chief return home in British garb, and to discover that although his views of the Romans differed widely from his own, he was still British at heart, and held firmly the opinion that the only hope for the freedom of Britain was the entire expulsion of the invaders.

He was gratified to find that Beric had become by no means what he considered effeminate. He was built strongly and massively, as might be expected from such parents, and was of the true British type, that had so surprised the Romans at their first coming among them, possessing great height and muscular power, together with an activity promoted by constant exercise.

Beric had fallen back upon the customs of his people as thoroughly as if he had never dwelt in the stately Roman town. He was as ready as before to undertake the longest hunting expeditions, to sleep in the forest, to go from sunrise to sunset without breaking his fast. When not engaged in hunting he practised incessantly hurling the javelin and other warlike exercises, while of an evening he frequently related stories of Roman history to any chiefs or other guests of his mother, on which occasions the humbler followers would gather thickly in the background, evincing an interest even greater than that which they felt in the songs and legends of the bards.

===Chinese===

他瞎着眼恨罗马人，一路上直打鼓，生怕回来一看，贝里克整个化成了罗马人。少年在卡马卢杜努姆那些年，他屡次替他母亲捎信进去，回程穿林子的时候，想起小首领身边那一片光景，一路只是摇头。贝里克半穿了罗马的装束；听他用罗马话同那些宅子里的人谈笑——那份富贵排场，在波杜克眼里几乎带着邪气——他心里难受。可他对贝里克的那份忠，叫他半个字也没敢往部落里漏；见小首领一身不列颠衣裳回家，又见他虽说对罗马人的看法同自己差着十万八千里，心却还是不列颠的心，一口咬定不列颠自由的唯一指望就是把入侵者尽数赶走，他喜得不行。

更叫他称心的是，贝里克绝没有变成他以为的那种娘娘腔。这少年骨架结实厚重——有那样的爹娘，原该如此——一身地道的不列颠体格：当年头一回见面就叫罗马人吃了一惊的那种体格，高挑、多力，再加上常年操练出来的一身活络。

贝里克回部落的规矩，回得干干净净，仿佛从没在那座气派的罗马城里住过。长途的围猎，他照旧抢着去；林子里过夜，他照旧睡得着；从日出到日落不进一口食，他照旧熬得住。不打猎的时候，他练功不歇：掷标枪，演各样兵器；晚上，他常给母亲的座上客——那些酋长和头人们——讲罗马史里的故事。讲这些的时候，下层的随从们黑压压围在后排，那份入神，比听吟游诗人的歌谣传说还要高出一头。

===Original===

Beric generally chose stories relating to periods when Rome was hardly pressed by her foes, showing how the intense feeling of patriotism, and the obstinate determination to resist, in spite of all dangers, upon the part of the population, and the discipline and dogged valour of the soldiers, saved her from destruction. He was cautious to draw no parallel openly to the case of Britain. He knew that the Romans were made acquainted, by traitors in their pay, with much that passed among the native tribes, and that at first they were sure to interest themselves in his proceedings. At present there could be no thought of a rising, and the slightest sign of disaffection might bring disaster and ruin upon his tribe. Only when some unexpected event, some invasion of the rights of the Britons even more flagrant than those that had hitherto taken place, should stir the smouldering fire of discontent, and fan it into a fierce flame of revolt from end to end of Britain, could success be hoped for.

No Roman could have found fault with Beric’s relation of their prowess or their valour; for he held them up to the admiration of his hearers. “No wonder Rome is great and powerful,” he said, “when its people evince so deep a love of country, so resolute a determination in the face of their enemies, so unconquerable a spirit when misfortune weighs upon them.”

To the men he addressed all this was new. It was true that a few princes and chiefs had visited Rome, occasionally as travellers desiring to see the centre of her greatness, more often as exiles driven from Britain by defeat in civil strife, but these had only brought back great tales of Rome’s magnificence, and the Britons knew nothing of the history of the invaders, and eagerly listened to the stories that Beric had learned from their books in the course of his studies. The report of his stories spread so far that visits were paid to the village of Parta by chiefs and leading men from other sections of the Iceni to listen to them.

===Chinese===

贝里克挑的故事，多拣罗马被强敌逼到绝境的年头：讲它的百姓怎样凭着一腔爱国热血、一股不避万难的死硬抵抗，讲它的兵怎样靠纪律和韧劲，一次次把它从覆亡边上拉回来。他小心得很，从不当众把话说破，往不列颠身上引。他知道，部落里的事，多有他们收买的奸细报给罗马人；起先，人家必定盯着他的动静。眼下谈不上起事，一点反迹露出来，就够他部落遭殃。只有等到一件天外飞来的事——一件比以往更加无法无天、欺到不列颠人头上的横暴——把底下那堆闷着的怨火点着，扇成从南到北烧遍全岛的冲天烈焰，才有胜算可指望。

罗马人听贝里克讲自家的武运和勇烈，挑不出半点毛病：他是把罗马抬起来给听众景仰的。“难怪罗马这样伟大、这样强盛，”他说，“它的人民这样爱他们的国，在敌人面前这样咬得定，厄运压顶的时候，这口气这样扑不灭。”

这些话，在他听的人耳朵里全是新的。不错，有几位王孙酋长到过罗马——有时的确是去瞻仰它伟大的中心，更多的，是族内争斗败了，亡命去的——可他们带回来的，不过是罗马如何豪奢的大话；对入侵者的来历，不列颠人一概不知，所以贝里克从书上读来的那些故事，人人听得入迷。他讲史的名声传得那样远，爱西尼别的支派的酋长和头人们，竟专程赶到帕尔塔的村子里来听。

===Original===

Oratory was among the Britons, as among most primitive tribes, highly prized and much cultivated. Oral tradition among such peoples takes the place of books among civilized nations. Story and legend are handed down from father to son, and the wandering bard is a most welcome guest. Next only to valour oratory sways and influences the minds of the people, and a Ulysses had greater influence than an Ajax. From his earliest childhood Beric had listened to the stories and legends told by bards in the rough palace of his father, and his sole schooling before he went to Camalodunum had been to learn these by heart, and to repeat them with due emphasis and appropriate gesture. His father had been one of the most eloquent and influential of the chiefs of the Iceni, and had early impressed upon him the importance of cultivating the power of speech.

His studies in Roman history, too, had taught him the power exercised by men with the gift of moving multitudes by their words; he had learned from books how clearly and distinctly events could be described by a careful choice of words, and attention to form and expression, so that almost unconsciously to himself he had practised the art in his relations of the tales and legends of British history to Berenice and her mother. Thus, then, the manner no less than the matter of his recitals of Roman story, gained him a high estimation among his hearers, and he was already looked upon as a young chief likely to rise to a very high position among the Iceni. Among the common herd his glowing laudations of Roman patriotism, devotion, and sacrifice, caused him to be regarded with disfavour, and the epithet “the Roman” was frequently applied to him. But the wiser spirits saw the hidden meaning of his stories, and that, while holding up the Romans as an example, he was endeavouring to teach how much can be done by patriotism, by a spirit of self sacrifice, and by unity against a common foe. Parta was also proud of the congratulations that distinguished chiefs, famed for their wisdom throughout the tribe, offered to her on the occasion of their visits.

===Chinese===

在不列颠人中间，同在多数初创的部落里一样，演说是一门顶受看重、顶下苦功的学问。口传的历史，在这样的民族里，顶的是文明国度的书。故事和传说，父传子，子传孙；四方的吟游诗人，到哪儿都是最受欢迎的客。论摇动人心、左右人心，口才仅次于勇武；一个奥德修斯，抵得过一位埃阿斯。贝里克从记事起，就在父亲那粗朴的王堂里听诗人讲史说传；去卡马卢杜努姆之前，他受的全部教育，就是把它们背熟，再照着该有的轻重、配上该有的手势，复述出来。他父亲当年是爱西尼众酋长里最能言、最有份量的人物之一，早早就把“练口舌”这件事，压进了他的心里。

他研读罗马史，又教他见识了那号人物的本事：凭一张嘴，搬动千军万马。他从书上学会了：字句怎样拣选、章法声口怎样讲究，一件往事就能讲得清清楚楚、历历如在眼前。这样，不知不觉间，他给贝伦妮刻母女讲不列颠的传说故事时，早把这门功夫练在里头了。于是，罗马故事从他嘴里出来，那讲法同那内容一样，为他赢得了听众的高看；他已被众人当作一位前程远大的少年酋长。在下层粗人堆里，他那样热烈地夸罗马人的爱国、忠勇和舍己，倒招人白眼，“罗马人”这个诨名，常安在他头上。可明白人听得出他故事底下埋的话：他把罗马人举作榜样，正为的是教人看清——爱国的心、舍己的志、同仇的合，能成多大的事。各支派里素称睿智的酋长们登门道贺，帕尔塔也与有荣焉。

===Original===

“Beric will be a great chief,” one of the wisest of these said to her; “truly his sojourn among the Romans has done great things for him. It would be well, indeed, if every noble youth throughout the island were to have such schooling, if he had your son’s wit in taking advantage of it. He will be a great orator; never among our bards have I heard narrations so clear and so well delivered; although the deeds he praises are those of our oppressors, one cannot but feel a thrill of enthusiasm as he tells them. Yea, for the moment I myself felt half a Roman when he told us of the brave youth who thrust his hand into the flames, and suffered it to be consumed in order to impress the invader with a knowledge of the spirit that animated the Romans, and of the three men who held against a host the bridge that their friends were breaking down behind them.

===Chinese===

“贝里克会成为一位大首领，”其中最贤明的一位对她说，“他在罗马人中间那一趟，真没白住。说真的，全岛哪一家的少年子弟都该去受这样一场教——只要有你儿子这份会捡便宜的聪明。他会成为一位大演说家；我们的诗人里，我从没听过谁把事情讲得这样清楚、这样好听；他夸的虽是压咱们的人的功业，听他讲，你偏忍不住心头一热。是啊，他讲那位少年英杰把手伸进火里，任它烧烂，好叫入侵者领教领教罗马人的那股气；讲那三个人，抵着千军，守着身后自家人正在拆的桥——听到那里，连我自己，一时都做了半回罗马人。”

===Original===

“If he could stir me thus by his tales of the deeds of our enemies, what will it be when some day he makes the heroes of Britain his theme, and calls upon his countrymen to imitate their deeds! I have heard him called ‘the Roman,’ Parta. Now that I have listened to him I know that he will, when the time comes, be one of Rome’s most formidable foes. I will tell you now that Prasutagus, our king, and his queen Boadicea, spoke to me about Beric, and begged me to come hither to see for myself this youth of whom they had heard reports from others, some saying that he had returned a Roman heart and soul, while others affirmed that, while he had learned much from them, he had forgotten nothing of the injuries he had received at their hands in the death of his father, and the disaster of the tribe. I shall know now what to tell them. To Prasutagus, whose fear of the Romans is even greater than his hatred for them, I shall say that the lad is full of the glories of Roman story, and that there is no fear of his doing or saying aught that will excite the anger or suspicion of the Romans. To Boadicea, who hates the Romans far more than she fears them, I shall tell the truth, and shall inform her that when the time comes, as assuredly it some day will, that the Iceni are called upon to defend their liberties against Rome, in Beric she will find a champion of whom I predict that he will be worthy to take his place in our history by the side of Caractacus and Cassivelaunus. May our gods avert that, like them, he fall a victim to British treachery!”

After leaving Boduoc, Beric crossed the bridge built by the Romans over the Stour, and entered the city. Camalodunum was the chief seat of the Roman power in England. Although but so short a time had elapsed since Claudius had occupied it, it was already a large city. A comparatively small proportion, however, was Roman work, but all bore the impress of Roman art and civilization, for Cunobeline, whose capital it had been, was a highly enlightened king, and had introduced Roman ways and methods among his people. Men instructed in their arts and architecture had been largely employed in the building of the town, and its edifices would have borne comparison with those in minor towns in the Roman provinces.

===Chinese===

“他讲敌人的事迹，尚且这样撩得我热血，有朝一日，他把不列颠的英雄作题目，唤同胞们学他们的行，那又该是怎样一番光景！我听见有人叫他‘罗马人’，帕尔塔。如今听过他，我知道：时辰一到，他会是罗马最凶的敌人之一。现在我不瞒你：咱们的王普拉苏塔古斯同王后波阿迪西亚，都同我说起过贝里克，托我亲自来瞧瞧这个各路传闻里的少年——有人说他回来时里里外外已是罗马人；也有人说，他学是学了许多，父亲之死、部落之殃，他一笔也没忘。如今我知道该回去怎么回话了。对普拉苏塔古斯——他怕罗马，比恨罗马还多——我就说：这孩子满脑子罗马史上的荣光，一言一行，决不会撩拨罗马人的怒、招罗马人的疑。对波阿迪西亚——她恨罗马，远过于怕——我就说实话：告诉她说，等到那一天——那一天必定会来——爱西尼人应召为自由同罗马相拼的时候，她在贝里克身上会找到一位战士；我敢断言，此人将同卡拉塔库斯和卡西维劳努斯并肩，站在咱们史册里。只求咱们的神明保佑，别叫他同他们两位一样，栽在不列颠人自己的背叛手里！”

别过波杜克，贝里克过了罗马人在斯陶尔河上架的桥，进了城。卡马卢杜努姆是罗马人在英格兰的权力中枢。距克劳狄乌斯占据它虽还没多少年，却已经是一座大城。不过罗马人自己的工程只占一小部分；其余的，也统统带着罗马艺术和教化的印记——因为库诺贝林——此城旧主——本就是一位开通的君王，早把罗马的路数引给了他的人民。当年筑城，雇了许多通晓罗马技艺营造的匠人；城里的楼馆，同罗马行省的一般城镇相比也不逊色。

===Original===

The conquerors, therefore, found much of their work done for them. The original possessors of the houses and of the highly cultivated lands lying round the town were ejected wholesale, and the Romans, establishing themselves in their abodes and farms, then proceeded to add to, embellish, and fortify the town. The 2nd, 9th, and 14th Legions were selected by Claudius to found what was called the colony, and to take possession of the surrounding country. Plautius was appointed propraetor, or governor, and establishing himself in the royal palace of Cunobeline, his first step was to protect the city from renewed attacks by the Britons. He accordingly erected vast works to the westward of the town, extending from the sea to the river, by which means he not only protected the city from attack, but gained, in case of an assault by overpowering numbers, the means of retiring safely to Mersea Island, lying a short distance from the shore.

===Chinese===

于是，征服者们发现，许多活计早有人替他们干好了。城里原有的住户，连同城周那一片精耕的良田，成批成批被赶了出去；罗马人住进他们的宅院田庄，接着便扩城、修城、设防。克劳狄乌斯点出第二、第九、第十四三个军团，来建这座“殖民地”，接收四郊的乡野。普劳提乌斯受命为总督；他把大营扎进库诺贝林的王宫，第一步，便是防备不列颠人卷土重来。他在城西起了一道浩大的工事，从海一直连到河边——这样，不但护住了城，一旦众寡悬殊、强敌来攻，还可从容退到近岸的默西岛上。

===Original===

A council house and a tribunal were erected for the Roman magistrates; temples, a theatre, and baths raised. The civilian population increased rapidly. Architects, artists, and musicians, decorators, skilled artisans, and traders were attracted from the mainland to the rising city, which rapidly increased in wealth and importance. Conspicuous on the most elevated position stood a temple erected to the honour of Claudius, who was raised by the grateful legionaries to divine rank. So strong and populous was the city that the Trinobantes, during the years that had elapsed since the Romans took possession of it, remained passive under the yoke of their oppressors, and watched, without attempting to take part in them, the rising of the Iceni and Brigantes, the long and desperate war of the Silures and Ordovices under Caractacus, and the reduction of the Belgae and Dumnonii from Hampshire to Cornwall by Vespasian. Yet, had their spirit remained unbroken, there was an opportunity for revenge, for a large part of the veteran legionaries had been withdrawn to take part in the struggle against the western tribes. The tribe had, however, been disarmed, and with Camalodunum on the north, and the rising towns of London and Verulamium on the south, they were cut off from other tribes, and could not hope for final success, unless the powerful Iceni, who were still semi-independent, rose in the national cause. Whether their easy defeat of this tribe soon after the occupation of Camalodunum had rendered the Romans contemptuous of their fighting powers, or that they deemed it wiser to subdue the southwest and west of England, and to strike a heavy blow at the Brigantes to the north before interfering with a powerful tribe so close to their doors, is uncertain; but doubtless they felt that so long as Prasutagus reigned there was little fear of trouble in that quarter, as that king protested himself the friend and ally of Rome, and occupied himself wholly in acquiring wealth and adding to his personal possessions.

===Chinese===

罗马的官长们有了议事的公廨和法庭；庙宇、剧场和浴场也立了起来。民户增加得飞快。建筑师、画师、乐师、装点匠、精工巧匠和商贾，从大陆被这座新兴的城市引来，城的财富和声势一日盛过一日。全城最高的地方，赫然立着一座庙，奉祀克劳狄乌斯——感戴的军团士兵，已把他抬上了神的位格。这座城如此坚固、人烟如此繁盛，以致特里诺班特人自罗马人占城以来，一直驯伏在压榨者的轭下，眼睁睁看着爱西尼和布里甘特人的起事、看着西尔人和奥多威斯人在卡拉塔库斯旗下那场漫长酷烈的血战、看着韦帕芗把贝尔盖人和杜姆诺尼人从汉普郡一路削到康沃尔，都插不上手。其实，倘若他们那口气没断，报仇的机会不是没有：老兵军团的相当一部分，已被抽去打西边的部落了。只是这支部落早被缴了械；北有卡马卢杜努姆，南有日见兴旺的伦蒂尼恩和维鲁拉米恩两座新城，他们同旁的部落隔绝，孤军难成大事——除非强大的、尚且半独立的爱西尼人为全族的义旗而起。是因为占了卡马卢杜努姆之后轻轻松松赢过这个部落一回，罗马人便看轻了他们的战力呢，还是他们认定先平定英格兰的西南西陲、先重创北面的布里甘特人，再动这个近在肘腋的强部更稳妥，说不准；不过无疑，他们觉得：只要普拉苏塔古斯在位，那一头就出不了乱子——那位王自居罗马的朋友和盟邦，一心一意只忙着攒钱、添置私产。

===Original===

The scene in Camalodunum was a familiar one to Beric. The streets were thronged with people. Traders from Gaul and Italy, Roman artisans and workmen, haughty legionaries with shield and helmet, civil officials, Greek players, artists and decorators, native tribesmen, with the products of their fields or the spoils of the chase, walking with humble mien; and shopkeepers sitting at the open fronts of their houses, while their slaves called the attention of passersby to the merits of the goods. Here were the rich products of Eastern looms, there the cloths and linen of Rome, further on a smith’s shop in full work, beyond that a silversmith’s, next door to which was a thriving trader who sold unguents and perfumes, dyes for the ladies’ cheeks and pigments for their eyebrows, dainty requisites for the toilette, and perfumed soap. Bakers and butchers, vendors of fish and game, of fruit, of Eastern spices and flavourings abounded.

Druggists and dealers in dyes for clothing and in the pigments used in wall decorations and paintings were also to be found; and, in fact, this Roman capital of a scarcely subjugated country contained all the appliances for luxury and comfort that could be found in the cities of the civilized provinces.

The only shops at which Beric paused were those of the armourers and of the scribes, at some of which were exhibited vellums with the writings of the Greek and Roman poets and historians; and Beric muttered to himself, “If I am ever present at the sack of Camalodunum these shall be my share of the spoil, and I fancy that no one is likely to dispute their possession with me.”

But he did not linger long. Boduoc would be waiting for him, and he could not hurry over his visit, the first he had paid since his absence; therefore he pushed on, with scarce a glance at the stately temple of Claudius, the magnificent baths or other public buildings, until he arrived at the villa of Caius Muro, which stood somewhat beyond the more crowded part of the town.

===Chinese===

卡马卢杜努姆的这番光景，贝里克看熟了。街上熙熙攘攘：高卢和意大利来的客商，罗马的匠人工役，顶盔带盾、趾高气扬的军团兵，文官吏员，希腊的伶人、画师和装点匠；本地部落的男女，揣着田里的出产或猎获，恭恭敬敬地走；店主坐在敞开的铺面前，他们的奴隶朝过路人吆喝着货色的好处。这边是东方织机上的绸缎，那边是罗马的呢料麻布，再过去，一家铁匠铺炉火正旺，挨着是一家银匠，隔壁那家生意兴隆，卖香膏香水、太太们颊上的胭脂、画眉的颜料、梳妆的精巧物件，还有香皂。面包师、肉贩，卖鱼卖野味卖水果的，贩东方香料调味的，满街都是。

卖药材的、卖布料染料和墙面彩绘颜料的铺子，也一应俱全；说实在的，这座罗马人在一块刚刚降服的土地上立的都城，文明行省城市里有的那些奢侈安逸的物件，它一样不缺。

贝里克只在一类铺子前停留过：兵器匠的和书抄匠的——有几家的架子上摆着羊皮卷，抄的是希腊罗马诗人和史家的文字。贝里克自言自语：“哪天要是让我赶上洗劫卡马卢杜努姆，这些就归我那份；我料想，没人会跟我抢。”

可他没有多耽搁。波杜克还在等他，这一趟又不好草草——这是他走后头一回登门。于是他一路只管往前，对克劳狄乌斯那座庄严的神庙、对那些华美的浴场和别的公家楼馆，连眼角都很少扫；直到走到盖乌斯·穆罗的宅邸——那立在城外、稍离开闹市的一角。

## II / 二
===Original===

City and Forest

The house of Caius Muro had been built six years before on the model of one owned by him in the Tuscan hills. Passing through the hall or vestibule, with its mosaic pavement, on which was the word of welcome, “Salve!” Beric entered the atrium, the principal apartment in the house. From each side, at a height of some twenty feet from the ground, extended a roof, the fall being slightly to the centre, where there was an aperture of about eight feet square. Through this light and air made their way down to the apartment, the rainfall from the roofs and opening falling into a marble tank, called the impluvium, below the level of the floor, which was paved with squares of coloured marble. On either side of the atrium were the small sleeping chambers, the bed places being raised and covered with thick mats and rugs.

The walls of the bed chambers as well as of the atrium were painted in black, with figures and landscapes in colour. On the centre of the side facing the vestibule was the tablinum, the apartment of Caius Muro himself. This formed his sitting room and study. The floor was raised about a foot above that of the atrium, and it was partly open both on that side and on the other, looking into the peristylium, so that, while at work, he commanded a view of all that was going on in the atrium and in the courtyard. In the centre of this was a fountain surrounded by plants. From the courtyard opened the triclinium, or dining room, and also rooms used as storerooms, kitchen, and the sleeping places of the slaves.

===Chinese===

城与林

盖乌斯·穆罗的宅子是六年前起的，照的是他在托斯卡纳山间另一座宅子的格局。穿过前厅，脚下是马赛克地面上拼出的迎客字样“欢迎”，贝里克进了中堂——全宅的主间。两侧各探出一面屋顶，离地约二十英尺，微微朝中间倾，正中留一个八英尺见方的天口。光和气从天口落下来；雨顺屋顶淌进天口，落进一方低于地面的石槽，唤作承雨池；堂内的地面，铺的是彩色大理石的方砖。中堂两侧，一间间小小的卧房，榻位垫高，铺着厚厚的草垫和毛毯。

卧房同中堂的墙上，一律黑底，描着彩色的画像和风景。正对前厅那一面的中央是书房，盖乌斯·穆罗自己的房间，做他的起居室兼书房。地面比中堂高出一英尺，朝中堂和后院两边都半敞着，这样他做事时，中堂和院子里的动静，尽收眼底。院子正中一池喷泉，四外环着花木。院子往里开的是餐厅，另有几间，作库房、厨房和奴隶的睡处。

===Original===

At the back of the peristylium was the oecus, or state apartment, where Caius received distinguished guests, and where, in the lifetime of Julia, entertainments were given to the ladies of the colony. Like the triclinium, this room was also partially open at both ends, affording the guests a view of the graceful fountain on the one side and of the garden on the other. In winter wooden frames, with heavy hangings, were erected across these openings and that of the tablinum, for the Romans soon found the necessity for modifying the arrangements which, although well suited for an Italian climate, were wholly unfit for that of Britain. The opening in the centre of the atrium was then closed with an awning of oiled canvas, which admitted a certain amount of light to pass, but prevented the passage of rain and snow, and kept out much of the cold. There was a narrow passage between the atrium and the peristylium; this was called the fauces. Above the chambers round the atrium was a second story, approached by a staircase from the peristylium; here were the apartments of the ladies and of the female slaves.

As Beric entered the atrium, a man, who was reading a roll of parchment, rose to his feet.

“Welcome, Beric!” he said warmly.

“All hail, preceptor!” the lad replied. “Are all well here?”

“All well, Beric. We had looked to see you before, and Berenice has been constantly asking me when you were coming.”

“I had been absent over four years, you see,” Beric replied, “and it was not easy to get away from home again. Now I must speak to Caius.” He crossed the apartment, and stood at the entrance to the tablinum. Caius looked up from a military treatise he was perusing.

“Ah, Beric! it is you! I am glad to see you again, though I am sorry to observe that you have abandoned our fashions and taken to the native garb again.”

“It was necessary, Caius,” Beric said. “I should have lost all influence with the tribe had I not laid aside my Roman dress. As it is, they regard me with some doubt, as one too enamoured of Roman customs.”

“We have heard of you, Beric, and, indeed, report says that you speak well of us, and are already famous for your relations of our history.”

===Chinese===

后院再往后，是正厅——盖乌斯接待贵客的地方；尤利娅在世时，殖民地的夫人们也在这里饮宴。同餐厅一样，这间屋子两头也半敞着，客人一头望得见那座秀雅的喷泉，一头望得见花园。到了冬天，这些敞口连同书房那面的敞口，都要架上木框、挂上厚帘——罗马人很快发现：这些在意大利气候里恰到好处的安排，放到不列颠全然不合用。届时中堂正中的天口，用油布天棚罩住，透进一些光，挡住雨雪，也隔掉大半寒气。中堂同后院之间，有一条窄过道，唤作咽喉道。中堂四周的卧房上头还有一层楼，由后院的楼梯上去；楼上，是女眷和女奴的住处。

贝里克走进中堂时，一个正读着羊皮卷的男人站起身来。

“欢迎你，贝里克！”他热络地说。

“您好，老师！”少年答道，“家里都好么？”

“都好，贝里克。我们原盼你早些来，贝伦妮刻一个劲儿地问我，你什么时候到。”

“现在，我得同盖乌斯说句话。”贝里克穿过堂屋，立在书房门口。盖乌斯从正读着的一卷兵书里抬起头来。

“啊，贝里克！是你！再见着你，我很高兴；只是遗憾地看到，你丢开了咱们的装束，又穿起土人的衣裳了。”

“这是不得已的，盖乌斯，”贝里克说，“不脱下罗马衣裳，我在部落里就要一点分量没有了。就这样，他们还拿怀疑的眼睛看我，当我太贪恋罗马的风俗呢。”

“你的事我们听说了，贝里克；说实在的，人家讲你说咱们的好，讲咱们的史事，已经讲出了名。”

===Original===

“I thought it well that my countrymen should know your great deeds,” Beric said, “and should see by what means you have come to rule the world. I received nought but kindness at your hands, and no prisoner’s lot was ever made more easy than mine. To you and yours I am deeply grateful. If your people all behaved as kindly towards the natives of this country as you did to me, Britain would be conquered without need of drawing sword from scabbard.”

“I know not that, Beric; to rule, one should be strong as well as kind. Still, as you know, I think that things might have been arranged far less harshly than they have been. It was needful that we should show ourselves to be masters; but I regret the harshness that has been too often used, and I would that not one of us here, from the governor down to the poorest soldier, was influenced by a desire for gain, but that each was animated, as he assuredly should be, only by a desire to uphold the glory and power of Rome. But that would be expecting too much from human nature, and even among you there are plenty ready to side against their countrymen for the sake of Roman gold. In that they have less excuse than we. Custom and habit have made our wants many, and all aim at attaining the luxuries of the rich. On the other hand, your wants are few, and I see not that the piling up of wealth adds in any way to your happiness.”

“That is true, Caius. I quite agree with you that it is far more excusable for a Roman to covet wealth than for a Briton; and while I blame many officials and soldiers for the harshness with which they strive to wring all their possessions from my countrymen, I deem their conduct as worthy and honourable when compared with that of Britons who sell their country for your gold.”

===Chinese===

“我原想，该让我的同胞知道你们的伟业，”贝里克说，“也让他们看看，你们凭什么得了天下。我在你们手里受的净是恩义，再没有哪个囚徒的日子，比我的更松快了。对您和您一家，我深深感恩。你们的人若是个个都像您待我这样待这地方的土人，不列颠不用拔剑出鞘，就征服了。”

“那倒未必，贝里克；要治国，光有仁慈不够，还得有铁腕。不过你也知道，我的看法是：事情本可以安排得远不像如今这样苛刻。咱们必须摆出主子的威势，这有必要；可动辄横暴，我一向引以为憾。但愿咱们在这里的每一个人，从总督到最穷的大兵，都没有一个贪财的心，人人一心，只想着撑起罗马的光荣和权势——人人本该如此。可这指望，对人性的要求太高了；就连你们中间，肯为罗马的金子出手对付自己同胞的，也大有人在。论起情有可原，他们比咱们更没有借口。风俗和习惯，把咱们的欲求养得太多，人人都盯着富人那份奢华。你们呢，欲求本来少；依我看，财货堆成山，并不给你们添半分快活。”

“这话不假，盖乌斯。罗马人贪财，比不列颠人贪财情有可原得多——这一点我全同意你。官员和兵卒用狠手段从我同胞身上刮钱刮产，我怪他们；可同拿国家去换你们金子的不列颠人一比，他们的行径，倒算得上端正体面了。”

===Original===

“We must take the world as we find it, Beric. We may regret that greed and the love of luxury should influence men, as we may grieve that they are victims of other base passions; but it is of no use quarrelling with human nature. Certain it is that all vices bring their own punishment, and that the Romans were a far nobler race when they were poor and simple, in the days of the early consuls, than they are now, with all their power, their riches, and their luxuries. Such is the history of all peoples﻿—of Egypt, of Persia, of Greece, and Carthage; and methinks that Rome, too, will run the course of other nations, and that some day, far distant maybe, she will sink beneath the weight of her power and her luxury, and that some younger and more vigorous people will, bit by bit, wrest her dominions from her and rule in her place.

“As yet, happily, I see no signs of failing in her powers. She is still vigorous, and even in the distant outskirts of the empire the wave of conquest flows onward. Happily for us, I think, it can flow no farther this way; there is but one island beyond this to conquer, and then, as in Western Gaul and Iberia, the ocean says to Rome, ‘Thou shalt go no farther.’ Would that to the south, the east, and north a similar barrier checked our progress, then we could rest and be content, and need no longer waste our strength in fresh conquests, or in opposing the incursions of hordes of barbarians from regions unknown to us even by report. I could wish myself, Beric, that nature had placed your island five days’ sail from the coasts of Gaul, instead of placing it within sight. Then I might have been enjoying life in my villa among the Tuscan hills with my daughter, instead of being exposed at any moment to march with the Legion against the savage mountaineers of the west. Ah! here comes Berenice,” he broke off, as his daughter, attended by her old nurse, entered the atrium from the vestibule. She hastened her steps as she saw Beric standing before her father in the tablinum.

“I knew you would come back, Beric, because you promised me; but you have been a long time in keeping your word.”

===Chinese===

“世界是什么样子，咱们就只能当它是什么样子，贝里克。贪心和奢欲左右着人，我们可以叹；人叫别的下作情欲拿住，我们也可以悲；可同人性怄气，没有用处。千真万确的是：一切恶行自带它的罚——当年的罗马人，在早期执政官那种贫穷简朴的日子里，比今天这个握着权势、财富和奢华的罗马，高贵得多。列国的历史，都是这一条：埃及如此，波斯如此，希腊和迦太基也如此。依我看，罗马也逃不脱列国这条老路：有朝一日——兴许远得很——它要在自己的权势和奢华底下沉下去，一个更年轻、更有血气的民族，会一块一块把它的疆土夺过去，坐上它的位子。

“所幸眼下我还看不出它气力衰竭的迹象。它依然强健，连帝国极远的边地，征服的浪头也还在朝前涌。为我们着想，倒算万幸：往这边，这浪涌不动了；再过去只剩一座岛可夺，往后就像在高卢西部和伊比利亚一样，大洋对罗马说：‘你到此为止。’但愿在南边、东边和北面，也有这样一道栏挡住咱们的脚步，那咱们就能歇下来、知足了，不必再把力气耗在新的征服上，也不必再去挡那些连传闻都没到过咱们耳朵的荒地来的蛮族。贝里克，我自己常常想：造化若把你这座岛放在离高卢海岸五天航路的地方，而不是抬眼望得见，那多好。那样，我这会儿就可以同女儿在托斯卡纳山间的庄园里享清福，不必随时提防着随军团开拔，去对付西边山里的蛮子。啊！贝伦妮刻来了。”他收住话头——女儿由老奶妈陪着，正从前厅进来；一眼望见贝里克立在书房里父亲跟前，她加快了脚步。

“我知道你会回来的，贝里克，因为你应过我；可你这应许，拖了好久才兑现。”

===Original===

“I am not my own master at home, any more than I was here, Berenice,” he said, “and my mother would not hear before of my leaving her. I have only come now for an hour’s visit, to see that all goes well in this house, and to tell you that I had not forgotten my promise; the next time I hope to pay a longer visit. At daybreak tomorrow we have a party to hunt the wolves, which have so multiplied as to become a danger in the forests of late.”

“I should like to go out to see a wolf hunt, Beric.”

“I fear that would not be possible,” he said; “the woods are thick and tangled, and we have to force our way through to get to their lair.”

“But last winter they came close to the town, and I heard that some came even into the streets.”

“Yes, they will do so when driven by hunger; but they were hunting then and not being hunted. No, Berenice, I fear that your wish to see a wolf hunt cannot be gratified; they are savage beasts, and are great trouble and no loss to us. In winter they carry off many children, and sometimes devour grown up people, and in times of long snow have been known to attack large parties, and, in spite of a stout resistance by the men, to devour them. In summer they are only met singly, but in winter they go in packs and kill numbers of our cattle.”

“I should like to go into the woods,” the girl said earnestly, “I am tired of this town. My father says he will take me with him some day when he goes west, but so far I have seen nothing except this town and Verulamium, and the country was all just as it is here, fields and cultivation. We could see the forests in the distance, but that was all. My father says, that if we went west, we should travel for miles through the forest and should sleep in tents, but that we cannot do it till everything is quiet and peaceful. Oh, Beric! I do wish the Britons would not be always fighting.”

Beric smiled. “The British girls, Berenice, say they wish the Romans would not be always fighting.”

===Chinese===

“在家里，我同在这里一样，当不了自己的主，贝伦妮刻，”他说，“我母亲先前不肯听我提走。这回，我也只能来坐一个钟头，看看这一家子可安好，也告诉你们：我没有忘了我的许诺。下一回，我盼着能多住几天。明天天一亮，我们要结伙去猎狼——狼近来繁殖得太凶，林子里已经成了祸患。”

“我倒想去看一回猎狼呢，贝里克。”

“只怕去不成，”他说，“林子又密又乱，得一路砍出道来，才到得了狼窝。”

“可去年冬天，狼都逼近城边了，我还听说有几只窜进了街里。”

“是的，饿急了它们就来；不过那是它们在猎，不是被猎。不，贝伦妮刻，你想看猎狼的心愿怕是难了遂；它们是凶兽，于我们是天大的麻烦，死了毫不可惜。冬天它们叼走许多孩子，有时连大人也吃；大雪连绵的年头，它们还敢袭击大帮的人，任凭汉子们死命抵抗，照样把人吃掉。夏天只见单只，冬天成群结队，咬死我们好多牛。”

“我想进林子里去，”姑娘恳切地说，“这城我住腻了。父亲说，哪天去西边，带我一起去；可到如今，我除了这座城和维鲁拉米恩，哪儿也没去过，乡下的光景同这里一模一样：田啊，庄稼啊。远处的林子倒望得见，可也就望望罢了。父亲说，往西去，要在林子里走上好多里，夜里睡帐篷；可是一切太平安定了才能去。哦，贝里克！我真希望不列颠人别老是打仗。”

贝里克笑了。“不列颠的姑娘们呢，贝伦妮刻，她们希望罗马人别老是打仗。”

===Original===

“It is very troublesome,” she said pettishly. “I should like everyone to be friends, and then there would be no need to have so many soldiers in Britain, and perhaps the emperor would order our legions home. Father says that we ought to look upon this as home now, for that the legion may remain here for years and years; but he said the other day that he thought that if everything was quiet here he should, when I am sixteen years old, obtain leave from the governor, and go back to Rome for two or three years, and I think, though he has not said so outright, that he will perhaps retire and settle there.”

“It would be much the best for you,” Beric said earnestly. “I should be sorry, because you have been very kind to me, and I should grieve were you to leave me altogether; but there may be trouble here again some day, and I think it would be far better for you to be back in Rome, where you would have all the pleasures and delights of the great capital, and live in ease and comfort, without the risk of your father having to march away to the wars. I know that if I were your father I would take you back. He says that his villa there is exactly like this, and you have many relations there, and there must be all sorts of pleasures and grand spectacles far beyond anything there is here. I am sure it would be better for you, and happier.”

“I thought that you would be quite sorry,” she said gravely.

“So I shall be very sorry for myself,” Beric said; “as, next to my own mother, there is no one I care for so much as you and your father. I shall miss you terribly; but yet I am so sure that it would be best for you to be at home with your own people, that I should be glad to hear that your father was going to take you back to Rome.”

But Berenice did not altogether accept the explanation. She felt really hurt that Beric should view even the possibility of her going away with equanimity, and she very shortly went off to her own apartment; while a few minutes later, Beric, after bidding goodbye to Caius, started to rejoin Boduoc, whom he found waiting at the edge of the forest.

That evening Berenice said to her father, “I was angry with Beric today, father.”

“Were you, child? what about?”

===Chinese===

“真是烦透了，”她撅着嘴说，“我巴不得人人都做朋友，那就不需要在不列颠屯这么多兵，兴许皇帝还会把咱们的军团召回老家去呢。父亲说，咱们如今就该把这里当家看了，军团兴许一待就是许多许多年；可他前几天又说，我想：倘若这里一切太平，等我满了十六岁，他就向总督告假，带我回罗马住上两三年；还有——他没明说，可我想得出来——他多半就此告老，在那里落了户。”

“那样对你们最好不过，”贝里克恳切地说，“我心里会难受，因为你们待我极好，你们要是把我整个撇下，我会伤心的；可这里说不定哪天又要出乱子，你们回罗马去要好得多：京城的种种热闹快乐都归你，日子过得安逸舒服，也不用担心你父亲被调去打仗。我要是你父亲，我就带你回去。他说他那里那座庄园同这一模一样；你在那边又有很多亲戚；那边必定还有各式各样的乐子和盛典，这里有的根本没法比。我拿得准：那样对你更好，也更快乐。”

“我还当你听了会挺难过呢。”她正色说。

“我自己当然会很难过，”贝里克说，“除了我母亲，我再没有像疼你和疼你父亲这样疼的人了。我会想你们想得厉害；可我又那样确信，你回到自己人身边最妥当，所以一听说你父亲要带你回罗马，我反倒会高兴。”

可贝伦妮刻没有全受下这个解释。一想到贝里克听见她可能要走，居然这样平平静静，她心里真觉着委屈，不多时就回自己房里去了。几分钟后，贝里克辞过盖乌斯，动身回林子边找波杜克；波杜克果然候在林缘。

当天晚上，贝伦妮刻对父亲说：“我今天生贝里克的气了，爸爸。”

“是么，孩子？为了什么？”

===Original===

“I told him that perhaps in another three years, when I was sixteen, you would take me to Rome, and that I thought, perhaps, if we went there you would not come back again; and instead of being very much grieved, as I thought he would, he seemed quite pleased at the idea. Of course he said he was sorry, but he did not really seem to be, and he says he thought it would be very much better for me. I thought he was grateful, father, and liked us very much, and now I am quite disappointed in him.”

Caius was silent for a minute or two.

“I do not think Beric is ungrateful,” he said, “and I am sure that he likes us, Berenice.”

“He said he did, father, that he cared for us more than anyone except his mother; but if he cared for us, surely he would be very, very sorry for us to go away.”

“Beric is a Briton, my dear, and we are Romans. By this time he must have thoroughly learned his people’s feelings towards us. I have never believed, as some do, that Britain is as yet completely conquered, and that when we have finished with the Silures in the west our work will be completely done.

“Beric, who knows his countrymen, may feel this even more strongly than I do, and may know that, sooner or later, there will be another great effort on the part of the Britons to drive us out. It may be a year, and it may be twenty, but I believe myself that some day we shall have a fierce struggle to maintain our hold here, and Beric, who may see this also, and who knows the feeling of his countrymen, may wish that we should be away before the storm comes.

===Chinese===

“我告诉他，兴许再过三年，等我十六岁，您就带我去罗马；又说，我想我们要是去了，您也许就不回来了。我原当他听了会难过得很，谁知他一听这主意，倒像挺高兴。他嘴上说舍不得，可瞧着不像真心舍不得；还说，他想那样对我是好上加好。我原以为他知咱们的情、真心喜欢咱们呢，这下我可看错他了。”

盖乌斯沉默了一两分钟。

“我看贝里克不是忘恩的人，”他说，“而且我拿得准，他喜欢咱们，贝伦妮刻。”

“他是这么说的，爸爸；他说除了他母亲，再没有比咱们更叫他挂心的人了。可他要是真挂心咱们，咱们要走，他该非常非常难过才是。”

“贝里克是不列颠人，孩子，咱们是罗马人。到如今，他必把他族人对咱们的心思摸透了。我不像有些人那样，信不列颠已经服服帖帖、等咱们收拾完西边的西尔人便大事全毕。

“贝里克识得他的同胞，这一层他兴许比我看得还透：不列颠人要把咱们赶出去，早晚会再来一次大的。也许一年，也许二十年，可我自己相信：总有一天，咱们要打一场恶仗，才守得住这里。贝里克兴许也看见了这一层；他既知道同胞的心气，自然巴不得风暴来之前，咱们已经走远。

===Original===

“There is but little doubt, Berenice, that we despise these people too much, still less that we treat them harshly and cruelly. Were I propraetor of Britain, I would rule them differently. I am but the commander of a legion, and my duty is but to rule my men. I would punish, and punish sternly, all attempts at rising; but I would give them no causes for discontent. We treat them as if their spirit were altogether broken, as if they and their possessions were but our chattels, as if they possessed no rights, not even the right to live. Some day we shall find our mistake, and when the time comes the awakening will be a rude one. It is partly because I see dimly the storm gathering in the distance that I long to be home again. As long as your mother lived this seemed a home to me, now I desire rest and quiet. I have done my share of fighting, I have won honour enough, and I may look before long to be a general; but I have had enough of it, and long for my quiet villa in the Alban hills, with an occasional visit to Rome, where you can take part in its gaieties, and I can have the use of the libraries stored with the learning of the world. So do not think harshly of Beric, my child; he may see the distant storm more plainly than I do. I am sure that he cares for us, and if he is glad at the news that we are going, it is because he wishes us away and in safety before the trouble comes.

===Chinese===

“咱们把这些人看得太低、待得太苛、太狠，这没有多少疑问，贝伦妮刻。我若是不列颠的总督，治理的法子就两样了。我不过是一个军团的统领，我的职分只是管好我的兵。有人敢反，我惩办，而且严惩；可我不给他们记恨的因由。咱们如今待他们，只当他们的气性已经断绝，只当他们和他们的家当都是咱们的私产，只当他们没有任何权利——连活命的权利都没有。总有一天，咱们会发现自己错了；等到那一天，这一记醒，会醒得疼。我多少瞧见远处正聚着风暴，这也是我想回老家的一层缘故。你母亲在世时，这里于我还像个家；如今我只求安稳清静。仗我打够了，荣名也挣够了，再过不久，有望升到将军；可我够本了，一心想回阿尔班山里那座清净的庄园，隔三差五进一回罗马——你在那里有你的热闹，我呢，守着那些收藏了天下学问的书斋。所以别把贝里克想得太坏，我的孩子：他兴许比我更看清了那场远处的风暴。我拿得准他是疼咱们的；他听了咱们要走的消息高兴，正因为他盼着大祸到来之前，咱们已脱身到了安稳地方。”

===Original===

“Nero has come to the imperial throne, and the men he is sending hither are of a widely different stamp from the lieutenants of Claudius. The latter knew that the Britons can fight, and that, wild and untutored as they are, it needed all the skill and courage of Ostorius and Vespasian to reduce them to order. The newcomers regard them as slaves to be trampled upon, robbed, and ill used as they choose. I am sure they will find their mistake. As long as they deal only with the tribes thoroughly subdued, the Trinobantes, the Cantii, the Belgae, and the Dumnonii, all may be quiet; they dare not move. But the Iceni and Brigantes, although they both have felt the weight of our swords, are still partly independent, and if pressed too severely will assuredly revolt, and if they give the signal all Britain may be up in arms again. I am scoffed at if I venture to hint to these newcomers that there is life yet in Britain. Dwelling here in a Roman city, it seems to them absurd that there can be danger from the savages who roam in the forests that stretch away from beyond the river at our very feet to the far distant north, to regions of which we are absolutely ignorant. I regard what Beric has said as another warning.”

“But I thought that Beric was our friend, father, and you told me you had heard that he was teaching his countrymen how great is our history.”

“Beric is a Briton in the midst of Britons, child. He is a partially tamed wolf cub, and had he been sent to Rome and remained there he would have done credit to our teaching. He is fond of study, and at the same time fond of arms; he might have turned out a wise citizen or a valiant soldier. But this was not done. He has gone back again among the wolves, and whatever his feelings towards us personally may be, he must side with his own people. Did they suspect him of being Roman at heart they would tear him in pieces. I believe that as he knows our strength, and that in the end we must conquer, his influence will always be on the side of peace; but if arms are taken up he will have no choice but to side with his countrymen, and should it be another ten years before the cloud bursts, he may be one of our most formidable opponents. Don’t blame him, child; he only shows his regard for you, by wishing you back safely in Rome before trouble arises.”

===Chinese===

“尼禄登了帝位，他往这里派的人，同克劳狄乌斯麾下那些副将，全然是两个坯子。旧人知道不列颠人能打：别看他们野蛮未开化，当年奥斯托利乌斯和韦帕芗用尽了本事和胆气，才把他们压服。新来的这些人呢，只当他们是奴隶，随他们践踏、抢夺、糟蹋。我拿得准他们要吃亏。只要他们打交道的，尽是彻底降服了的部落——特里诺班特人、肯特人、贝尔盖人、杜姆诺尼人——天下自会太平：那些人不敢动。可爱西尼和布里甘特人，虽说都领教过咱们的剑，却还留着半分自主；逼得太狠，必定反；他们一举旗，全不列颠可能重新拿起武器。我朝这些新人提一句‘不列颠还有活气’，就招人讥笑。他们住在这座罗马城里，觉得脚边河对岸直到极北——那些咱们连传闻都摸不着边的地方——绵延无尽的森林里游荡的野人，能有什么危险，简直可笑。贝里克今天说的这番话，我当作又一记警钟。”

“可我以为贝里克是咱们的朋友呢，爸爸；您还告诉过我，听说他在教他的同胞，讲咱们的历史多么伟大。”

“贝里克如今是不列颠人堆里的不列颠人，孩子。他是一头半驯的狼崽；当初若把他送去罗马、留在那里，他必能替咱们的教化争脸。他既爱读书，又爱武艺，本可以长成一位贤明的公民，或是一员勇武的战将。可是没有这样安排。他回到狼群里去了；他同咱们私人之间的情分且不论，到了关头，他必站在他族人那一边。族人若疑他心向罗马，会把他撕碎。我相信，他既知咱们的实力、又知咱们终归要赢，他的话锋必总在和平这一边；可一旦刀兵起，他没有第二条路，只能同同胞站在一起。倘若这团乌云再过十年才炸开，他也许会成为咱们顶厉害的对手之一。别怪他，孩子；他盼你趁祸事未起、平平安安回罗马去，这正是在疼你。”

===Original===

“You are just in time, Beric,” Boduoc said as the young chief joined him. “The sun is but a hand’s breadth above that hill. Here are your spear and sword where you hid them, though why you should have done it I know not, seeing that they have not yet ventured to order us to disarm.”

“And if they did we should not obey them, Boduoc; but as the Trinobantes have long been forbidden to carry arms, it might have caused trouble had I gone armed into the town, and we don’t want trouble at present. I went on a peaceful visit, and there was no occasion for me to carry my weapons. But give me a piece of that deer flesh and an oaten cake; we have a long march before us.”

“Why, did you not eat with them?”

“No. I was, of course, invited, but I had but a short time to stop and did not wish it to seem as if I had come for a taste of Roman dainties again.”

As soon as the meal was eaten they set out. It was but a track through the forest, for although the trees had been cleared away for a width of twenty feet there was but little traffic, for the road was seldom traversed, save by an occasional messenger from Prasutagus. It had been used by the legions at the time that Ostorius had built a line of forts stretching from the Nen to the Severn, and by it they had advanced when the Iceni had risen; but from that time it had been unused by them, as the Iceni had paid their tribute regularly, and held aloof from all hostile movements against them. Prasutagus was always profuse in his assurance of friendship towards Rome, and save that the Roman officers visited his capital once a year to receive their tribute, they troubled but little about the Iceni, having their hands occupied by their wars in the south and west, while their main road to the north ran far to the west of Camalodunum.

“We shall arrive about midnight,” Beric said as they strode along.

“We may or we may not,” Boduoc said curtly.

“What is to prevent us, Boduoc?”

===Chinese===

“你来得正好，贝里克，”少年首领归队时，波杜克说，“太阳离那座山只剩一掌高了。你的矛和剑在这儿，你藏的地方——虽说藏它做什么，我不明白：他们还没胆量叫咱们缴械。”

“真叫缴，咱们也不缴，波杜克；不过特里诺班特人早就不许带兵器了，我带着兵器进城，免不了惹麻烦——眼下咱们不要麻烦。我走的是亲戚，用不着兵器。给我一块鹿肉、一张燕麦饼就好；前头的路长着呢。”

“怎么，你没在他们家吃饭？”

“没有。请是请了的；可我待的工夫短，也不愿叫人看着，我又来尝罗马点心的味道。”

吃完，两人上路。那不过是一条穿林的便道：树虽清出了二十英尺宽，来往却稀少，除了普拉苏塔古斯那边偶尔的信使，难得有人走。当年奥斯托利乌斯修那道从宁河连到塞文河的堡垒线，军团走的就是它；爱西尼人造反时，他们也是打这条道进的兵。可打那以后，罗马人便没用过它：爱西尼人的贡赋年年交足，同一切反罗马的举动也一概不沾。普拉苏塔古斯对罗马的友谊，向来表白得殷勤备至；除了罗马军官一年一赴他的都城受贡，他们也不大理会爱西尼人——南边西边的战事缠着手，他们通往北方的大道，又远在卡马卢杜努姆以西。

“咱们半夜前后能到。”两人大步走着，贝里克说。

“到得了到不了，难说。”波杜克没好气。

“什么东西拦咱们，波杜克？”

===Original===

“Well, the wolves may prevent us, Beric; we heard them howling several times as we came along this morning. The rapacious brutes have not been so bold for years, and it is high time that we hunted them down, or at any rate made our part of the country too hot to hold them. I told Borgon before I started that if we did not return by an hour after midnight it would be because we had been obliged to take to a tree, and that he had better bring out a party at the first break of day to rescue us.”

“But we have never had any trouble of that kind while we have been hunting, Boduoc.”

“No; but I think there must have been some great hunts up in Norfolk, and that the brutes have come south. Certain it is that there have in the last week been great complaints of them, and, as you know, it was for that reason that your mother ordered all the men of the tribe to assemble by tomorrow morning to make war against them. The people in the farms and villages are afraid to stay out after nightfall. No man with arms in his hands fears a wolf, or even two or three of them, in the daytime; but when they are in packs they are formidable assailants, even to a strong party. Things are getting as bad now as they were twenty years ago. My father has told me that during one hard winter they destroyed full half our herds, and that hundreds of people were devoured by them. They had to erect stockades round the villages and drive in all the cattle, and half the men kept guard by turns, keeping great fires alight to frighten them away. When we have cleared the land of those two legged wolves the Romans, we shall have to make a general war upon them, for truly they are becoming a perfect scourge to the land. It is not like the wild boar, of which there might with advantage be more, for they do but little harm, getting their food for the most part in the woods, and furnishing us with good eating as well as good sport. But the wolves give us nothing in return, and save for the sport no one would trouble to hunt them; and it is only by a general order for their destruction, or by the offer of a reward for their heads, that we shall get rid of them.”

“Well, let us press on, Boduoc. I would not that anything should occur to prevent us starting with the rest in the morning.”

===Chinese===

“狼啊，贝里克。今儿早上过来，狼嚎咱们就听见好几回。这帮贪嘴的畜生，多少年没这样猖狂了，是时候围剿它们了——至少，叫咱们这一带叫它们待不下去。动身前我告诉博尔贡：过了半夜一点我们还没到，那就是上了树了，叫他天一亮就带人出来接应。”

“可咱们打猎，从来没出过这路事，波杜克。”

“是没有。可我看北边诺福克那边必是来了几场大猎，把这帮畜生撵南边来了。上礼拜告它们的状可不少，这你是知道的；正为这个，你母亲才传令全族男丁，明天一早齐集，对狼开战。如今农庄村里的人，天一黑就不敢出门。白天，手里有兵器的汉子，一头狼、两三头狼都不怕；可它们成了群，就是一伙硬扎的对手，大帮的人也吃力。眼下这事，坏得同二十年前一样了。我父亲告诉我，有一年苦冬，它们糟蹋了咱们几乎一半的牛，几百口人叫它们吃了。各村子不得不围起栅栏，把牛全赶进来，半数男人轮班守夜，生起大火吓它们。等咱们把两条腿的狼——罗马人——清出这片地，回头还得对这帮四条的来一场总剿；说真的，它们快成这片地的一场瘟了。它们不比野猪：野猪多些倒有好处，为害不大，吃的多半在林子里找，还供咱们好肉、好猎。狼呢，什么也不给；若不为猎趣，谁也不耐烦猎它。非得下一道总剿的令，或者悬赏狼头，才除得净。”

“那就赶路吧，波杜克。明早可别因为咱们误了正事。”

===Original===

“We are walking a good pace now,” Boduoc said, “and shall gain but little by going faster. One cannot run for six hours; and besides it is as much as we can do to walk fast in the dark. Did we try to run we should like enough fall over a stump or root, and maybe not arrive there even though the wolves stopped us not.”

For two hours more they strode along. Boduoc’s eyes had been trained by many a long night spent among the woods, and dark as it was beneath the overarching trees, he was able to discern objects around him, and kept along in his regular stride as surely and almost as noiselessly as a wild beast; but the four years spent in the Roman town had impaired Beric’s nocturnal vision; and though he had done much hunting since his return home, he was far from being able to use his eyes as his companion did, and he more than once stumbled over the roots that crossed the path.

“You will be on your head presently,” Boduoc growled.

“It is all very well for you, Boduoc, who have the eyes of a cat; but you must remember we are travelling in the dark, and although I can make out the trunks on either hand the ground is all black to me, and I am walking quite at hazard.”

“It is not what I should call a light night,” Boduoc admitted.

“Well, no, considering that there is no moon, and that the clouds that were rising when the sun went down have overspread all the sky. I don’t see that it could well be darker.”

“Well we will stop at that hut in the little clearing, somewhere about half a mile on, and get a couple of torches. If you were to fall and twist your foot you would not be able to hunt tomorrow.”

“What is that?” Beric exclaimed as a distant cry came to their ears.

“I think it is the voice of a woman,” Boduoc said. “Or maybe it is one of the spirits of evil.”

Beric during his stay among the Romans had lost faith in most of his superstitions. “Nonsense, Boduoc! it was the cry of a woman; it came from ahead. Maybe some woman returning late has been attacked by wolves. Come along,” he shouted, and he started to run, followed reluctantly by his companion.

“Stop, Beric, stop!” he said in a short time, “I hear other sounds.”

“So do I,” Beric agreed, but without checking his pace. “My eyes may be dull, Boduoc, but they are not so dull as your ears. Why, don’t you know the snarling of wolves when you hear them?”

===Chinese===

“咱们如今步子已经不慢，”波杜克说，“再快也快不出多少。六个钟头的路，人跑不下来；再说黑地里，能快走就已经是极限了。真要跑，十有八九栽在树桩树根上，狼不来拦，只怕也到不了。”

两人又走了两个钟头。波杜克那双眼睛，是在林子里熬了许多长夜练出来的；头顶枝叶交覆、地上一团漆黑，他照样辨得出四下的物事，迈着不紧不慢的大步，稳得、轻得几乎同野兽一样。贝里克那四年罗马城的日子，却把夜眼养钝了；回家后虽说打了不少猎，离他同伴那双眼睛还差得远，路上横着的树根，他不止一次绊了脚。

“再走，你就头朝下了。”波杜克咕哝。

“你说得轻巧，波杜克，你长着一对猫眼；可你得记着，咱们是摸黑赶路。两边树干我勉强认得出，地面对我全是一团黑，我等于瞎走。”

“要依我说，今晚也称不得亮。”波杜克认了。

“可不是：没有月亮，日头落时起的那层云，又铺满了天。再黑，黑不到哪里去了。”

“那就在前头半里上下、林中小空场那间窝棚歇一歇，讨两支火把。你要摔一跤崴了脚，明天就猎不成狼了。”

“那是什么？”一声遥远的叫喊传进耳朵，贝里克失声问。

“我听是个女人的声音，”波杜克说，“要么，是哪路邪祟。”

在罗马人中间住过这几年，贝里克对旧时那些迷信，大半已不信了。“胡说，波杜克！是女人喊。就在前头。多半是哪个晚归的女人撞上狼了。走！”他喊了一声，拔腿就跑，同伴老大不情愿地跟上。

“站住，贝里克，站住！”跑了一阵，波杜克说，“我听见别的动静了。”

“我也听见，”贝里克应道，脚下没停，“我的眼睛兴许钝，波杜克，可还没你耳朵钝。怎么，狼的龇牙声你听不出来？”

===Original===

Again the loud cry of distress came on the night air. “They have not seized her yet,” Beric said. “Her first cry would have been her last had they done so. She must be in that hut, Boduoc, and they are trying to get at her. Maybe her husband is away.”

“It is wolves,” Boduoc agreed in a tone of relief. “Since that is all I am ready for them; but sword and spear are of no avail against the spirits of the air. We must be careful though, or instead of us attacking we may be attacked.”

Beric paid no attention. They had as they passed the hut that morning stopped for a drink of water there, and he saw now before his eyes the tall comely young woman with a baby in her arms and two children hanging to her skirts. In a short time they stood at the edge of the little clearing by the side of the path. It was lighter here, and he could make out the outline of the rude hut, and, as he thought, that of many dark figures moving round it. A fierce growling and snarling rose from around the hut, with once or twice a sharp yell of pain.

“There are half a dozen of them on the roof,” Boduoc said, “and a score or more round the hut. At present they haven’t winded us, for the air is in our faces.”

“I think we had best make a rush at them, Boduoc, shouting at the top of our voices as we go, and bidding the woman stand in readiness to unbar the door. They will be scared for a moment, not knowing how many of us there may be, and once inside we shall be safe from them.”

“Let us get as near as we can before we begin to shout, Beric. They may run back a few paces at our voice, but will speedily rally.”

Holding their spears in readiness for action they ran forward. When within thirty yards of the hut Boduoc raised his voice in a wild yell, Beric adding his cry and then shouting, “Unbar your door and stand to close it as we enter.”

There was, however, no occasion for haste. Boduoc’s sudden yell completely scared the wolves, and with whimpers of dismay they scattered in all directions. The door opened as Beric and his companion came up, and they rushed in and closed it after them. A fire burned on the hearth. A dead wolf lay on the ground, the children crouched in terror on a pile of rushes, and a woman stood with a spear in her hand.

===Chinese===

那声凄厉的呼救又一次划破夜空。“还没扑上她，”贝里克说，“真扑上了，头一声就是末一声。她必定在那间窝棚里，波杜克；狼在想法子够到她。多半她男人不在家。”

“是狼，”波杜克松了口气，“只是狼，那我随时奉陪；可刀剑对付不了天上的邪祟。不过咱们得当心：别攻不成，反叫它们围了。”

贝里克没听他絮叨。早上路过那间窝棚时，他们讨过水喝；此刻，他眼前又是那个高挑标致的年轻女人：怀里一个吃奶的娃，裙边拽着两个小儿。不多时，两人已立在道旁那片小空场的边上。这里亮些，窝棚的粗轮廓认得出；在他看来，还有许多黑影绕着它转。窝棚四周腾起一片凶恶的嗥叫和低吼，其间夹着一两声尖利的痛嚎。

“房顶上有六七头，”波杜克说，“房周围二十头还多。眼下它们还没闻见咱们——风正对着咱们的脸。”
