Beric was received with loud acclamations by the Iceni, and was escorted by a shouting multitude to the queen, who had taken her place at a point where she could watch the operations. She held out her hand to him. “You have succeeded, Beric,” she said; “and my thanks and those of all here﻿—nay, of all Britain﻿—are due to you. In half an hour the temple will be open to attack.”

“Hardly in that time, queen,” he replied. “The faggots will doubtless have done their work by then, but it will be hours before the embers and stonework will be sufficiently cool to enable men to pass over them to the assault.”

“We can wait,” the queen said. “A messenger, who left the camp of Cerealis at daybreak, has just arrived, and at that hour nothing was known to the Romans of our attack here. They will not now arrive until tomorrow.”

===Chinese===

第二排照做，第三排照做，一路做到只剩两排。这两排的两端各二人，登上柴堆，把柴捆搁上顶去，站着的只剩六人。贝里克居中；此刻他蹲下身，藏在这六捆柴的荫下，把火炬凑上柴堆。他盯着火苗往上蹿起来，才发一声令；六人撂下柴捆，随他飞快跑进侧廊——那里是安全地界：罗马人知道侧廊挨不了打，顶上没开窟窿。不列颠人看见神庙两头腾起烟柱、跟着蹿出火舌，欢喜得发了狂。火越蹿越高，直舔到头顶的顶棚。

火烧了半个钟头；到头，只剩一大堆通红的炭火，外头的人很快便能隔着它望进庙堂。庙门和门后的障碍，都毁了。一听见同胞欢呼“柴烧透了”，贝里克便吹响号；两廊的部众冲过空场，罗马人的飞矢一箭也没来搅——大祸临头的恐惧攥住了他们，哪还顾得上别人。爱西尼高声欢呼迎接贝里克，欢叫的人潮把他簇拥到王后跟前——她早选定了观战的位置。她朝他伸出手。“你成了，贝里克，”她说，“我的谢，这里的谢——不，全不列颠的谢，都该给你。半个钟头后，神庙就任凭攻打。”

“怕不止半个钟头，王后，”他答，“柴捆到时候必烧透；可余烬和石料，得凉上几个钟头，人才踩得过它们去攻。”

“等得起，”王后说，“天亮时离开塞雷利斯大营的信使刚到；那个时辰，罗马人对咱们攻城的事还一无所知。他们明天之前到不了。”

===Original===

Not until the afternoon was it considered that the entrances would be cool enough to pass through. Then the Sarci prepared for the attack, binding pieces of raw hide under their feet to protect them from the heated stonework. They were formed ten abreast. Beric took his place before the front line of one of the columns, and with levelled spears they advanced at a run towards the doors. A shower of missiles saluted them from the roof. Some fell, but the rest, pressing on in close order, dashed through the gateway and flung themselves upon the Roman soldiers drawn up to oppose their passage. The resistance was feeble. The Romans had entirely lost heart and could not for a moment sustain the weight of the charge. They were swept away from the entrance, and the Britons poured in.

Standing in groups the Romans defended themselves in desperation; but their efforts were vain, and in five minutes the last defender of the place was slain. As soon as the fight was over the whole of the Iceni rushed tumultuously forward with exultant shouts and filled the temple; then a horn sounded and a lane was made, as Boadicea, followed by her chiefs and chieftainesses, entered the temple. The queen’s face was radiant with triumph, and she would have spoken but the shouting was so loud that those near her could not obtain silence. They understood, however, when advancing to the statues of the gods that stood behind the altars, she waved her spear. In an instant the tribesmen swarmed round the statues, ropes were attached to the massive figures, and Jupiter, Mars, and Minerva fell to the ground with a crash, as did the statue of the Emperor Claudius.

===Chinese===

直到下午，两处入口才凉得能过人。萨尔西随即备战：脚底绑上生皮块，隔开滚烫的石料。他们十人一排列队。贝里克立在其中一个纵队的排头前；千矛放平，众人朝庙门跑步推进。庙顶上一阵飞矢迎接。倒了一些；余下的压着队形，抢进庙门，扑在列阵阻拦的罗马兵身上。抵抗是软的：罗马人的心气早散了，这一冲的分量，他们一瞬也扛不住。门口被扫开，不列颠人灌了进去。

罗马人成堆死斗；可是枉然，五分钟内，这里最后一个守者倒下了。战斗一歇，全体爱西尼欢呼着汹涌而入，挤满了庙堂；随后号声一响，人潮闪出一条道——波阿迪西亚领着她的酋长和女酋长们，进了神庙。王后的脸放着得胜的光。她本要说话，可欢呼声震天，近旁的人压不出片刻安静。可众人看懂了：她走到祭坛后立着的神像前，把矛一挥。刹那间，部众蚁拥而上，绳索套上那几尊巨大的神像；朱庇特、玛斯、密涅瓦，轰然倒地；克劳狄乌斯皇帝的塑像，同样栽了下来。

===Original===

A mighty shout hailed its downfall. The gods of the Britons, insulted and outraged, were avenged upon those of Rome; the altars of Mona had streamed with the blood of the Druids, those of Camalodunum were wet with the gore of Roman legionaries. The statues were broken to pieces, the altars torn down, and then the chiefs ordered the tribesmen to fetch in faggots. Thousands went to the forest, while others pulled down detached houses and sheds that had escaped the flames, and dragged the beams and woodwork to the temple. By nightfall an enormous pile of faggots was raised round each of the eight interior columns that in two lines supported the roof. Torches were applied by Boadicea, her two daughters and some of the principal Druids, and in a short time the interior of the temple was a glowing furnace. The beams of the ceiling and roof soon ignited and the flames shot up high into the air.

All day the Trinobantes had been pouring in, and a perfect frenzy of delight reigned among the great crowd looking on at the destruction of the temple that had been raised to signify and celebrate the subjugation of Britain. Women with flowing hair performed wild dances of triumph; some rushed about as if possessed with madness, uttering prophecies of the total destruction of the Romans; others foamed at the mouth and fell in convulsions, while the men were scarcely less excited over their success. Messengers had already brought in news that at midday Cerealis had learned that Camalodunum had been attacked, and that the legion was to start on the following morning to relieve the town.

The news had been taken to him by one of the Trinobantes, who had received his instructions from Aska. He was to say that the town had suddenly been attacked and that many had fallen; but the greater portion of the population had escaped to the temple, which had been vainly attacked by the Iceni. The object of this news was to induce Cerealis to move out from his fortified camp. The chiefs felt the difficulty of assaulting such a position, and though they had dreaded the arrival of Cerealis before the temple was taken, they were anxious that he should set out as soon as they saw that Beric’s plan of attack had succeeded, and that the temple was now open to their assault.

===Chinese===

一声巨吼，迎接它的倒下。受辱遭践的不列颠诸神，在罗马诸神身上报了仇：莫纳的祭坛淌过德鲁伊的血，卡马卢杜努姆的祭坛，如今泡透了罗马军团兵的污血。神像砸得粉碎，祭坛掀翻在地；随后酋长们传令部众搬柴。几千人奔向林子；另一些拆下逃过火劫的散屋和棚舍，把梁木门窗拖到庙前。入夜，支撑殿顶的两列八根内柱，每根脚下都码起了冲天的柴山。波阿迪西亚、她的两个女儿和几位大德鲁伊亲手点火；不多时，殿内成了一座喷火的洪炉。顶棚和殿顶的梁木很快烧着，火头直蹿半空。

特里诺班特人一整天川流不息地涌来；人山里一片狂欢——望着这座为宣示、庆贺不列颠臣服而起的神庙化为灰烬。披发的妇女们跳着野性的凯旋舞；有的满场疯奔，像被鬼附了，口里念着罗马人尽灭的预言；有的口吐白沫，抽搐倒地；男人们对这场胜的狂热，也不遑多让。信使早把消息带到：正午时分，塞雷利斯得知卡马卢杜努努姆被攻，军团明晨开拔来救。

这消息，是他的一名特里诺班特人送去的——奉的是阿斯卡的指点：只说城池突遭攻击，死了许多人；大半居民逃进了神庙，爱西尼攻它不克。送这个信，为的是引塞雷利斯出他那座设防的大营。酋长们明白强攻那样的阵地何等难；先前，庙未下时，他们怕他来；如今见贝里克的攻法成了、神庙唾手可得，倒巴不得他早早动身。

===Original===

At midnight the roof of the temple fell in, and nothing remained but the bare walls and the columns surrounding them. The chiefs ordered their followers to make their way through the still burning town and to gather by tribes outside the defensive works, and there lie down until morning, when they would march to meet the legion of Cerealis. At daybreak they were again afoot and on the march southward, swollen by the accession of the Trinobantes and by the arrival during the last two days of tribes who had been too late to join the rest at Cardun. The British force now numbered at least fifty thousand.

“It is a great army, Beric,” Boduoc said exultingly as they moved forward.

“It is a great host,” Beric replied. “I would that it were an army. Had they all even as much training as our men I should feel confident in the future.”

“But surely you are confident now, Beric; we have begun well.”

“We have scarcely begun at all,” Beric said. “What have we done? Destroyed a sleeping town and captured by means of fire a temple defended by four hundred men. We shall win today, that I do not doubt. The men are wrought up by their success, and the Romans are little prepared to meet such a force﻿—I doubt not that we shall beat them, but to crush a legion is not to defeat Rome. I hope, Boduoc, but I do not feel confident. Look back at the Sarci and then look round at this disordered host. Well, the Romans in discipline and order exceed the Sarci as much as we exceed the rest of the Iceni. They will be led by generals trained in war; we are led by chiefs whose only idea of war is to place themselves at the head of their tribe and rush against the enemy. Whether courage and great numbers can compensate for want of discipline remains to be seen. The history of Rome tells me that it has never done so yet.”

After five hours’ marching some fleet footed scouts sent on ahead brought in the news that the Romans were approaching. A halt was called, and the chiefs assembled round the queen’s chariot in council. Beric was summoned by a messenger from the queen.

===Chinese===

半夜，殿顶塌了；剩下的，只有四壁和环立的石柱。酋长们传令部众穿过犹自燃烧的城，按部落聚在工事外头，就地歇下，明晨开拔，去迎塞雷利斯的军团。天一亮，大军又起，向南进发——特里诺班特人来会，近两天迟到未能赶上卡尔敦集结的部落也到了，队伍愈发浩大。此刻这支不列颠大军，至少有五万人。

“好一支大军，贝里克，”开进时，波杜克扬扬得意地说。

“好一大群人，”贝里克答，“我倒愿它是一支军。哪怕人人都只及咱们的人一半的训练，我对往后也有底了。”

“可你如今总该有底了吧，贝里克；咱们开头开得多好。”

“还谈不上开头，”贝里克说，“咱们做了什么？踏平了一座睡着的城；借火攻拿下一座四百人守的庙。今天会赢，这我不疑：弟兄们叫胜利烧得热热的，罗马人又全没料着这样一支大军——赢，我看是稳的。可碾碎一个军团，不是打垮罗马。我是盼，波杜克，盼，可不是稳。回头看看萨尔西，再看看身边这一大团乱糟糟的人。罗马人在纪律和章法上超出萨尔西多少，咱们就超出其余爱西尼多少。他们带兵的，是兵事里滚出来的将；领咱们的，是酋长——他们兵法的全部，就是把自己往队伍前头一站，朝敌人扑。勇气和大兵数补不补得上没纪律的亏，走着瞧。罗马的历史告诉我：到现在还没补上过。”

行军五个钟头后，先派出去的快腿探子回报：罗马人到了。大军停下；酋长们聚到王后战车旁议事。王后遣人召贝里克。

===Original===

“You must always attend our councils,” she said when he came up. “You have proved that, young as you are, you possess a knowledge of war that more than compensates for your lack of years. You have the right, after capturing the temple for us, to take for the Sarci the post of honour in today’s battle. Choose it for yourself. You know the Romans; where do you think we had better fight them?”

“I think we could not do better than await them here,” he said. “We stand on rising ground, and one of the Trinobantes to whom I have just spoken says that there is a swamp away on the left of our front, so that the Roman horsemen cannot advance in that direction. I should attack them in face and on their left flank, closing in thickly so as to prevent their horsemen from breaking out on to the plain at our right and then falling upon us in our rear. Since you are good enough to say that I may choose my post for the Sarci, I will hold them where they stand; then, should the others fail to break the Roman front, we will move down upon them and check their advance while the rest attack their flanks.”

This answer pleased some of the chiefs, who felt jealous of the honour the small tribe had gained on the previous day. They were afraid that Beric would have chosen to head the attack.

“Does that plan please you?” Boadicea asked.

“It is as well as another,” one of the chiefs said. “Let the Sarci look on this time while we destroy the enemy. I should have thought Beric would have chosen for his tribe the post of honour in the attack.”

“The Romans always keep their best troops in reserve,” Beric said quietly; “in a hard fight it is the reserve that decides the fate of the battle.”

“Then let it be so,” Boadicea said. “Is the swamp that you speak of deep?”

“It is not too deep for our men to cross,” one of the chiefs of the Trinobantes said; “but assuredly a horseman could not pass through it.”

“Very well, then, let the Trinobantes attack by falling upon the Romans on our right; the Iceni will attack them in front; and the Sarci will remain where they stand until Beric sees need for them to advance.”

===Chinese===

“往后议事，你须场场都在，”他到了，王后说，“你已经证明：你人虽年少，胸中那点兵学，早已抵过了年岁。昨日庙是你拿下的，今日这一仗，萨尔西有权挑荣誉之位。你自己挑吧。你识得罗马人；你看咱们在哪儿同他们打最相宜？”

“我看，就在这里候他们，再好不过，”他说，“咱们踞着坡地；方才问过的一个特里诺班特人说，咱们正面左前方远处有一片沼泽，罗马的马队从那边过不来。应当正面攻，再攻他们的左翼；两翼朝里挤紧，堵死他们的骑兵从咱们右首突围上原野、再兜咱们后路的路子。您既许我替萨尔西择位，我们便原地不动；倘若旁人攻不破罗马正面，我们便压下去，顶住他们，旁人再攻两侧。”

这话叫几位酋长听得顺耳——头天这个小部落抢走的那份光彩，本叫他们心里发酸；他们正怕贝里克挑走攻坚的头功。

“这个方略，各位中意么？”波阿迪西亚问。

“同旁的打法差不多，”一位酋长说，“这一回，叫萨尔西在边上看咱们收拾敌人。我原当贝里克会给他的部落挑攻坚的荣誉之位呢。”

“罗马人向来把最精的兵留作后备，”贝里克平静地说，“硬仗里，定胜负的往往是后备。”

“那就这样定了，”波阿迪西亚说，“你说的那片沼泽，深么？”

“咱们的人过得去，”特里诺班特的一位酋长说，“骑兵是万万过不去的。”

“很好。那便这样：特里诺班特人攻咱们右首，扑罗马人；爱西尼正面攻；萨尔西原地不动，等贝里克看着需要了再进。”

===Original===

In a few minutes the Roman legion was seen advancing, with a portion of the cavalry in front and the rest in the rear. The queen, whose chariot was placed in front of the line, raised her spear. A tremendous shout was raised by the Britons, and with wild cries the tribes poured down to the attack, while the women, clustered on the slopes they had left, added their shrill cries of encouragement to the din. The Romans, who, believing that the Britons were still engaged in the attack on Camalodunum, had no expectation of meeting them on the march, halted and stood uncertain as the masses of Britons poured down to the attack. Then their trumpets sounded and they again advanced, the cavalry in the rear moving forward to join those in the advance, but before they accomplished this the Britons were upon them. Showers of darts were poured in, and the horsemen, unable to stand the onslaught, rode into the spaces between the companies of the infantry, who, moving outwards and forming a solid column on either flank, protected them from the assaults of their foes.

The Britons, after pouring in showers of javelins, flung themselves, sword in hand, upon the Roman infantry; but these with levelled spears showed so solid a front that they were unable to break through, while from behind the spearmen, the light armed Roman troops poured volleys of missiles among them. Boadicea called Beric to her side.

“It is as you said, Beric; the order in which the Romans fight is wonderful. See how steadily they hold together, it is like a wild boar attacked by dogs; but they will be overwhelmed, see how the darts fly and how bravely the Iceni are fighting.”

===Chinese===

几分钟后，罗马军团出现了：一部分骑兵在前，其余殿后。王后的战车立在阵前；她把矛一举。不列颠人迸出一声惊天动地的呐喊，喊着叫着漫坡而下；妇女们聚在他们让出的坡上，把又尖又利的助威声，添进这片喧嚣。罗马人本当好整以暇，只当不列颠人还在围攻卡马卢杜努姆，全没料到会在行军路上撞见，愣愣地站住了。人潮压下来，他们的号角才响，才重又开进；殿后的骑兵往前赶着同前队会合，还没合上，不列颠人已到。标枪泼雨般浇下；马队吃不住这一顿，纷纷躲进步兵各队的间隙里；步兵朝两侧张开，在两翼各结成一个实心纵队，把马队护在当中。

不列颠人标枪倾泻了一阵，随即拔剑扑上罗马步卒；可那边长矛放平，正面严实得像堵墙，撞不开；矛手身后，罗马轻装兵又把一阵阵投矢泼进人堆。波阿迪西亚把贝里克唤到车边。

“全叫你说着了，贝里克；罗马人打仗这份章法，神了。瞧他们咬得多紧——活像一条被群狗围攻的野猪。可他们撑不住的：瞧标枪飞得多密，爱西尼人打得多狠。”

===Original===

The tribesmen, indeed, were attacking with desperate bravery. Seizing the heads of the spears they attempted to wrest them from their holders, or to thrust them aside and push forward within striking distance. Sometimes they partially succeeded, and though the first might fall others rushing in behind reached the Romans and pressed them backwards, but reserves were brought up and the line restored. Then slowly but steadily the Romans moved forward, and although partial success had at some points attended those who attacked them in flank, the front of the column with serried spears held its way on in spite of the efforts of the Britons to arrest the movement. Presently the supply of javelins of their assailants began to fail, and the assaults upon the head of the column to grow more feeble, while the shouts of the Roman soldiers rose above the cries of their assailants.

“Now it is time for us to move down,” Beric said; “if we can arrest the advance their flanks will be broken in before long. Now, men,” he shouted as he returned to his place at the head of the Sarci, “now is the time to show that you can meet the Romans in their own fashion. Move slowly down to the attack, let no man hasten his pace, but let each keep his place in the ranks. Four companies will attack the Romans in front, the others in column five deep will march down till they face the Roman flank, then they will march at it, spears down, and break it in.”

Beric sounded his bugle, and ten deep the four hundred men moved steadily down to the attack of the Romans. The five front ranks marched with levelled spears, those behind prepared to hurl their darts over their heads. When within fifty yards of the enemy the Sarci raised their battle cry, and the Iceni engaged with the Romans in front, seeing the hedge of spears advancing behind them, hurriedly ran off at both flanks and the Sarci advanced to the attack.

===Chinese===

部众果然在拼命。他们攥住矛头，或夺或掀，想抢进一臂的杀人距离。有时也真得手几分：头一个倒了，后头涌上来的够着了罗马人，把他们压得后退；可后备一添，阵线又合。于是罗马人缓慢而坚定地朝前挪；两翼虽然有几处得手，正面那面矛林却顶着不列颠人的死力，一路拱进。不久，攻方标枪渐渐告罄，对纵队正面的攻击弱了下来；罗马兵的呐喊，倒压过了攻方的呼叫。

“咱们该压下去了，”贝里克说，“顶住他们，两翼要不了一久便破。好，弟兄们！”他回到萨尔西排头，喊道，“显本事的时候到了：拿罗马人自家的路数会他们！缓缓压下去，谁也不许抢步，各守各列。四个百人队正面攻；其余的结五列纵队，压下去对着罗马人的侧翼站定，然后放平矛，齐步撞进去！”

贝里克一声号，四百人十列纵深，稳稳压向罗马人。前五列端矛，后列备好越顶掷枪。离敌五十码，萨尔西喊起战嚎；正面同罗马人绞着的爱西尼人，回头望见身后那面逼来的矛林，忙不迭朝两侧闪开——萨尔西顶了上去。

===Original===

The Romans halted involuntarily, astonished at the spectacle. Never before had they encountered barbarians advancing in formation similar to their own, and the sight of the tall figures advancing almost naked to the assault﻿—for the Britons always threw off their garments before fighting﻿—filled them with something like consternation. At the shouts of their officers, however, they again got into motion and met the Britons firmly. The additional length Beric had given to the spears of the Sarci now proved of vital advantage, and bearing steadily onward they brought the Romans to a standstill, while the javelins from the British rear ranks fell thick and fast among them. Gradually the Romans were pressed backwards, quickly as the gaps were filled up by those behind, until the charging shout of the Sarci on their flank was heard. Beric blew his horn, and his men with an answering shout pressed forward faster, their cries of victory rising as the Romans gave way.

Still the latter fought stubbornly, until triumphant yells and confused shouts told them that the flank had given way under the attack of the Britons. Then Beric’s horn sounded again, the slow advance was converted into a charge, the ranks behind closed up, and before the weight and impetus of the rush the Roman line was broken. Then the impetuosity of the Sarci could no longer be restrained, in vain Beric blew his horn. Flinging down their spears and drawing their swords the Britons flung themselves on the broken mass, the other tribesmen pouring in tumultuously behind them.

For a few minutes a desperate conflict raged, each man fighting for himself, but numbers prevailed, the Roman shouts became feebler, the war cries of the Britons louder and more triumphant. In ten minutes the fight was over, more than two thousand Roman soldiers lay dead, while Cerealis and the cavalry, bursting their way through their assailants, alone escaped, galloping off at full speed towards the refuge of their fortified camp. The exultation of the Britons knew no bounds. They had for the first time since the Romans set foot on their shore beaten them in a fair fight in the open. There was a rush to collect the arms, shields, and helmets of the fallen Romans, and two of the Sarci presently brought the standards of the legion to Beric.

===Chinese===

罗马人不由自主收住了脚，被眼前这一幕惊住：蛮族结着同他们一模一样的阵形压过来，这是破天荒头一遭；再加上那一排排近乎赤裸的高大身影——不列颠人开打前照例脱衣——直扑上来，他们心里那点惊，近乎慌了。可军官们一声呼喝，队伍重又动起来，硬顶着接战。贝里克给萨尔西加长的那截矛，此刻显出了性命攸关的便宜：这一面人墙稳稳前压，把罗马人顶得站不住脚；后排的标枪又稠又急，往人堆里落。罗马人一步步被压退——缺口才开，后排即刻填上——直到侧翼传来萨尔西冲锋的呐喊。贝里克吹响号，部众应声加速；罗马人一让，他们的得胜之声越喊越高。

罗马人兀自死斗，直到侧翼的溃乱和乱喊告诉他们：那一头，在不列颠人的猛攻下垮了。贝里克的号再响；缓进化作冲锋，后排朝前一拢；这一冲的分量和势头底下，罗马阵线破了。至此，萨尔西这股猛劲再也勒不住，贝里克的号吹得再响也是枉然：长矛撂地，剑拔出鞘，不列颠人扑上溃乱的人堆；其余部众，洪水似的灌在后头。

好几分钟，一场殊死的混战翻滚着，人人各自为战；可人多吃住了：罗马人的喊声渐弱，不列颠人的战嚎越喊越响、越喊越狂。十分钟，仗打完了。两千多罗马兵陈尸就地；塞雷利斯带着骑兵，从围攻的人堆里硬撞出一条路，独独逃了出去，朝他们设防大营的庇护全速狂奔。不列颠人的狂喜没了边：自罗马人踏上他们的海岸以来，他们头一回在旷野的堂堂之阵里，把罗马人打败了。众人抢着去收阵亡罗马人的兵刃、盾和盔；两个萨尔西人随即把军团的鹰旗捧到贝里克面前。

===Original===

“Follow me with them,” he said, and, extricating himself from the throng, ascended the slope to where Boadicea, surrounded with women who were dancing and joining in a triumphant chant of victory, was still standing in her chariot.

“Here are the Roman standards, the emblems of victory,” Beric said as he approached the chariot.

Boadicea sprang down, and advancing to him, embraced him warmly. “The victory is yours, Beric,” she said. “Keep these two eagles, and fix them in your hall, so that your children’s children may point to them with pride and say, ‘It was Beric, chief of the Sarci, who first overthrew the Romans in the field.’ But there is no time to be lost;” and she turned to her charioteer, who carried a horn. “Sound the summons for the chiefs to assemble.”

There were several missing, for the Britons had suffered heavily in their first attack.

“Chiefs,” she said, “let us not lose an instant, but press on after the Romans. Let us strike before they recover from their confusion and surprise. Catus Decianus may be in their camp, and while I seek no other spoil, him I must have to wreak my vengeance on. See that a party remain to look to the wounded, and that such as need it are taken to their homes in wagons.” The horns were at once sounded, the tribesmen flocked back to the positions from which they had charged, and resumed their garments. Then the march was continued.

They presented a strange appearance now. Almost every man had taken possession of some portion or other of the Romans’ arms. Some had helmets, others shields, others breastplates, swords, or spears. The helmets, however, were speedily taken off and slung behind them, the heads of the Iceni being vastly larger than those of the Romans, the tallest of whom they overtopped by fully six inches. The arms of the officer who commanded under Cerealis were offered to Beric, but he refused them.

“I fight to drive the Romans from our land,” he said, “and not for spoil. Nothing of theirs will I touch, but will return to the forest when all is over just as I left it.”

===Chinese===

“举着旗，跟我来。”他说着挤出人堆，登上坡去——波阿迪西亚还立在战车上，四围的妇女们且舞且唱，唱着得胜的凯歌。

“罗马人的军旗到了，胜利的标记。”走近战车，贝里克说。

波阿迪西亚跳下车，迎上前来，热烈地拥抱他。“这场胜是你的，贝里克，”她说，“这两面鹰，你留着，钉在你的堂上；好叫你儿子的儿子指着它们骄傲地说：头一个在阵前打翻罗马人的，是萨尔西的贝里克。可是，一刻也不能耽搁了。”她转向带号角的车夫，“吹号，召酋长们集合。”

到了几个，缺了几个——头一轮攻击，不列颠人折损极重。

“酋长们，”她说，“一刻不歇，追着罗马人打。趁他们惊魂未定，一击而下。卡图斯·德奇亚努斯多半就在他们营里；别的掳获我一概不要，这个人，我必要拿到，出我这口气。留下一队人照应伤号；用得着的，拿大车送回家去。”号角齐响，部众涌回各自冲锋前的位置，重新披挂。大军继续开进。

此刻这副行头好不奇异：几乎人人从罗马人的兵装里捡了一样半样——有盔的、有盾的、有胸甲的、有刀有矛的。只是盔很快便摘下来背在了背后：爱西尼人的脑袋比罗马人大得多，个头更比他们高出足有六英寸。塞雷利斯手下那员副将的兵装也献给了贝里克，他谢绝了。

“我为的是赶罗马人出海，不是为掳获，”他说，“他们的一针一线我不要；事了之后回林子，怎么去的，怎么回。”

===Original===

By evening they approached the Roman camp. A portion of the legion had been left there when Cerealis set out, and in the light of the setting sun the helmets and spearheads could be seen above the massive palisades that rose on the top of the outworks. The Britons halted half a mile away, fires were lighted, and the men sat down to feast upon the meat that had been brought in wagons from Camalodunum. Then a council was held. As a rule, the British councils were attended by all able bodied men. The power of the chiefs, except in actual war, was very small, for the Britons, like their Gaulish ancestors, considered every man to be equal, and each had a voice in the management of affairs. Thus every chief had, before taking up arms, held a council of his tribesmen, and it was only after they had given their vote for war that he possessed any distinct power and control.

When the council began, one of the chiefs of the Trinobantes was asked first to give a minute description of the Roman camp. The works were formidable. Surrounding it was a broad and deep fosse, into which a stream was turned. Beyond this there was a double vallum or wall of earth so steep as to be climbed with great difficulty. In the hollow between the two walls sharp stakes were set thickly together. The second wall was higher than the first, and completely commanded it. Along its top ran a solid palisade of massive beams, behind which the earth was banked up to within some three and a half feet from the top, affording a stand for the archers, slingers, and spearmen.

The council was animated, but the great majority of chiefs were in favour of leaving this formidable position untouched, and falling upon places that offered a chance of an easier capture. The British in their tribal wars fought largely for the sake of plunder. In their first burst of fury at Camalodunum they had, contrary to their custom, sought only to destroy; but their thirst for blood was now appeased, they longed for the rich spoils of the Roman cities, both as trophies of victory and to adorn their women. The chiefs represented that already many of their bravest tribesmen had fallen, and it would be folly to risk a heavy loss in the attack upon such a position.

===Chinese===

傍晚，大军逼近罗马营。塞雷利斯出征时留下了一部分军团；落日的光里，壁垒顶上探出成排的盔和矛尖。不列颠人在半英里外停下，生火起灶，坐吃从卡马卢杜努努姆大车运来的肉食。随后，议事。按不列颠的规矩，凡丁壮皆得与议。酋长的权柄，除了临阵，本就有限：不列颠人同他们高卢的祖先一样，认人人平等，人人对公事有发言权。所以每位酋长举兵之前，都先开过族众的会；是他们投了战票，他才有了号令之权。

议事开场，先请特里诺班特的一位酋长把罗马大营细细描述一遍。那工事，委实难啃：外围一道又宽又深的壕，引了活水；壕外两道土垒，垒壁陡得几乎爬不上；两垒之间的沟里，尖桩密密插满。第二道垒高于第一道，居高临下。垒顶是一整道粗木栅；栅后堆土，堆到离顶三英尺半，正好站弓手、投石手和矛手。

议得热烈；可大半酋主都主张放着这块硬骨头不动，转打那些好啃的地方。不列颠人打部落间的仗，多一半为的是掳获。头一回在卡马卢杜努努姆，那阵狂怒里，他们一反常例，只为杀不为抢；如今血渴解了，又馋起罗马城里的肥货——既是战利品，又好妆点自家女人。酋长们又陈说：族中勇士已折了许多，再拿人命去填这样的阵地，是蠢。

===Original===

What matter, they argued, if two or three hundred Romans were left there for the present? They could do no harm, and could be either captured by force or obliged to surrender by hunger after Suetonius and the Roman army had been destroyed. Not a day should be lost, they contended, in marching upon Verulamium, after which London could be sacked, for, although far inferior in size and importance to Camalodunum and Verulamium, it was a rising town, inhabited by large numbers of merchants and traders, who imported goods from Gaul and distributed them over the country.

Beric’s opinion was in favour of an instant assault, and in this he was supported by Aska and two or three of the older chiefs; but the majority were the other way, and the policy of leaving altogether the fortified posts garrisoned by the Romans to be dealt with after the Roman army had been met and destroyed was decided upon. One of the arguments employed was that while the capture of these places would be attended with considerable loss, it would add little to the effect that the news of the destruction of the chief Roman towns would have upon the tribes throughout the whole country, and would take so long that Suetonius might return in time to succour the most important places before the work was done. Aska walked away from the council with Beric.

“They have decided wrongly,” he said.

“I do not think it much matters,” Beric replied. “Everything hangs at present upon the result of our battle with Suetonius. If we win, all the detached forts must surrender; if we lose, what matters it?”

“You think we shall lose, Beric?”

“I do not say that,” Beric said; “but see how it was today. The Iceni made no more impression upon the Roman column than if they had been attacking a wall. They hindered themselves by their very numbers, and by the time we meet the Romans our numbers will be multiplied by five, perhaps by ten. But shall we be any stronger thereby? Will not rather the confusion be greater? Today the Roman horse fled; but had they charged among us, small as was their number, what confusion would they have made in our ranks! A single Briton is a match for a single Roman, and more. Ten Romans fighting in order might repel the assault of a hundred, and as the numbers multiply so does the advantage of discipline increase. I hope for victory, Aska, but I cannot say that I feel confident of it.”

===Chinese===

他们辩道：留它两三百罗马人又何妨？眼下害不了人；等苏埃托尼乌斯和罗马大军一灭，或力取，或饿降，跑不了它。当务之急，一天也不能误，是直取维鲁拉米恩；随后便可扫荡伦蒂尼恩——那城虽比卡马卢杜努努姆和维鲁拉米恩小、要紧程度也低，却是新兴之地，商贾云集，从高卢贩货，转销全岛。

贝里克的主张是即刻强攻，支持他的有阿斯卡和两三位老酋长；可多数在那一边，遂定了这个方略：凡罗马人驻守的设防据点一概绕开，待罗马大军被寻歼之后，再逐个收拾。他们摆的一条道理是：拔这些钉子折损不小，添的彩头却有限——两座罗马大城覆灭的消息，已够震动全岛各部；何况逐点攻取旷日持久，苏埃托尼乌斯多半赶得及回救最要紧的几处。散议后，阿斯卡同贝里克并肩走开。

“他们定错了。”他说。

“我看错也不大，”贝里克答，“眼下万事，都悬在咱们同苏埃托尼乌斯那一战上。赢了，孤悬的堡垒不降也得降；输了，那些又算得了什么？”

“你觉得咱们要输，贝里克？”

“没这么说，”贝里克道，“可你瞧今天这一场。爱西尼人撞那支罗马纵队，同撞一堵墙有什么两样？他们叫自己的人数拖累着；等到会战，咱们的人数还要翻五倍，兴许十倍。可人就真强了么？只怕乱得更凶。今天罗马马队逃了；可它若朝咱们一冲——就那么点人——咱们的队伍要乱成什么样！单个对单个，一个不列颠人抵一个罗马人还有富余；十个罗马人有章法地打，能顶一百个的扑击。人数往上翻，纪律的便宜也跟着翻。我盼着赢，阿斯卡；可稳，我说不出口。”

===Original===

Marching next morning against Verulamium, they arrived there in the afternoon and at once attacked it. The resistance was feeble, and bursting through in several places the Iceni and Trinobantes spread over the town, slaughtering all they found. Not only the Romans, but the Gauls settled in the city, and such Britons as had adopted Roman customs were put to the sword. The city was then sacked and set on fire. It was now decided that instead of turning towards London they should march west in order that they might be joined by other tribes on their way and meet Suetonius returning from Wales.

There was no haste in their movements. They advanced by easy stages, their numbers swelling every day, tribe after tribe joining them, as the news spread of the capture and destruction of the two chief Roman towns, and the defeat and annihilation of one of the legions. So they marched until, a fortnight after the capture of Verulamium, the news arrived that Suetonius, marching with all speed towards the east, had already passed them, gathering up on his way the garrisons of all the fortified posts. Then the great host turned and marched east again. Beric regretted deeply the course that had been taken. Had the garrisons all been attacked and destroyed separately, the army they would have to encounter would have been a little more than half the strength of that which Suetonius would be able to put into the field when he collected all the garrisons.

===Chinese===

次日晨，大军扑向维鲁拉米恩，午后抵达，即刻攻城。抵抗微弱；爱西尼和特里诺班特人从几处突入，漫城屠杀，逢人便杀：不光罗马人，连城里侨居的高卢人、连同那些改了罗马习俗的不列颠人，也一概死于剑下。随后屠城、纵火。于是又议定：不折向伦蒂尼恩，改道西行——一路好收各部来会，好迎自威尔士回师的苏埃托尼乌斯。

行军并不赶急。他们从容一站一站地走，人马天天见涨：两座罗马首城陷落焚毁、一个军团败没的消息传开，部落一个接一个赶来入伙。这样走了半个月，维鲁拉米恩陷落的第十四天，消息到了：苏埃托尼乌斯全速东进，已然赶过了他们，沿路把各设防据点的守军尽数收归帐下。于是这支大军掉头，重又东进。贝里克为此深以为憾：倘若各处守军早被各个击破，如今要会的那支敌军，顶多只有苏埃托尼乌斯收齐守军后的一半多一点。

===Original===

But the Britons troubled themselves in no way. They regarded victory as certain, and expressed exultation that they should crush all the Romans at one blow in the open field, instead of being forced to undertake a number of separate sieges. Still marching easily, they came down upon the valley of the Thames and followed it until they arrived at London. They had expected that Suetonius would give battle before they arrived there. He had indeed passed through the town a few days previously, but had disregarded the prayers of the inhabitants to remain for their protection. He allowed all males who chose to do so to enlist in the ranks and permitted others to accompany the army, but he wished before fighting to be joined by Cerealis and the survivors of his legion, and by the garrisons of other fortified posts. The Britons therefore fell upon London, slaughtered all the inhabitants, and sacked and burned the town. It was calculated that here and in the two Roman cities no less than 80,000 persons had been slain. This accomplished, the great host again set out in search of Suetonius. They were accompanied now by a vast train of wagons and chariots carrying the women and spoil.

Beric was not present at the sack of London. As they approached the town and it became known that Suetonius had marched away, and that there would be no resistance, he struck off north. Since they had left Verulamium the tribesmen had given up marching in military order. They were very proud of the credit they had gained in the battle with the Romans, but said that they did not see any use in marching tediously abreast when there was no enemy near. Beric having no power whatever to compel them, told them that of course they could do as they liked, but that they would speedily forget all they had learned. But the impatience of restraint of any kind, or of doing anything unless perfectly disposed to do it, which was a British characteristic, was too strong, and many were influenced by the scoffs of the newcomers, who, not having seen them in the day of battle, asked them scornfully if the Sarci were slaves that they should obey orders like Roman soldiers.

===Chinese===

可不列颠人半点不愁。他们只当赢是囊中之物，还庆幸正好把罗马人一网打尽于旷野，省得一座一座去围。大军仍旧从从容容，下到泰晤士河谷，沿河而下，到了伦蒂尼恩。他们原等着苏埃托尼乌斯在城外接仗。他确实几天前刚过此城，却不理居民哀求他留下护城的祷告。他允凡愿从军的男丁尽数编进行伍，也准旁人随军；可他要先收齐塞雷利斯和他残部、各处守军，再言开战。于是不列颠人扑向伦蒂尼恩，屠尽居民，洗掠焚城。据估算，此城连同那两座罗马城，被杀者不下八万。事毕，大军重又出动，寻苏埃托尼乌斯决战；随军的，是浩浩荡荡一列大车战车，载着妇人和掳获。

伦蒂尼恩遭屠时，贝里克不在场。大军临城、确知苏埃托尼乌斯已去、城内将无抵抗时，他掉头向北。自离维鲁拉米恩，部众早把行军队形丢了。同罗马人那一仗挣下的名头，他们得意得很，却说：近处没有敌人，何苦没完没了并着膀子闷头走。贝里克无权强逼，只得说随他们的便，只是学的东西很快要忘光。可是那股受不了半分拘束、不合心意便一根指头不动的不列颠脾气，到底占了上风；新来的人没见过他们临阵的威风，冷言冷语地问：萨尔西莫非是奴隶，学罗马大兵听号令？这话又撩倒了不少人。

===Original===

Boduoc, although he had objected to the drill at first, and had scoffed at the idea of men fighting any better because they all kept an even distance from each other, and marched with the same foot forward, had now become an enthusiast in its favour and raged at this falling away. But Beric said, “It is no use being angry, Boduoc. I was surprised that they consented at first, and I am not surprised that they have grown tired of it. It is the fault of our people to be fickle and inconstant, soon wearying of anything they undertake; but I do not think that it matters much now. We alone were able to decide the fight when there were but two thousand Roman spearmen; but when we meet Suetonius, he will have ten thousand soldiers under him, and our multitude is so great that the Sarci would be lost in the crowd. If the Britons cannot beat them without us, we should not suffice to change the fortunes of the day.”

It was partly to escape the sight of the sack of London, partly because he was anxious to know how Berenice and Cneius Nepo were faring that Beric left the army, and drove north in a chariot. After two days’ journey he arrived at the cottage of Boduoc’s mother. The door stood open as was the universal custom in Britain, for nowhere was hospitality so lavishly practised, and it was thought that a closed door might deter a passerby from entering. His footsteps had been heard, for two dogs had growled angrily at his approach. The old woman was sitting at the fire, and at first he saw no one else in the hut.

“Good will to all here!” he said.

“It is the young chief!” the old woman exclaimed, and at once two figures rose from a pile of straw in a dark corner of the room.

“Beric?”

“Yes, it is I,” he said. “How fares it with you, Berenice? You are well, Cneius, I hope? You have run no risks, I trust, since you have been here?”

“We are well, Beric,” the girl said; “but oh the time has seemed so long! It is not yet a month since you sent us here, but it seems a year. She has been very kind to us, and done all that she could, and the girls, her daughters, have gone with me sometimes for rambles in the wood; but they cannot speak our language. Not another person has been here since we came.”

===Chinese===

波杜克当初本来反对操练，讥笑过“人人隔一样远、迈同一只脚，仗就打得好了”的说法；如今却成了这一门的热心人，见这般散伙，气得跳脚。贝里克却说：“气也没用，波杜克。当初他们肯练，我就纳罕；如今练腻了，我不奇怪。咱们的族人，就吃在这善变、无长性、做什么都三分钟热度的毛病上。不过眼下，我看也不大要紧了。那日区区两千罗马矛手，非得靠咱们才定了局；可会苏埃托尼乌斯时，他手下上万兵，咱们的人海那样大，萨尔西丢进去连个影都不剩。不列颠人若离了咱们打不赢，咱们也独力翻不了这一天的盘。”

贝里克离营北去，一半为躲开伦蒂尼恩屠城的场面，一半为探望贝伦妮刻和克内乌斯·内波的境况；他乘一辆战车走了两天，到了波杜克母亲的小屋。门敞着——不列颠处处如此：天下再没有比它更好客的地方，掩门倒像挡了过路人进门的路。人未到，狗先朝他低吼了两声，脚步早惊动了屋里。老妇人坐在火边；起初，屋里再看不见别人。

“愿这一家都好！”他说。

“是小首领！”老妇人喊；屋角暗处那堆草上，随即立起两个人影。

“贝里克？”

“是我，”他说，“你可好，贝伦妮刻？您也好吧，克内乌斯？到这儿以后，没担什么风险吧？”

“我们挺好，贝里克，”姑娘说，“只是这日子，怎么这样长！你送我们来，还不足一个月，倒像过了一年。她待我们好得很，事事尽心；她的女儿们，有时还带我到林子里走走；只是她们不会说咱们的话。打我们来，再没有第二个人上过门。”

===Original===

“What is the news, Beric?” Cneius asked. “No word has reached us. The old woman and her daughters have learned something, for the eldest girl goes away sometimes for hours, and I can see that she tells her mother news when she returns.”

Beric briefly told them what had happened, at which Berenice exclaimed passionately that the Britons were a wicked people.

“Then there will be a great battle when you meet Suetonius, Beric,” Cneius said. “How think you will it go?”

“It is hard to say,” Beric replied; “we are more than one hundred and fifty thousand men against ten thousand, but the ten thousand are soldiers, while the hundred and fifty thousand are a mob. Brave and devoted, and fearless of death I admit, but still a mob. I cannot say how it will go.”

“How long shall we stay here, Beric?” Berenice asked. “When will you take me to my father?”

“If we are beaten, Berenice, you will rejoin him speedily; if we win﻿—”

“He will not be alive,” she broke in.

Beric did not contradict her, but went on, “I will see that you are placed on board a ship and sent to Gaul; it is for this I come here today. Cneius, in two or three days we shall meet Suetonius; if we win, I will return to you myself, or if I am killed, Boduoc or his brother, both of whom I shall charge with the mission, will come in my place and will escort you to the coast and see that you are placed on board ship. If we lose, it is likely that none of us will return. I shall give the old woman instructions that in that case her daughter is to guide you through the forest and take you on until you meet some Roman soldiers, or are within sight of their camp, then you will only have to advance and declare yourself.”

Then he turned and spoke for some time to Boduoc’s mother in her own language, thanking her for the shelter that she had given the fugitives, and giving instructions as to the future. He took a hasty meal, and started at once on his return journey in order to rejoin the Sarci as the army advanced from London. Berenice wept bitterly when he said goodbye, and Cneius himself was much affected.

“I view you almost as a son,” he said; “and it is terrible to know that if you win in the battle, my patron Caius and my countrymen will be destroyed, while if they win, you may fall.”

===Chinese===

“外头什么消息，贝里克？”克内乌斯问，“咱们一个字也听不着。老婆子和她女儿们想必知道些什么：大丫头有时一出去就是几个钟头，回来我瞧得出，她报给她娘听了。”

贝里克把经过简略讲了。贝伦妮刻听着，激愤地喊不列颠人是个凶残的民族。

“那么你们会着苏埃托尼乌斯，必有一场大会战了，贝里克，”克内乌斯说，“你看胜负如何？”

“难说，”贝里克答，“咱们十五万多人，对一万；可那一万是兵，这十五万是乌合之众。勇敢、肯舍命、不怕死，我都认；可乌合之众终归是乌合之众。打成什么样，我说不上。”

“我们还要在这儿待多久，贝里克？”贝伦妮刻问，“几时送我去父亲那里？”

“倘若咱们败了，贝伦妮刻，你很快就能同他团聚；倘若赢了——”

“他就活不成了。”她截住话头。

贝里克不驳她，只接着说：“我会安排你上船去高卢；今天来，正为这一件。克内乌斯，两三天内咱们就要会着苏埃托尼乌斯。赢了，我亲自回来；我若阵亡，波杜克或他兄弟——这差事我会托给他们俩——必有一人来，护送你们到海边，看着你们上船。败了，我们多半一个也回不来。我会交代老婆子：到了那步，叫她女儿领你们穿林子，一直送到撞见罗马兵、或望得见他们营盘为止；你们只管上前报明身份。”

随后他转向波杜克的母亲，用她的话说了好一阵：谢她收留这两个逃难的人，又交代了往后的安排。他匆匆吃了一餐，立刻动身回程，好赶在大军出伦蒂尼恩时回到萨尔西。道别时，贝伦妮刻哭得伤心，克内乌斯自己也大为动情。

“我几乎当你是儿子了，”他说，“可想到这一层，实在难熬：你们赢了，我的恩主盖乌斯和我的同胞就要死绝；他们赢了，倒下的也许就是你。”

===Original===

“It is the fortune of war, Cneius. You know that we Britons look forward to death with joy; that, unlike you, we mourn at a birth and feast at a burial, knowing that after death we go to the Happy Island where there is no more trouble or sorrow, but where all is peace and happiness and content; so do not grieve for me. You will know that if I fall I shall be happy, and shall be free from all the troubles that await this unfortunate land.”

===Chinese===

“这就是战争的运气，克内乌斯。你知道，我们不列颠人是笑着迎死的；同你们不一样，我们生辰举哀，葬礼设宴——知道死后去的是极乐岛，那里再无烦忧悲苦，只有安宁、喜乐和知足。所以，别为我悲伤。你只须知道：我若倒下，我是快活的，这片倒霉土地上等着人的一切烦难，都同我无关了。”

## VII / 七
===Original===

Defeat of the Britons

London was but a heap of ashes when Beric arrived there. It had been a trading place rather than a town. Here were no Roman houses or temples with their massive stone work; it consisted only of a large collection of wooden structures, inhabited by merchants and traders. It lay upon a knoll rising above the low swampy ground covered by the sea at high water, for not till long afterwards did the Romans erect the banks that dammed back the waters and confined them within their regular channel. The opposite shore was similarly covered with water at high tide, and forests extended as far as the eye could reach. London, in fact, occupied what was at high water a peninsula, connected with the mainland only by a shoulder extending back to the hills beyond it, and separated by a deep channel on the west from a similar promontory.

It was a position that, properly fortified by strong walls across the isthmus, could have been held against a host, but the Romans had not as yet taken it in hand; later, however, they recognized the importance of the position, and made it one of the chief seats of their power. Even in the three days that he had been absent Beric found that the host had considerably increased. The tribes of Sussex and Kent, as they heard of the approach of the army, had flocked in to join it, and to share in the plunder of London.

Another day was spent in feasting and rejoicing, and then the army moved northward. It consisted now of well nigh two hundred thousand fighting men, and a vast crowd of women, with a huge train of wagons. Two days later, news reached them of the spot where Suetonius had taken up his position and was awaiting their attack, and the army at once pressed forward in that direction. At nightfall they bivouacked two miles away from it, and Beric, taking Boduoc with him, went forward to examine it. It was at a point where a valley opened into the plain; the sides of the valley were steep and thickly wooded, and it was only in front that an attack could well be delivered.

“What think you of it, Beric?” Boduoc asked.

===Chinese===

不列颠人的溃败

贝里克赶到时，伦蒂尼恩已只剩一堆灰。它本是个商埠，算不得城：没有罗马人的石砌宅邸庙宇，只有一大片木屋，住着商贾贩夫。它踞在一座小丘上，丘下是低洼的泽地，涨潮便叫海水漫了——罗马人筑堤拦水、把水锁进河道，还是许多年以后的事。对岸同样是高潮一片汪洋，森林直铺到目力的尽头。伦蒂尼恩占的这一角，涨潮时实为一座半岛，只靠一条朝后连到丘地的肩颈通着大陆，西面隔一道深水汊，同另一处岬角相望。

这样的地势，只要在颈上筑起坚墙，千军也攻不进；只是罗马人还没下手——后来他们到底识得此地之要，把它经营成了权势的重镇之一。贝里克离开不过三天，便见大军又壮了许多：苏塞克斯和肯特的部落，听得大军将到，纷纷赶来入伙，也赶来分伦蒂尼恩这一杯羹。

又用了一天宴乐庆祝，大军向北进发。此刻，战斗人员已近二十万，外加浩浩荡荡的妇人和大车队。两天后，苏埃托尼乌斯择定阵地候战的消息到了；大军即刻朝那边压去。日暮，他们在两英里外扎营。贝里克带着波杜克上前查看。那是一处山谷出口的所在：谷坡陡峻，林木稠密，只有正面一途，攻得起。

“你看这里怎么样，贝里克？”波杜克问。

===Original===

“Suetonius relies upon our folly,” Beric said; “he is sure that we shall advance upon him as a tumultous mob, and as but a small portion can act at once our numbers will count but little. The position would be a bad one had we any skill or forethought. Were I commander tomorrow I should, before advancing to the attack, send a great number round on either side to make their way through the woods, and so to attack on both flanks, and to pour down the valley in their rear, at the same time that the main body attacked in the front. Then the position would be a fatal one; attacked in front and rear and overwhelmed by darts from the woods on the flanks, their position would be well nigh desperate, and not a man should escape.”

“But we must overwhelm them,” Boduoc said. “What can ten thousand men do against a host like ours?”

===Chinese===

“苏埃托尼乌斯吃准了咱们的糊涂，”贝里克说，“他笃定咱们会拿一团乱哄哄的人海压上去；一时能接战的就这么些人，人数便算不得数。这地势，咱们若有些头脑和远虑，倒是个死地。明天若是我指挥，进攻之前，先遣大批人马从两翼穿林而上，攻他两侧，再顺他背后的山谷灌下去，同正面主力一齐动手。那样，这阵地就成了他的坟：腹背受敌，两翼林中飞矢如雨，他的处境便近乎绝望，一个也走不脱。”

“可咱们总归淹也淹死他们，”波杜克说，“一万人，在咱们这样的人海面前能怎样？”

===Original===

“It may be so, Boduoc. Yet I feel by no means sure of it. At any rate we must prepare for defeat as well as victory. If we are beaten the cause of Britain will be lost. As we advance without order we shall fly without order, and the tribes will disperse to their homes even more quickly than they have gathered. Of one thing you may be sure, the Roman vengeance will be terrible. We have brought disgrace and defeat upon them. We have destroyed their chief cities. We have massacred tens of thousands. No mercy will be shown us, and chiefly will their vengeance fall upon the Iceni. When we return to the camp, go among the men and ask them whether they mean to fight tomorrow as they fought Cerealis, or whether they will fight in the fashion of the rest. I fear that, wild as all are with enthusiasm and the assurance of victory, they will not consent to be kept in reserve, but will be eager to be in the front of the attack. I will go with you, and will do my best to persuade them; but if they insist on fighting in their own way, then we will go to them one by one, and will form if we can a body, if only a hundred strong, to keep, and if needs be, retreat together. In speed we can outrun the heavy armed Roman soldiers with ease, but their cavalry will scour the plain. Keeping together, however, we can repel these with our lances, and make good our escape. We will first make for home, load ourselves with grain, and driving cattle before us, and taking our women and children, make for the swamps that lie to the northwest of our limits. There we can defend ourselves against the Romans for any length of time.”

“You speak as if defeat were certain,” Boduoc said reproachfully.

“Not at all, Boduoc; a prudent man prepares for either fortune, it is only the fool that looks upon one side only. I hope for victory, but I prepare for defeat; those who like to return to their homes and remain there to be slaughtered by the Romans, can do so. I intend to fight to the last.”

===Chinese===

“也许淹得死，波杜克。可我半点不敢笃定。无论如何，败、胜两头都得备下。这一败，不列颠的事业就完了：进军没个章法，溃逃更没个章法；部落散回家去，比聚起来还快。有一层你只管信：罗马的报复，会可怕得很。咱们折了他们的脸，毁了他们的首城，屠了他们好几万人。饶恕，一分也没有；而这报复，头一个落在爱西尼头上。回营之后，你去弟兄们中间问问：明天，他们是照打塞雷利斯那样打，还是同旁人一个打法？我怕——大伙儿全叫热劲和必胜的心冲昏了——不肯留在后备，只抢着打头阵。我同你一起去，尽力劝；他们若执意照自己的路数打，那咱们就一个一个去寻，能凑多少凑多少——哪怕只有一百人——约定同守同退。论腿脚，披重甲的罗马步兵咱们甩得轻松；可他们的骑兵会扫荡原野。只要不散，长矛足以挡住马队，走得了。咱们先回家，装上粮食，赶着牛，带上妻儿，往咱们地界西北的沼泽去。在那里，罗马人熬多久，咱们守多久。”

“你说话，倒像败局已定。”波杜克怨道。

“全不是，波杜克。明白人胜败两头都备，只有蠢材只看一头。我盼赢，可我备败。愿回家去等罗马人屠的，请便；我是要打到最后的。”

===Original===

Upon rejoining the Sarci, Beric called them together, and asked them whether they wished on the following day to rush into the battle, or to remain in solid order in reserve. The reply was, that they wished for their share of glory, and that did they hold aloof until the battle was done and the enemy annihilated they would be pointed out as men who had feared to take their share in the combat. When the meeting had dispersed Beric and Boduoc went among them; they said nothing about the advantage that holding together would be in case of defeat, but pointed out the honour they had gained by deciding the issue of the last battle, and begged them to remain in a solid body, so that possibly they might again decide the battle. As to disgrace, they had already shown how well they could fight, and that none could say that fear had influenced their decision. Altogether two hundred agreed to retain their ranks, and with this Beric was satisfied. He then went off to find his mother, who was as usual with the queen. She would not hear of any possibility of defeat.

“What!” she said. “Are Britons so poor and unmanly a race, that even when twenty to one they cannot conquer a foe? I would not believe it of them.”

“I don’t expect it, mother, but it is best to be prepared for whatever may happen.” He then told her of the arrangements he had made.

“You may be right, Beric, in preparing for the worst, but I will take no part in it. The queen has sworn she will not survive defeat, nor shall I. I will not live to see my country bound in Roman chains. A free woman I have lived, and a free woman I will die, and shall gladly quit this troubled life for the shores of the Happy Island.”

Beric was silent for a minute. “I do not seek to alter your determination, mother, but as for myself, so long as I can lift a sword I shall continue to struggle against the Romans. We shall not meet tomorrow; when the battle once begins all will be confusion, and there would be no finding each other in this vast crowd. If victory is ours, we shall meet afterwards; if defeat, I shall make for Cardun, where, if you change your mind, I shall hope to meet you, and then shall march with those who will for the swamps of Ely, where doubtless large numbers of fugitives will gather, for unless the Romans drive their causeways into its very heart they can scarce penetrate in any other way.”

===Chinese===

回到萨尔西人中，贝里克把众人聚拢，问他们：明天，是随大流抢着冲，还是留在后备、结阵不动？回答是：荣耀他们也要一份；若袖手到仗完敌灭，人家要指他们脊梁，说他们是怕上阵的人。会散后，贝里克和波杜克到人堆里挨个去说：败时抱团的好处，只字不提；只讲上一仗定局挣下的体面，劝他们仍旧抱成一块铁，说不定再定一回局。至于脸面，他们早已亮过本事，谁也不能说他们这个决定是出于怕。总共说下二百人肯守队列；这个数，贝里克知足了。随后他去寻母亲——她照例在王后身边。母亲连“败”字都不要听。

“什么！”她说，“不列颠人是这样穷酸、这样没骨头的种族么？二十个打一个还赢不了？我不信他们这样。”

“我也不指望输，母亲；可万事备个万一最好。”接着他把自己的安排讲了。

“你备最坏的，兴许有理，贝里克；可我不沾这个边。王后起过誓，败则必死；我也一样。活着看祖国锁进罗马的锁链，我不干。我生是自由的女人，死也做自由的女人；高高兴兴撇下这烦扰的尘世，去极乐岛的岸边。”

贝里克沉默了一阵。“我不劝您改主意，母亲；至于我，只要还举得动剑，就同罗马人斗下去。明天咱们见不着了：仗一开场就是一片乱海，这样大的人堆里谁寻得见谁。赢了，事后相见；败了，我奔卡尔敦去——您若回心转意，我盼在那里见您——再同愿走的人投伊利沼泽。逃难的人，必定大批涌到那里；罗马人若不把驰道修进沼泽的心脏，别的路进不来。”

===Original===

So sure were the Britons of victory that no council was held that night. There were the enemy, they had only to rush upon and destroy them. Returning to his men, Beric met Aska.

“I have just been over to your camp to see you, Beric. I have talked with Boduoc, who told me frankly that you did not share the general assurance of an easy victory. Nor do I, after what I saw the other day﻿—how we dashed vainly against the Roman line. He tells me that your men, save a small party, have determined to fight tomorrow in the front line with the rest, and I lament over it.”

“It would make no difference in the result,” Beric said; “in so great a mass as this we should be lost, and even if we could make our way to the front, and fall upon the Romans in a solid body, our numbers are too small to decide the issue; but at least we might, had the day gone against us, have drawn off in good order.”

“I will take my station with you,” Aska said; “I have, as all the Iceni know, been a great fighter in my time; but I will leave it to the younger men tomorrow to win this battle. My authority may aid yours, and methinks that if we win tomorrow, none can say that you were wrong to stand aloof from the first charge, if Aska stood beside you.”

Thanking the chief warmly for the promise, Beric returned to the Sarci. Feasting was kept up all night, and at daybreak the Britons were on foot, and forming in their tribes advanced within half a mile of the Roman position. Then they halted, and Boadicea with her daughters and the chiefs moved along their front exhorting them to great deeds, recalling to them the oppression and tyranny of the Romans, and the indignity that they had inflicted upon her and her daughters; and her addresses were answered by loud shouts from the tribesmen. In the meantime the wagons had moved out and drew up in a vast semicircle behind the troops, so as to enable the women who crowded them to get a view of the victory. So great was the following that the wagons were ranged four or five deep. Beric had drawn up the men who had agreed to fight in order, in a solid mass in front of the tribe. He was nearly on the extreme left of the British position. Aska had taken his place by his side. His mother, as in her chariot she passed along behind Boadicea, waved her hand to him, and then pointed towards the Romans.

===Chinese===

不列颠人胜算满心，那夜连议事会都没开：敌人就在那里，冲上去灭了便是。回营路上，贝里克撞见阿斯卡。

“我刚去你们营里找你，贝里克。同波杜克谈过了；他直言，你不像众人那样笃定轻取。我也不——看过前日咱们怎样白白撞在罗马人的阵线上之后。他告诉我，你的人除一小队外，都决定明天挤到前排同旁人一起冲。我听了痛心。”

“改不了结局，”贝里克说，“这样大的人海里，我们这点人丢进去连个响也没有；就算挤得到前排、结着阵扑上去，人数也定不了局。可倘若天不利我，至少还能成列而退。”

“明天，我把位置摆在你身边，”阿斯卡说，“爱西尼人人知道，我年轻时也是一员骁将；可这一仗，我让给年轻人去赢。我这把老骨头这点威望，兴许帮得上你；我想，只要阿斯卡立在你旁边，日后谁也不能说你避过头一轮冲锋，是错了。”

贝里克热谢了老人的许诺，回到萨尔西营中。宴乐通宵达旦；天一亮，不列颠人起身，按部落列队，推进到离罗马阵地半英里处站定。波阿迪西亚携女儿们和众酋长沿阵前巡行，激励众人建功，数说罗马人的欺压暴虐、加于她母女的奇辱；她的呼喊，回回换得部众雷动的吼声。这期间，大车开了出来，在部队身后排成一个巨大的半圆，好叫车上拥挤的妇女们看这场胜利。随军的人这样多，大车排了四五层深。贝里克把肯守队列的人结成一块实阵，立于全族之前；位置差不多在不列颠全军的最左端。阿斯卡立在他身旁。他母亲随在波阿迪西亚车后经过，朝他挥了挥手，又朝罗马人一指。

===Original===

“Look, Aska,” he said presently; “do you see that deep line of wagons forming all round us? In case of disaster they will block up the retreat. A madness has seized our people. One would think that this was a strife of gladiators at Rome rather than a battle between two nations. There will be no retreat that way for us if disaster comes. We must make off between the horn of the crescent and the Romans. It is there only we can draw off in a body.”

“That is so, Beric,” the chief said; “but see! the queen has reached the end of the lines, and waves her spear as a signal.”

A thundering shout arose, mingled with the shrill cries of encouragement from the women, and then like a torrent the Britons rushed to the attack in confused masses, each tribe striving to be first to attack the Romans. The Sarci from behind the company joined in the rush, and there was confusion in the ranks, many of the men being carried away by the enthusiasm; but the shouts and exhortations of Beric, Aska, and Boduoc steadied them again, and in regular order they marched after the host. In five minutes the uproar of battle swelled high in front. Beric marched up the valley until he arrived at the rear of the great mass of men who were swarming in front of the Roman line, each man striving to get to the front to hurl his dart and join in the struggle. The Romans had drawn up twelve deep across the valley, the heavy armed spearmen in front, the lighter troops behind, the latter replying with their missiles to the storm of darts that the Britons poured upon them. With desperate efforts the assailants strove to break through the hedge of spears; their bravest flung themselves upon the Roman weapons and died there, striving in vain to break the line.

===Chinese===

“看，阿斯卡，”他随即说，“看见四面围上来那一圈深深的大车阵了么？一旦崩了，它们堵死退路。咱们的族人疯了。看这架势，倒像罗马竞技场上的角斗，不像两国的会战。真到大祸临头，那一边退不了。咱们只能从月牙的角和罗马人之间穿出去；只有那里，还退得成一个队伍。”

“正是，贝里克，”老酋长说，“瞧！王后到了阵线尽头，举矛为号了。”

一声雷吼冲天而起，掺着妇女们又尖又利的呐喊；随后，不列颠人如山洪决口，一团一团乱糟糟地扑向敌阵，个个部落抢着头一个接战。萨尔西后排的人也卷进了这股洪流，行列一时乱了——许多人叫热劲冲走了——靠贝里克、阿斯卡和波杜克的呼喝，才又稳住，成列随在主力之后。五分钟后，前方战声大作。贝里克沿谷而上，直到挤在罗马阵前那大团人堆的尾部——人人争抢前排，好掷出标枪、加入厮杀。罗马人十二列纵深，横锁谷口：重装矛手在前，轻装兵在后，拿投矢回敬不列颠人泼来的标枪雨。攻的人拼命想凿穿那道矛林：最勇的，直接扑上罗马人的兵器，死在矛尖上，也没撞开那道线。

===Original===

For hours the fight continued, but the Roman wall remained unbroken and immovable. Fresh combatants had taken the place of those in front until all had exhausted their store of javelins. In vain the chiefs attempted to induce their followers to gather thickly together and to make a rush; the din was too great for their voices to be heard, and the tribesmen were half mad with fury at the failure of their own efforts to break the Roman line. Beric strove many times to bring up his company in a mass through the crowd to the front. The pressure was too great, none would give way where all sought to get near their foes, and rather than break them up he remained in the rear in spite of the eager cries of the men to be allowed to break up and push their way singly forward.

“What can you do alone,” he shouted to them, “more than the others are doing? Together and in order we might succeed, broken we should be useless. If this huge army cannot break their line, what could two hundred men do?” At last, as the storm of javelins began to dwindle, a mighty shout rose from the Romans, and shoulder to shoulder with levelled spears they advanced, while the flanks giving way, the cavalry burst out on both sides and fell upon the Britons. For those in front, pressed by the mass behind them, there was no falling back, they fell as they stood under the Roman spears. Stubbornly for a time the tribesmen fought with sword and target; but as the line pressed forward, and the horsemen cut their way through the struggling mass, a panic began to seize them.

The tribes longest conquered by the Romans first gave way, and the movement rapidly spread. Many for some time desperately opposed the advance of the Romans, whose triumphant shouts rose loudly; but gradually these melted away, and the vast crowd of warriors became a mob of fugitives, the Romans pressing hotly with cries of victory and vengeance upon their rear. Beric’s little band was swept away like foam before the wave of fugitives. For a time it attempted to stem the current; but when Beric saw that this was in vain he shouted to his tribesmen to keep in a close body and to press towards the left, which was comparatively free. Fortunately the Roman horse had plunged in more towards the centre, and the ground was open for their retreat.

===Chinese===

战了几个钟头，罗马那堵墙纹丝不动。前排倒下，后排补上，直到人人掷尽了标枪。酋长们想催部众挤作一团合力猛冲，也是白想：喧嚣震天，谁也听不见谁；部众撞不开罗马阵线，自己先狂怒得半疯。贝里克几番想率他这一队成块穿过人堆顶到前排：挤不动——人人都在抢着上前，谁也不让路。他只得守在后面，任凭弟兄们吵着要散了各自往前钻。

“单打独斗，你们能比旁人多干什么？”他朝他们喊，“抱团、有章法，才有指望；散了，一文不值！这十几万大军若撞不开他们的阵，二百人顶什么用？”末了，标枪的雨渐渐稀了；罗马人迸出一声巨吼，端矛并肩压上；两翼一让，骑兵从两杀出，扑进不列颠人堆里。前排的人被身后的人海顶着，退无可退，就站在原地，一排排倒在罗马矛下。部众持剑举盾死顶了一阵；可正面一压、马队一凿，恐慌便咬住了他们。

降罗马最久的那些部落先垮，垮势转眼燎原。还有人殊死抵了一阵，罗马人得胜的呐喊震天价响；可这点抵抗也渐渐化了，十几万战士的大军，散成了溃逃的人海；罗马人踏着得胜和复仇的呼号，在背后穷追。贝里克那一小队，像浪头前的一撮泡沫，被溃潮卷着走。起初还想顶住这股流；看看无用，贝里克喝令部众抱紧、朝左首挤——那边相对空些。所幸罗马马队扎得偏中，左翼这片地，退路是敞的。

===Original===

Thousands of flying men were making towards the rear, but with a great effort they succeeded in crossing the tide of fugitives, and in passing through outside the semicircle of wagons. Here they halted for a moment while Beric, climbing on the end wagon, surveyed the scene. There was no longer any resistance among the Britons. The great semicircle within the line of wagons was crowded by a throng of fugitives behind whom, at a run now, the Roman legions were advancing, maintaining their order even at that rapid pace. Outside the sweep of wagons women with cries of terror were flying in all directions, and the horses, alarmed by the din, were plunging and struggling, while their drivers vainly endeavoured to extricate them from the close line of vehicles.

“All is lost for the present,” he said to Aska, “let us make for the north; it is useless to delay, men; to try to fight would be to throw away our lives uselessly, we shall do more good by preserving them to fight upon another day. Keep closely together, we shall have the Roman cavalry upon us before long, and only by holding to our ranks can we hope to repel them.”

Many of the women from the nearest wagons rushed in among the men, and, placing them in their centre, the band went off at a steady trot, which they could maintain for hours. The din behind was terrible, the shouts of the Romans mingled with the cries of the Britons and the loud shrieks of women. The plain was already thick with fugitives, consisting either of women from the outside wagons or men who had made their way through the mass of struggling animals. Here and there chariots were dashing across the plain at full gallop. Looking back from a rise of the ground a mile from the battlefield, they saw a few parties of the Roman horse scouring the plain; but the main body were scattered round the confused mass by the wagons.

“There will be but few escape,” Aska said, throwing up his arms in despair; “the wagons have proved a death trap; had it not been for them the army would have scattered all over the country, and though the Roman horse might have cut down many, the greater number would have gained the woods and escaped; but the wagons held them just as a thin line of men will hold the wolves till the hunters arrive and hem them in.”

===Chinese===

几千溃兵往回涌；这一队人死命一挣，横过了溃潮，穿出大车阵的半圆之外。在这里，他们略停了停，贝里克登上末尾一辆大车，四下观望。不列颠人这边，再无一丝抵抗。车阵半圆之内，塞满了溃逃的人海；人海背后，罗马军团跑步压来——便在那种急步下，队形依然不乱。车阵外圈，妇人们哭喊着四散奔逃；牲口受了喧嚣的惊，尥着蹶子乱挣，车夫徒劳地想把它们从密不透风的车阵里摘出来。

“眼下全完了，”他对阿斯卡说，“往北走。耽搁无益，弟兄们；这时候去打，是白扔性命；留着命改日再打，用处大得多。靠紧了！罗马马队转眼就到；惟有守住队列，才挡得住他们。”

近处几辆大车上的妇女，许多抢进了人堆里；众人把她们围在当中，整队小跑而去——这个步子，他们跑得上几个钟头。身后的喧嚣骇人：罗马人的呐喊、不列颠人的哭喊、妇女们的尖叫，搅作一团。原野上已撒满逃命的人：有外圈大车上的妇女，也有从乱畜堆里挤出来的汉子。这儿那儿，战车全速横穿原野。离战场一英里的一道高坡上回望，只见几小股罗马马队正在扫荡；大队还围在车阵边那一团乱糟糟的人海外。

“逃得出去的，没有几个，”阿斯卡绝望地挥着双臂说，“大车成了死阱。没有它们，大军早散进四乡了；罗马马队尽管能砍倒许多，大半的人总归得上林子、逃得性命。可大车把人兜住了，就像一溜儿细弱的人墙兜住狼群，等猎人赶到，一网打尽。”

===Original===

The carts crowded with women, the plunging horses in lines three or four deep had indeed checked the first fugitives; then came the others crowding in upon them, and then before a gap wide enough to let them through could be forced, the Roman horse were round and upon them.

The pause that Beric made had been momentary, and the band kept on at their rapid pace until the woods were reached, and they were safe from pursuit; then, as they halted, they gave way to their sorrow and anguish. Some threw themselves down and lay motionless; others walked up and down with wild gestures; some broke into imprecations against the gods who had deserted them. Some called despairingly the names of wives and daughters who had been among the spectators in that fatal line of wagons. The women sat in a group weeping; none of them belonged to the Iceni, and their kinsfolk and friends had, as they believed, all perished in the fight.

“Think you that the queen has fallen?” Aska asked Beric.

“She may have made her way out,” Beric said; “we saw chariots driving across the plain. She would be carried back by the first fugitives, and it may be that they managed to clear a way through the wagons for her and those with her. If she is alive, doubtless my mother is by her side.”

“If the queen has escaped,” Aska said, “it will be but to die by her own hand instead of by that of the Romans. I am sure that she will not survive this day. There is nothing else left for her, her tribe is destroyed, her country lost, herself insulted and humiliated. Boadicea would never demand her life from the Romans.”

“My mother will certainly die with her,” Beric said, “and I should say that all her party will willingly share her fate. For the chiefs and leaders there will be no mercy, and for a time doubtless all will be slaughtered who fall into the Roman hands; but after a time the sword will be stayed, for the land will be useless to them without men to cultivate it, and when the Roman hands are tired of slaying, policy will prevail. It were best to speak to the men, Aska, for us to be moving on; will you address them?”

===Chinese===

果不其然：塞满妇女的大车、三四层深的惊马，先绊住了头一批溃兵；后面的又压上来；还没等撞开一条过得去的口子，罗马马队已兜过来、扑上了。

贝里克那一次停留不过一瞬；全队疾走不停，直到进了林子、脱了追兵，才停下脚。一停，悲痛和哀伤便决了堤。有的扑倒在地，一动不动；有的来回乱走，手势狂乱；有的破口咒弃绝了他们的神明；有的绝望地呼着妻女的名字——她们正坐在那排夺命的大车里观战。妇女们围坐一处哭泣；她们没有一个是爱西尼人，她们的亲人和朋友，她们信，都已葬在阵中了。

“你看王后殁了没有？”阿斯卡问贝里克。

“她也许杀出来了，”贝里克说，“咱们望见战车横穿原野。头一批溃兵会把她送出来；兴许他们替她和随行的人在车阵上撞开了一条路。她若活着，我母亲必在她身边。”

“王后若逃了，”阿斯卡说，“也不过是改死在自己手里，省得死在罗马人手里。我拿得稳，她熬不过今天。她也再没有别的路：部落亡了，国家亡了，她本人受辱受辱再受辱。波阿迪西亚决不会向罗马人讨命。”

“我母亲必随她同去，”贝里克说，“我看她那一车人，个个甘愿同死。酋长和头目们，没有活路；一时之间，落入罗马人手的，只怕见一个杀一个。可刀终有砍钝的时候：地里没有人耕，这片地于他们就是废的；杀乏了手，自有利害出来收场。咱们该同弟兄们说说，上路了，阿斯卡；你来讲几句？”

===Original===

The old chief moved towards the men, and raising his hand, called them to him. At first but few obeyed the summons, but as he proceeded they roused themselves and gathered round him, for his reputation in the tribe was great, and the assured tone in which he spoke revived their spirits.

“Men of the Sarci,” he said, “this is no time for wailing or lamentation; the gods of Britain have deserted us, but of this terrible day’s defeat none of the disgrace rests upon you. The honour of the victories we won was yours, and though but a small subtribe, the name of the Sarci rang through Britain as that of the bravest in the land. Had all of your tribe obeyed their young chief and fought together today as they have fought before, it may be that the defeat would have been averted; but you stood firmly by him when the others fell away, and you stand here without the loss of a man, safe in the forest and ready to meet the Roman again. You are fortunate in having such a leader. I may tell you that had his counsel prevailed you would not now be mourning a defeat. I, an old chief with long years of experience, believed what he said, young though he is, and saw that to fight in a confused multitude on such a field was to court almost certain defeat.

“Thus then I placed myself by his side, relying upon his skill in arms and your bravery, and throwing my fortune in with yours. I was not mistaken. Had you not firmly kept together and followed his instructions you too would have been enclosed in that vast throng of fugitives hemmed in among the wagons, slaughtered by the Roman footmen in their rear and cut down by their horse if they broke through the line of wagons. You may ask what is there to live for; you may say that the cause of Britain is lost, that your tribe is well nigh destroyed, that many of you have lost your wives and families as well. All this is true, but yet, men, all is not lost. Great as may have been the slaughter, large numbers must have escaped, and many of you have still wives and families at home. Before aught else is thought of these must be taken to a place of safety until the first outburst of Roman vengeance has passed.

===Chinese===

老酋长走向众人，抬手一唤。起初应者寥寥；可他一开口，人们便强打精神围拢来——他在族中威望素著，那副笃定的口气，又把大家的心气提了起来。

“萨尔西的弟兄们，”他说，“此刻不是嚎哭哀恸的时候。不列颠的神明弃了咱们；可这可怕一日的败，耻辱没有一丝落在你们头上。先前那几场胜的荣耀，是你们的；小小一个支派，萨尔西的名字，响遍了全不列颠——全地最勇的名字。今天，你们全族若都听了他们年轻首领的话、像从前那样抱团而战，这一败也许躲得过；可旁人散了，你们仍坚定地守着他；你们站在这里，未折一人，稳稳进了林子，随时再会罗马人。你们有这样一位首领，是你们的福。我告诉你们：听了他的主张，今日便无败可哀。我，一个熬了大半辈子的老酋长，信他的话——他年纪虽轻；我看得分明：在那样的战场上拿一团乱军去打，同自寻败局没有两样。

“所以我把自己的位置摆在他身边：信他的战法，信你们的勇，把我的命数同你们的拴在一处。我没有看错。倘若你们不是牢牢抱团、听他的号令，此刻也陷在那圈车阵围死的人海里了——背后叫罗马步卒屠，撞出车阵，也叫他们的马队剁。你们也许要问：还活着做什么？也许要说：不列颠完了，部落完了，许多人妻儿也没了。全是实情。可是弟兄们，还没有完。杀得再狠，逃出去的总有一大批；你们许多人，家里还有妻儿。别的且不论，先得把她们送到安稳地方，等罗马人这头一波报仇的血气过去。

===Original===

“Had Beric been the sole leader of the Britons from the first there would be no need of fearing their vengeance, for in that case none of their women and children would have been slain, and they would be now in our hands as hostages; but that is past. I say it only to show you how wise and far seeing as well as how brave a leader in battle is this young chief of yours. While all others were dreaming only of an easy victory over the Romans he and I have been preparing for what had best be done in case of defeat. To return to your homes would be but to court death, and if we are to die at the hands of the Romans it is best that we should die fighting them to the end. We have therefore arranged that we will seek a refuge in the Fen country that forms the western boundary of the land of the Iceni; there we can find strongholds into which the Romans can never force their way; thence we can sally out, and in turn take vengeance. There will rally round you hundreds of other brave men till we grow to a force that may again make head against the Romans. There at least we shall live as free men and die as free men.”

A shout of approval broke from the men.

“You need not starve,” Aska went on. “The rivers abound with fish and the swamps with waterfowl. There are islands among the swamps where the land is dry, and we can construct huts. Three days since, when he foresaw that it might be that a refuge would be needed, Beric despatched a messenger home with orders that a herd of three hundred cattle and another of as many swine should be driven to the spot near the swamps for which we propose to make, and they will there be found awaiting you.”

There was again a chorus of approval, and one of the men stepping forward said, “Beric is young, but he is a great chief. We will follow him wherever he will take us, and will swear to be faithful and obedient to him.” Every man raised his right arm towards the sky, and with a loud shout swore to be faithful to Beric.

===Chinese===

“倘若打头起，全军就都由贝里克一个人统领，今天倒不必怕罗马人报复：他们的妇孺一个也死不了，此刻全在咱们手里当人质。可这已过去。我提它，只为叫你们看清：你们这位年轻首领，何等明智、何等远见——不单阵前勇。人人做着轻取罗马人的迷梦时，同我一起，他已在备着败局之下最妥的路。回家去坐等，是伸着脖子等死；既然横竖难免死在罗马人手里，最好就战死在同他们拼到底的路上。所以我们计议定了：去沼泽地——爱西尼疆土西缘的那片水乡——寻一处避难的坚垒；罗马人进不来，咱们却出得去，轮到咱们讨债。成百的勇士会来投你们，直到咱们又长成一支同罗马人再掰一回腕子的力量。至少，咱们在那里活是自由人，死是自由鬼。”

众人迸出一片赞同。

“饿不着，”阿斯卡接着说，“河里鱼多，沼里水禽多；沼中有岛，岛上有干地，咱们搭得起窝棚。三天前，贝里克料到或许要用着一处退路，已打发信使回家传令：赶三百头牛、三百口猪，送到我们要去的那处沼泽边上候着。到了，就有得吃。”

又是一片叫好。一个人上前说：“贝里克年轻，可他是位大首领。他领我们去哪儿，我们去哪儿；我们宣誓效忠于他。”人人举右臂向天，高声立誓，誓死忠于贝里克。

===Original===

“You are right,” Aska said. “It is of no use to obey a chief only when ranged in battle; it is that which has ruined our country. There is nothing slavish in recognizing that one man must rule, and in obeying when obedience is necessary for the sake of all. As one body led by one mind you may do much; as two hundred men swayed by two hundred minds you will do nothing. I shall be with Beric, and my experience may be of aid to him. And if I, a chief of high standing among the Iceni, am well content to recognize in him the leader of our party, you may well do the same. Now, Beric, step forward and say what is next to be done.”

“I thank you,” Beric said when the shout of acclamation that greeted him when he stepped forward had subsided, “for the oath you have sworn to be faithful to me. I pretend not to more wisdom than others, and feel that in the presence of one so full of years and experience as Aska it is a presumption for one of my age to give an opinion; but in one respect I know that I am more fitted than others to lead you. I have studied the records of the Romans, of their wars with the Gauls and other peoples, and I know that their greatest trouble was not in defeating armies in the field but of overcoming the resistance of those who took refuge in fastnesses and harassed them continually by sorties and attacks. I know where the Romans are strong and where they are weak; and it is by the aid of such knowledge that I hope that we may long retain our freedom, and may even in time become so formidable that we may be able to win terms not only for ourselves but for our countrymen.

===Chinese===

“你们说得对，”阿斯卡说，“上阵才听号令、平时各行其是——正是这一条，毁了咱们的国家。认一人主事、认大义所在便服从，这里头没有半分奴气：两百个人一条心，成得事；两百个人两百条心，一事无成。我会跟贝里克在一起，我这点阅历，于他或有小补。连我——爱西尼里有头有脸的一位酋长——都甘居他下、认他做咱们这一伙的领头，你们更无话说。来，贝里克，上前说下一步怎么走。”

“谢谢你们，”贝里克应声上前，等欢呼声落定，才开口，“谢你们立誓效忠。我不敢自称比旁人多智；当着阿斯卡这样年高德劭的人，我这个年纪发表主张，本属僭越。可有一层，我自认比旁人配领你们：我读过罗马人的史册——他们同高卢人、同各族打仗的史册；我知道他们最头疼的，不是阵前破敌，而是对付那些退守天险、时时出袭、昼夜骚扰的人。我知道罗马人强在哪里、弱在哪里。靠这一点学问，我指望咱们长久守住这份自由，甚至有一天，强到替自己、也替同胞挣来体面的条款。”

===Original===

“The first step is to gather at our place of refuge those belonging to us. Therefore do you choose among yourselves twenty swift runners and send them to our villages, bidding the wives and families of all here to leave their homes at once, taking only such gear as they can carry lightly, and to make with all speed for Soto, a village in the district of the Baci, and but a mile or two from the edge of the great swamp country. It is there that the herds have been driven, and there they will find a party ready to escort them. Let all the other women and children be advised to quit their homes also, and to travel north together with the old men and boys. Bid the latter drive the herds before them. It may be months before they can return to their homes. It were best that they should pass altogether beyond the district of our people, for it is upon the Iceni that the vengeance of the Romans will chiefly fall. By presents of cattle they can purchase an asylum among the Brigantes, and had best remain there till they hear that Roman vengeance is satisfied.

“Let them as they journey north advise all the people in our villages to follow their example. Let those who will not do this take shelter in the hearts of the forests. To our own people my orders are distinct: no herd, either of cattle or swine, is to be left behind. Let the Romans find a desert where they can gather no food; let the houses be burnt, together with all crops that have been gathered. Warn all that there must be no delay. Let the boys and old men start within five minutes from the time that you deliver my message, to gather the herds and drive them north. Let the women call their children round them, take up their babes, make a bundle of their garments, and pile upon a wagon cooking pots and such things as are most needed, and then set fire to their houses and stacks and granaries and go. Warn them that even the delay of an hour may be fatal, for that the Roman cavalry will be spreading like a river in flood over the country. Beg them to leave the beaten tracks and journey through the woods, both those who go north and those who will meet us at Soto. Quick! choose the messengers; and such of you as choose had best hand to the one who is bound for his village a ring or a bracelet, or some token that your wives will recognize, so that they may know that the order comes from you.”

===Chinese===

“第一步，把咱们的人聚到退地去。所以，请你们自己挑出二十个快腿，打发回各村落：传这里所有人的妻儿，即刻离家，只带轻便能背的，全速赶往索托——巴奇地界的一个村子，离大沼泽边只一两英里。牛群已赶到那里；到了自有一队人接应。其余的妇孺，也劝她们弃家，同老人孩子一齐北行，叫孩子们赶着牲口在前。家里兴许几个月回不去；最好走出咱们族的地界之外——罗马人的报复，主要落在爱西尼头上。拿牛作礼物，可以在布里甘特人那里买一个容身之处，暂且住下，等听说罗马的怒气消了再说。

“北行一路，劝咱们各村的人都照样办。不肯的，也躲进林子深处去。对自己人，我的令是死的：牛群猪群，一头不留。叫罗马人找到一片荒——连粮都寻不出。屋子烧掉，收进仓的庄稼烧掉。告戒众人：不得延误。自你们传令之时起，五分钟内，孩子和老人就得动身聚牲口、往北赶。妇人们把孩子拢到身边，抱起吃奶的，衣裳打成一卷，锅碗和顶要紧的物件装上车；然后，点火烧了屋子、草垛、粮仓，走。告戒她们：哪怕误上一个钟头，也许就是生死——罗马的马队，会像决堤的河水漫过全境。劝她们离了大路，穿林子走——北去的和到索托会合的，都一样。快！挑信使去。愿意捎信的，最好交给你那村里去的人一枚戒指、一只镯子，或一样你妻子认得的东西，叫她们知道，这令是你发的。”

===Original===

Twenty young men were at once chosen, and Boduoc and two of the older men divided the district of the Sarci among them, allotting to each the hamlets they should visit. As soon as this was decided the rest of the band gave the messengers their tokens to their families, and then the runners started at a trot which they could maintain for many hours. The rest of the band then struck off in the direction in which they were bound. With only an occasional half hour for food and a few hours at night for sleep they pressed northward. Fast as they went the news of the disaster had preceded them, carried by fugitives from the battle.

At each hamlet through which they passed, Aska repeated the advice that had been sent to the Iceni. “Abandon your homes, drive the swine and the cattle before you, take to the forests, journey far north, and seek refuge among the Brigantes. A rallying place for fighting men will be found at Soto, on the edge of the great swamps; let all who can bear arms and love freedom better than servitude or death gather there.”

Upon the march swine were taken and killed for food without hesitation. Many were found straying in the woods untended, the herdsmen having fled in dismay when the news of the defeat reached them. As yet the full extent of the disaster was unknown. Some of the fugitives had reported that scarce a man had escaped; but the very number of fugitives who had preceded the band showed that this was an exaggeration. But it was not until long afterwards that the truth was known. Of the great multitude, estimated at two hundred and thirty thousand, fully a third had fallen, among whom were almost all the women and children whose presence on the battlefield had proved so fatal, and of whom scarce one had been able to escape; for the Romans, infuriated by the massacres at Camalodunum, Verulamium, and London had spared neither age nor sex.

On their arrival at Soto they obtained for the first time news of the queen. A chief of one of the northern subtribes of the Iceni had driven through on his chariot and had told the headman of the hamlet that he had been one of the few who had accompanied Boadicea in her flight.

===Chinese===

二十名青年当即选出；波杜克和两位老人把萨尔西的地界分作二十份，各村各落，一一指派。分派已定，全队的人把带给家小的信物交割给信使；信使们便起步小跑而去——这个步子，他们跑得上许多个钟头。其余的人随后朝自己的方向开拔。一日里不过偶尔歇半个钟头用饭，夜里睡上几个钟头，一路向北。任他们走得多快，惨败的消息总赶在前头——溃兵早把它带出去了。

路过的每个村子，阿斯卡都把发给爱西尼的那套告诫照样宣说：“弃家！赶着猪牛在前！进林子，远走北边，去布里甘特人那里避一避。要打的人，集合点在大沼泽边上的索托；凡拿得动兵器、爱自由胜过爱奴役和死的，都到那里去。”

行军一路，抓着猪就宰了吃，毫不迟疑；林中许多牲口无人看管，散走四野——败讯一到，牧人先吓跑了。灾祸到底多大，此刻还无人知晓。有溃兵报说：逃出的不足一成；可光这支队伍前头一路的溃兵人数，已见那是夸大。实情，是很久以后才知道的：那支号称二十三万的大军，整整三分之一倒在了战场；阵亡的，几乎包括了观战的所有妇孺——正是她们的到场，铸成这场大祸——脱身的不到一个；罗马人被卡马卢杜努姆、维鲁拉米恩和伦蒂尼恩的屠城烧红了眼，男女老幼，一概不饶。

到了索托，他们头一回得到王后的消息。爱西尼北边一支的一位酋长，曾驱车经过这里，告诉村长：随波阿迪西亚出逃的寥寥数人中，有他一个。

===Original===

At the call of the queen, he said, the men threw themselves on the line of wagons in such number and force that a breach was made through them, horses and wagons being overthrown and dragged bodily aside. The chariot with the queen and her two daughters passed through, with four others containing the ladies who accompanied her. Three or four chiefs also passed through in their chariots, and then the breach was filled by the struggling multitude, that poured out like a torrent. The chariots were well away before the Roman horse swept round the wagons, and travelled without pursuit to a forest twenty miles away. As soon as they reached this the queen ordered the charioteers to dig graves, and then calling upon the god of her country to avenge her, she and her daughters and the ladies with them had all drunk poison, brewed from berries that they gathered in the wood. The chiefs would have done so also, but the queen forbade them.

“It is for you,” she said, “to look after your people, and to wage war with Rome to the last. We need but two men to lay us in our graves and spread the sods over us; so that after death at least we shall be safe from further dishonour at the hands of the Romans.”

When they had drunk the poison the men were ordered to leave them for an hour and then to return. When they did so the ladies were all dead, lying in a circle round Boadicea. They were buried in the shallow holes that had been dug, the turf replaced, and dead leaves scattered over the spot, so that no Roman should ever know where the queen of the Iceni and her daughters slept.

Although Beric had given up all hope of again seeing his mother alive, the news of her death was a terrible blow to him, and he wept unrestrainedly until Aska placed a hand on his shoulder. “You must not give way to sorrow, Beric. You have her people to look to. She has gone to the Green Island, where she will dwell in happiness, and where your father has been long expecting her. It is not at a death that we Britons weep, knowing as we do that those that have gone are to be envied. Arouse yourself! there is much to be done. The cattle will probably be here in the morning. We have to question the people here as to the great swamps, and get them to send to the Fen people for guides who will lead us across the marshes to some spot where we can dwell above the level of the highest waters.”

===Chinese===

他说，王后一唤，士兵们以人数和死力扑上车阵，硬生生撞开一个豁口——马翻车倒，整辆整辆掀到一边。载着王后和两个女儿的战车冲了过去，随行贵妇的四辆车跟着；三四位酋长的战车也闯了出来；随后，豁口又叫挣扎的人海重新填满，人流如洪注涌出。战车走远之后，罗马马队才兜过车阵；一路无人追，直行到二十英里外一座林中。一到，王后便命车夫们掘墓；随后，呼她祖国的神为她复仇，她同两个女儿和随行贵妇，一齐饮下了毒——拿林中采的浆果现配的毒。酋长们也要同饮，王后止住了。

“看顾你们的族人、同罗马战到最后的，是你们，”她说，“埋我们、给我们盖上草皮的，两个人尽够了。这样，死后至少再不会有罗马人来辱我们的身子。”

毒酒饮毕，男人们奉命退开一个钟头再回来。回来时，贵妇们已都死了，环卧在波阿迪西亚四周。她们葬进那些浅浅的坑里，草皮覆回，枯叶撒满，好叫任何一个罗马人，永远认不出爱西尼的王后和她的女儿们睡在哪里。

母亲的死讯，贝里克虽早不抱生还之望，仍是一记重锤；他放声痛哭，直到阿斯卡把手搭上他的肩。“不能这样由着悲伤，贝里克。她的人民还指着你看顾。她去了绿岛，在那里安居极乐；你父亲已候她多年了。死别，本来不是我们不列颠人哭的时辰——去了的人，是人羡慕的。振作！多少事等着办。牛群明早多半就到。咱们得问问这里的人大沼泽的底细，请他们传话给沼民，讨几个向导，领咱们过泽，寻一块高得出过大水的地安身。”

===Original===

Beric put aside his private grief for the time, and several of the natives of the village who were accustomed to penetrate the swamps in search of game were collected and questioned as to the country. None, however, could give much useful information. There was a large river that ran through it, with innumerable smaller streams that wandered here and there. None had penetrated far beyond the margin, partly because they were afraid of losing their way, partly because of the enmity of the Fen people.

These were of a different race to themselves, and were a remnant of those whom the Iceni had driven out of their country, and who, instead of going west, had taken refuge in the swamps, whither the invaders had neither the power nor inclination to follow them.

“It is strange,” Aska said, “that just as they fled before us centuries ago, so we have now to fly before the Romans. Still, as they have maintained themselves there, so may we. But it will be necessary that we should try and secure the goodwill of these people and assure them that we do not come among them as foes.”

“There is no quarrel between us now,” the headman of the hamlet said. “There has not been for many generations. They know that we do not seek to molest them, while they are not strong enough to molest us. There is trade between all the hamlets near the swamps and their people; they bring fish and wildfowl, and baskets which they weave out of rushes, and sell to us in exchange for woven cloth, for garments, and sometimes for swine which they keep upon some of their islands.

“It is always they who come to us, we go not to them. They are jealous of our entering their country, and men who go too far in search of game have often been shot at by invisible foes. They take care that their arrows don’t strike, but shoot only as a warning that we must go no farther. Sometimes some foolhardy men have declared that they will go where they like in spite of the Fenmen, and they have gone, but they have never returned. When we have asked the men who come in to trade what has become of them they say ‘they do not know, most likely they had lost their way and died miserably, or fallen into a swamp and perished there;’ and as the men have certainly lost their lives through their own obstinacy nothing can be done.”

“Then some of these men speak our tongue, I suppose?” Aska said.

===Chinese===

贝里克把私痛暂且收起。村里几个惯于进沼打猎的人被聚来问话。可谁也说不出多少有用的：有一条大河穿泽而过，还有数不清的小汊，四处漫流；没有谁深入过泽缘以内——一半怕迷路，一半忌惮泽民。

泽民同他们不是一个种族，是当年被爱西尼赶出家园的那批人的残余：他们没有西去，反身遁入了沼泽；入侵者既无力、也无心追进去。
