But just after that there was the fire, and I had to go away with Scopus to the Alban Hills; and so, as she knew that I could not escort her, I never heard from her. I would that I had been with her that night she was arrested, then she might not have fallen into the hands of the guard. Indeed, had I been here I would have gone gladly, for it seemed to me there must be something strange in the religion that would induce a quiet gentle girl like her to go out at night unknown to her parents. Now I desire even more to learn about it. Her God must surely have given her the strength and courage that she showed when she chose death by lions rather than deny Him.”

“I, too, should like to know something about it,” Aemilia said. “By the way Ennia spoke, when she said you knew that she was a Christian, it seemed to me that, if you did know, which I thought was impossible, she thought you were angry with her for becoming a Christian.”

“I was angry with her not for being a Christian, but for going out without your father’s knowledge, and I told her so frankly. If it had been you I should not have been so much surprised, because you have high spirits and are fearless in disposition; but for her to do so seemed so strange and unnatural, that I deemed this religion of hers must be bad in that it taught a girl to deceive her parents.”

“What did she say, Beric?”

“I could see that she considered it her duty beyond all other duties, and so said no more, knowing nothing of her religion beyond what your father told me.”

“I wish Pollio had been here,” the girl said; “he would have thought as I do about the loss of Ennia. My father has his philosophy, and considers it rather a good thing to be out of the world. My mother was so horrified when she heard that Ennia was a Christian, that I am sure she is relieved at her death. I am not a philosopher, and it was nothing to me whether Ennia took up with this new sect or not. So you see I have no one who can sympathize with me. You can’t think how dreadful the thought is that I shall be alone in future.”

===Chinese===

“我确实知道，埃米利娅。”贝里克便把那晚从聚会回来、她同老奶妈遭歹人拦路、自己撞见相救的事讲了一遍。“她答应我，再去必先知会我，”他说，“那样我便护送她，保她无恙。可紧跟着就是大火，我随斯科帕斯上了阿尔班山；她知我无从护送，便一直没有信来。她被拿那晚，我若在场就好了——兴许她就不落进卫兵手里。真的，我在城里，是甘愿陪她去的：我早想明白，那门教里必有非常之处，否则叫不了一位那样娴静温软的姑娘瞒着父母深夜出门。如今我更想探究它了。她的神，必是给了她那份胆气——宁投狮口，不肯负他。”

“我也想懂一懂它，”埃米利娅说。“听恩尼娅那晚的口气，她说‘你知道我是基督徒’时，倒像——若你真知道，我原以为不可能——她当你恼她入了教。”

“我恼的不是她入教，是她瞒着父亲出门；我也这样明明白白对她说了。换作你，我倒不至这样奇怪：你性子烈，天生胆大；她那样做，太出常情，我只当那教不好，教女儿欺瞒父母。”

“她怎么说，贝里克？”

“看她把那事当成本分之上的本分，我便没再多说——她的教，除你父亲讲过的，我一无所知。”

“波利奥在就好了，”姑娘说；“失了恩尼娅，他必同我一样想。父亲有他的哲学，只当离世倒是一件好事；母亲听说恩尼娅入教时那样惊骇，此刻丢了女儿，心里多半倒松了口气。我不是哲人；恩尼娅附不附那新宗，于我原本无碍。你瞧，同情我的一个人也没有。往后的日子只剩我一个人——这个念头有多可怕，你想不到。”

===Original===

“We grow accustomed to all things,” Beric said. “I have lost all my relations, my country, and everything, and I am here a stranger and little better than a slave, and yet life seems not so unpleasant to me. In time this grief will be healed, and you will be happy again.”

“I am sure I should never have been happy, Beric, if she had died in the arena. I should always have had it before my eyes﻿—I should have dreamt of it. But why do you say that until today you have been almost a slave? Why is it different today?”

Beric told her of his new position.

“If I could take your position, and have your strength but for one night,” Aemilia said passionately, “I would slay the tyrant. He is a monster. It is to him that Ennia’s death is due. He has committed unheard of crimes; and he will kill you, too, Beric. He kills all those whom he once favours.”

“I shall be on my guard, Aemilia; besides, my danger will not be great, for he will have nothing to gain by my death. I shall keep aloof from all intrigues, and he will have no reason to suspect me. The danger, if danger there be, will come from my refusing to carry out any of his cruel orders. I am ready to be a guard, but not an executioner.”

“I know how it will end,” the girl sighed; “but I shall hope always. You conquered the lion, maybe you will conquer Nero.”

“Who is a very much less imposing creature,” Beric smiled. A slave girl at this moment summoned Aemilia into the house. She waited a moment.

“Remember, Beric,” she said, “that if trouble and danger come upon you, any such poor aid as I can give will be yours. I am a Roman girl. I have not the strength to fight as you have, but have the courage to die; and as, at the risk of your life, you saved Ennia for us, so would I risk my life to save yours. Remember that a woman can plot and scheme, and that in dealing with Nero cunning goes for as much as strength. We have many relatives and friends here, too, and Ennia’s death in the arena would have been viewed as a disgrace upon the whole family; so that I can rely upon help from them if need be. Remember that, should the occasion arise, I shall feel your refusal of my help much more bitterly than any misfortune your acceptance of it could bring upon me.” Then turning, the girl went up to the house.

===Chinese===

“样样都会惯的，”贝里克说。“我失了全部亲族、失了家国、失了一切，孤身在此，比奴隶强不了多少；可日子于我并不算难挨。这场哀痛迟早会平，你还会快乐起来。”

“她若死在场里，我一辈子不会快乐了，贝里克。那景象要一辈子在我眼前——要做进我的梦里。可你说，为什么到今天为止，你‘几乎是个奴隶’？今天又怎么不同了？”

贝里克说了他的新职。

“若能同你换个位置，把你这身力气借我一晚，”埃米利娅激愤地说，“我就杀了那暴君。他是怪物。恩尼娅的死，账在他身上。他犯下的罪骇人听闻；他也要杀你的，贝里克。他宠过的人，他一个不留。”

“我会提防，埃米利娅；况且我险不到哪里去：杀我，他毫无好处。我远离一切阴谋，他无从疑我。真有危险，也只在我不肯替他行凶的那一天。做护卫我愿意；刽子手，我不做。”

“我知道会是这个结局，”姑娘叹息道；“可我永远存指望。你降得住狮子，兴许也降得住尼禄。”

“——那可比狮子体面差远了。”贝里克笑道。一个女奴这时来唤埃米利娅进屋。她顿了一顿。

“记住，贝里克，”她说，“你若遭难临险，我这点微力，全归你用。我是罗马姑娘，没有你的力气去搏杀，可有胆量去死。你肯舍命把恩尼娅还给我们，我也肯舍命救你。记住：女人会设计、会运筹；对付尼禄，心计抵得上膂力。我们在此地亲戚朋友也多；恩尼娅若死在场里，等于奇耻加于全族——真到那一步，我支使得动他们。记住：真到那一天，你若不肯受我的助力，比任何祸事加身，更叫我痛。”说完，姑娘转身进屋去了。

===Original===

On arriving at Nero’s palace the next morning, and asking for Phaon, Beric was at once conducted to his chamber.

“That is well,” the freedman said as he entered. “Nero is in council with his architects at present. I will show you to your chamber at once, so that you will be in readiness.”

The apartment to which Phaon led Beric was a charming one. It had no windows in the walls, which were covered with exquisitely painted designs, but light was given by an opening in the ceiling, under which, in the centre of the room, was the shallow basin into which the rain that penetrated through the opening fell. There were several elegantly carved couches round the room. Some bronze statues stood on plinths, and some pots of tall aquatic plants stood in the basin; heavy hangings covered the entrance.

“Here,” Phaon said, drawing one of them aside, “is your cubicule, and here, next to it, is another. It is meant for a friend of the occupant of the room; but I should not advise you to have anyone sleep here. Nero would not sleep well did he know that any stranger was so close to his apartment. This, and the entrance at the other end of the room, lead into passages, while this,” and he drew back another curtain, “is the library.”

This room was about the same size as that allotted to Beric, being some twenty-five feet square. Short as the notice had been, a wooden framework of cedar wood, divided into partitions fifteen inches each way, had been erected round, and in each of these stood a wooden case containing rolls of manuscripts, the name of the work being indicated by a label affixed to the box. Seated at a table in one of the angles was the Greek Chiton, who saluted Beric.

“We shall be good friends, I hope,” Beric said, “for I shall have to rely upon you entirely for the Greek books, and it is you who will be the real librarian.”

Chiton was a man of some thirty years of age, with a pale Greek face; and looking at him earnestly Beric thought that it looked an honest one. He had anticipated that the man Nero had chosen would be placed as a spy over him; but he now concluded this was not so, and that Nero at present trusted him entirely.

===Chinese===

次晨到尼禄宫中，一问法翁，贝里克立刻被引进他的房间。

“来得正好，”获释奴见他进来便说。“尼禄正同建筑师们议事。我先领你去你的住处，好叫你随时齐备。”

法翁领贝里克到的那套屋子极雅致：四壁无窗，绘着精美的图案；光从顶上一口天井下来，井口正下方的屋子中央，是一方浅池，接住漏下来的雨水。四壁一圈雕工精雅的卧榻；几尊铜像立在座上，池中栽着几盆高高的水生植物；入口垂着厚重的帷幔。

“这儿，”法翁撩开其中一幅，“是你的卧室；挨着还有一间，本是备给这屋主人的朋友的——我劝你谁也别留宿。哪个外人离他的寝宫这样近，他就睡不安稳。这一头，连同屋子那一头的门，都通过道；这一间，”他掀开另一幅帘，“是书房。”

这间屋子同贝里克的那间差不多大，约摸二十五尺见方。日子那样急，四周竟已立起一圈香柏木的格架，纵横各十五寸一格，每格里立一只木匣，盛着手稿卷轴，匣上标签写着书名。屋角桌边坐着那希腊人基顿，起身向贝里克致意。

“我们要做好朋友，我希望，”贝里克说，“希腊文书全靠你了；真司书，其实是你。”

基顿三十来岁，一张希腊人的白脸。贝里克细看那张脸，看着诚实。他原估摸尼禄派来的这人必是眼线；此刻倒断定不是——眼下，尼禄是全然信他。

===Original===

“This passage,” Phaon said, “leads direct to Caesar’s private apartment, a few steps only separate them. The passage on this side of your room also leads there, so that either from here or from it you can be summoned at once. Now let us return to your room. It is from there you will generally go to Nero when he summons you. That door at the end of the short passage will not be kept locked, while this one from the library cannot be opened from your side. Three strokes of Nero’s bell will be the signal that he requires you. If after the three have sounded there is another struck smartly, you will snatch up your sword and rush in instantly by night or day.”

“What are my duties to be?” Beric asked when they had returned to his room, “for Chiton can discharge those of librarian infinitely better than I can do.”

“You will sit and read here, or pass the time as you like, until nine o’clock, at which hour Nero goes to the baths. At eleven he goes out to inspect the works or to take part in public ceremonies. At three he sups, and the meal lasts sometimes till seven or eight, sometimes until midnight. Your duties in the library will end when he goes to the baths, and after that you will be free, unless he summons you to attend him abroad, until supper is concluded. At night you will draw back the curtains between the passage and your room and that of your cubicule, so that you may hear his summons, or even his voice if loudly raised. You will lie down with your sword ready at hand. I should say your duties will begin at six in the morning, and it is only between that hour and nine that you will be a prisoner in the library.”

“I shall not find it an imprisonment,” Beric said. “Three hours is little enough to study, with all that wealth of books ready at hand. How about Chiton?”

===Chinese===

“这条过道，”法翁说，“直通恺撒的私室，只隔几步。你房间这一侧的过道也通那里：两头都唤得着你。回你房里去。平日他召你，多半从那里走。短过道尽头那扇门不上锁；书房这扇，从你那侧打不开。尼禄的铃摇三下，是唤你；三下之后若紧跟着清脆的一下——不管是黑夜白昼——抄起剑立刻冲进去。”

“我的职守到底是什么？”回到房里，贝里克问，“论司书，基顿胜我百倍。”

“你就坐这里读书，随意消磨，到九点：尼禄九点去浴场。十一点外出，看工程，或赴典仪。三点开晚饭——有时吃到七八点，有时吃到半夜。书房的职守到他进浴场便完；此后自由，除非他召你随驾出宫，直到晚宴散。夜里，把你房间和卧室通向过道的帘都撩开，好听得见他的召唤——他嗓子大时连话音也听得见。卧下时剑放手边。我看你的职守早晨六点起头；一天里，也只有六点到九点那三个钟头，你算锁在书房。”

“这算不得坐监，”贝里克说。“三个钟头，对着这一架子书，只嫌短。基顿呢？”

===Original===

“He will be on duty whenever the emperor is in the palace; beyond that he is free to go where he likes, so that he be ready at all times to produce any book that Nero may call for. Your meals will be brought up to you by your attendant from the imperial kitchen. There are, you know, baths in the palace for the use of the officials. You will find in this chest a supply of garments of all kinds suitable for different occasions, and here, in the cubicule, ready to hand, are a sword and dagger, with a helmet, breastplate, and shield, to be worn only when Caesar desires you to accompany him armed. If there is anything else that you require, you have but to give the order to your attendant, who will obtain it from the steward of the palace.”

At this moment a slave drew aside the hanging: “Caesar expects you, Beric.”

Nero was standing at the top of the steps into the garden when Beric entered.

“Walk with me, Beric,” he said. “For three hours I have been going into the affairs of the city, and hearing letters read from the governors of the provinces. It will be a change to talk of other things. Tell me about this Britain of yours. I know about your wars, tell me of your life at home.”

Beric at once complied. He saw that it was not information about religion and customs that the emperor desired to hear, but talk about simple matters that would distract his thoughts from the cares of state. He talked, then, of his native village, of his mother with her maids at work around her, of hunting expeditions as a boy with Boduoc, and how both had had a narrow escape of being devoured by wolves. Nero listened in silence as they strolled under the deep shade of the trees. At times he hardly seemed to be listening, but occasionally he asked a question that showed he was following what Beric said.

“Your talk is like a breath from the snow clad mountains,” he said at last, “or a cup of cold water to a thirsty traveller. The word Romans never occurred in it, and yet it was in our tongue. You were brought up among us, as I heard. Tell me of that.”

Briefly Beric described his life at Camalodunum.

“It is a strange mixture,” Nero said; “the cultivated Roman and the wild Briton. I understand now better than I did before, your risking your life for the Christian girl in the arena. You did not love her?”

===Chinese===

“皇帝在宫，他便当值；此外随意来去，只须随叫随到，尼禄要哪卷书，立刻取得出。你的一日三餐，由你的听差从御膳房送来。宫里有官员用的浴场。这只箱里备着各色场合的衣裳；卧室里，顺手处放着剑和短刃，另有盔、胸甲、盾——只等恺撒要你武装随驾时用。再缺什么，吩咐你的听差，他找宫中总管置办。”

正说着，一个奴隶撩帘进来：“恺撒召见，贝里克。”

贝里克进内时，尼禄正立在通园子的台阶顶上。

“陪我走走，贝里克，”他说。“整整三个钟头，全是城务，还有各行省总督的来信，念个没完。换个话题松快松快。讲讲你们那个不列颠。打仗的事我知道；讲讲你们在家的日子。”

贝里克会意：皇帝要听的，不是宗教风俗的学问，是些家常闲话，好把国务从脑子里岔开。他便讲起本乡的村落，讲母亲同环绕着她的女眷们做活，讲小时候同波杜克打猎、双双险些喂了狼。两人在浓荫下缓缓走着，尼禄静听，时而像没听，时而一问，又问在点上。

“你的话像雪山吹来的一口气，”末了他说，“又像给渴行人的一杯凉水。通篇没提‘罗马’两个字，说的却是我们的语言。听说你自小在我们中间长大，讲讲那段。”

贝里克简略说了他在卡马卢杜努姆的生活。

“真是一个奇特的合体，”尼禄说，“教化的罗马人和荒野的不列颠人。你在场上为那基督徒姑娘舍命，如今我比先前更懂了。你并不爱她？”

===Original===

“No, Caesar; we Britons do not think of marriage until we are at least five-and-twenty. We hold that young marriages deteriorate a race. Ennia was little more than a child, according to our notions. She was scarce sixteen, and when I saw her before, for a few days only, she was a year younger; but I think that I should have done the same had I never seen her before. We Britons, like the Gauls, hold women in high respect, and I think that few of my people would hesitate to risk their lives to save a helpless woman.”

“I think we are all for self here,” Nero said; “but we can admire what we should not think of imitating. I like you, Beric, because you are so different from myself and from all around me. We are products of Rome, you of the forest; every man here sighs for power or wealth, or lives for pleasure﻿—I as much as any. We suffer none to stand in our way, but trample down remorselessly all who hinder us. As to risking our lives for the sake of a woman, and that woman almost a stranger, such an idea would never so much as occur to us. This is not the only girl you have saved. I received a letter from Caius Muro some months ago, saying that the news had come to him in Syria that Beric, the young chief of the Iceni, who had so long withstood Suetonius, had been brought a prisoner to Rome, and he besought me, should Beric still be alive, to show favour to him, as he had saved his little daughter, when all others had been slain, at the sack of Camalodunum, and that he had hidden her away until after the defeat of Boadicea, and had then sent her safe and unharmed back to the Romans. The matter escaped my mind till now, though, in truth, I bade my secretary write to him to say that I would befriend you. But it is strange that, having so much life and spirit in that great body of yours, you should yet hold life so cheaply. It was the way with our forefathers, but it is not so now, perhaps because our life is more pleasant than theirs was. Tell me, has Phaon done all to make you comfortable? Is there aught else that you would wish? if so, speak freely.”

===Chinese===

“不爱，恺撒。我们不列颠人，不满二十五岁不议婚娶；我们信早婚坏种。照我们的规矩，恩尼娅不过是个孩子，还不满十六；我先前见她那几天，她还更小一岁。可就算从没见过她，我也一样做。我们不列颠人同高卢人一样，敬重妇人；为一个无助的女子舍命，我族中怕没有几个人会踌躇。”

“我们这里，人人只顾自己，”尼禄说；“可别人不做的，我们照样欣赏。我喜欢你，贝里克，因为你同我不同，同我周围的人都不同。我们是罗马造出来的，你是森林造出来的。这里人人渴的是权势钱财，活着为的是享乐——我也一样，不比别人少。谁挡道就踏倒谁，毫不容情。为一个女人、一个几乎陌路的女人舍命——这样的念头，我们脑子里根本生不出来。你救过的还不止她一个。几个月前我收到盖乌斯·穆罗的信，说叙利亚传来消息：同苏埃托尼乌斯周旋日久的爱西尼年轻首领贝里克，被解到了罗马；他恳我——若贝里克还在世——善待此人：卡马卢杜努姆被屠那天，众人尽死，此人数下他的小女，把她藏到波阿迪西亚败亡之后，毫发无损地送还了罗马人。这事我此刻才想起来；其实当时我就吩咐过秘书回信，说必照拂你。可也真怪：你那副大身量里装着那样多的血性朝气，却偏偏这样不把命当命。我们的祖先正是这样；如今不了——多半因为如今的日子，比他们那阵子舒坦。告诉我，法翁把你的起居都安置妥当了么？还缺什么？尽管说。”

===Original===

“There is one thing I should like, Caesar; I should like to have with me my follower Boduoc, he who was the companion of my boyhood, who fought with me in that hut against the wolves, and was ever by my side in the struggle among our fens. I ask this partly for my own sake, and partly that I may the better do the duty you have set me of acting as your guard. The air of palaces is heavy, and men wake not from sleep as when they lie down in the forest and carry their lives in their hands. I might not hear your call; but with him with me we could keep alternate watch through the night, and the slightest sounds would reach our ears. We could even take post close to the hangings of your chamber, just as the Praetorians guard all the avenues on the other side. I might even go further. There were twenty of my countrymen brought hither with me. All are picked men, not one but in strength and courage is my equal. I would say, place them in offices in the palace; make them door keepers, or place some of them here as labourers under your gardeners, then at all times you would have under your orders a body of twenty devoted men, who would escort you in safety though half Rome were in tumult. They would sleep together among the slaves, where I could instantly summon them. I can answer for their fidelity, they would follow me to the death against any foe I bade them attack.”

“It is an excellent idea, Beric, and shall be carried out. They were all sent to the ludi, if I mistake not, and will have skill as well as strength and courage. I will bid my secretary send an order for their discharge, and that they present themselves to Phaon tomorrow. He will find occupations for them, and I will myself bid him so dispose of them that they shall be well satisfied with their appointments. Truly, as you say, a guard of twenty gladiators of your strength and courage might well defend me against a host. Now it is time that I went to my bath.”

Upon the following day the British captives were all disposed as door keepers in the palace. Beric was present when they presented themselves before Phaon, and had afterwards a private interview with them. They were delighted at finding that they were again under his leadership. All hated as much as ever the occupation of gladiator, although only the man who had defeated Lupus had as yet appeared in the arena.

===Chinese===

“有一件，恺撒。我想把我的部下波杜克带到身边：他是我少年时的伙伴，同我在那间小屋里并肩斗过狼，在大泽的苦战里始终在我身边。求这一半为我自己，一半也为把您派给我的护卫之职做得更好。宫里的空气滞重，人睡下去不像睡在林中、性命悬在手上的人，一唤即醒。您召唤，我怕有听漏的时候；有他在，我们俩可以轮番守夜，最轻的动静也逃不过耳朵。我们甚至可以贴着您寝宫的帷幔设岗，正同禁卫在另一头把守各条通路一样。我还可以更进一步：同我一道解来的族人，还有二十个，个个是挑出来的，论胆勇力气不输我。求您把他们安插在宫里：看门的看门，做粗活的，派几个在园丁手下——那样您手里随时有二十个死士听令；半个罗马作起乱来，他们也能保您安然。他们同奴隶们睡在一处，我一唤即至。他们的忠心我敢以身家担保：我指哪，他们打到死。”

“好主意，贝里克，就照办。他们不都分在各学堂么？力气胆勇之外，还添了武艺。我命秘书发文放人，明天叫他们到法翁那里报到。法翁自有安排；我还要亲自吩咐，把差事派得人人满意。真的，像你说的，二十条你这样胆勇的角斗士做卫队，一伙乱兵也近不得我。该去沐浴了。”

次日，那批不列颠俘虏全数派了宫门看守。他们到法翁跟前报到时贝里克在场，随后又同他们单独谈了一回。众人见重新归他麾下，无不欢喜。角斗这行当，他们恨之如故——虽说登过场的，至今只有胜卢普斯的那一个。

===Original===

“Your duties will be simple and easy,” Beric said. “You will only have to see that no strangers pass you without authority. Each of you will have one or more attendants with you, who will take the names of those who present themselves to those whom they wish to see, and will, on bringing an authorization for them to pass, escort them to the person with whom they have business. Of course the orders will be different at different posts, but these you will receive from the officials of the chamberlain. You will be on duty, as I learn, for six hours each day, and will for the rest of the time be free to go where you please. I suppose by this time all of you have learned sufficient Latin to converse freely. Remember that at nine o’clock in the evening you must all be in the palace. Phaon has arranged for an apartment that you will occupy together. There you will keep your arms, and be always ready, when you receive a message from me, to attend prepared for fighting. There is one thing more: do not mingle with the Romans more than you can help; listen to no tales relating to the emperor, and let no man discuss with you any question of state. Everything that is done in the palace is known, and were you seen talking with any man who afterwards fell under the suspicion of Nero it might cost you your lives. Remember that, whatever may be the duties assigned to you here, we are really assembled as a sort of special bodyguard to him; he is our general. It is no business of ours what his private acts may be. It may be that he is cruel to the powerful and wealthy, but on the other hand he spends his money lavishly on the people of Rome, and is beloved by them. If they as Romans do not resent his acts towards senators and patricians it is no business of ours, strangers and foreigners here, to meddle in the matter. It may be that in time, if we do our duty well, Nero may permit us to return to Britain.”

There was a murmur of approval.

“Nero may cut off the head of every man in Rome for what I care,” Boduoc said. “I owe nothing to the Romans. They are all our enemies, from the highest to the lowest; and if Nero is disposed to be our friend he can do what he likes with them. But I do wish he had given us something more to do than to hang about his palace.”

===Chinese===

“你们的差事简单轻省，”贝里克说。“只须拦住无凭无照的生人。每人手下有一两个听差，替来客通名报见；领了准进的条子，再引客去见要见的人。各岗的细则不同，内廷官员会交代。当值每天六个钟头，其余时间随意来去。我想如今大家的拉丁话都够会话了。记住：每晚九点，全数回宫。法翁拨了一间屋子给你们同住；兵器存在那里，一得我的信，立刻披挂听点。还有一件：同罗马人少掺和；关于皇帝的话，一概不听；国事，谁同你们谈也不接。宫里的事没有透不出去的；哪个同你们攀谈过的人日后犯了尼禄的疑，是要命的。记住：无论在宫里当什么差，我们其实是他的特种近卫，他便是我们的统领。他私底下的行止，与我们无干。他对豪门富室或竟残酷；另一面，他把钱财大把撒给罗马的平民，平民爱他。他们罗马人自己尚且不忌恨他对元老贵族的作为，我们这些外人、番人，更没有插手的份。好好当差，日子久了，尼禄兴许放我们回不列颠。”

众人低声应诺。

“罗马人个个掉脑袋，与我何干，”波杜克说。“我欠罗马人的，一个子儿也没有。上上下下，全是我们的仇人；尼禄既肯做我们的朋友，随他去收拾他们。只可惜他不曾派点正经差事，光叫我们在宫门口闲晃。”

===Original===

Six months passed. Beric stood high in favour with Nero. Two or three times, in order to test the vigilance of his guard, he had sounded his bell. On each occasion an armed figure had instantly entered his room, only to retire when he waved his hand; so that the slave who slept at the other door found Nero alone when he entered, and brought him a cooling drink, or performed some other little office that served as an excuse for his summons, the emperor being well aware how great would be the jealousy of the Praetorian guard, were report to reach them that Caesar had guards save themselves.

Beric often followed in the train of the emperor when he went abroad; and as it speedily became known that he was a favourite of Nero, his friendship was eagerly sought by those who frequented the court, and his good offices solicited by those who had requests to make of the emperor. Large sums of money had been sometimes offered him for his good offices, but he steadily refused to accept any presents whatever, or to mingle in the affairs of others, except in very occasional cases, where it seemed to him that those who sought his aid had been cruelly and unfairly dealt with by officials or venal magistrates.

The sale of his jewels had brought him in a large sum of money, which he had placed in the hands of Norbanus; and the handsome appointments Nero had assigned to his office were very much more than sufficient for his wants. He was always a welcome guest at the house of Norbanus, and now that he was an official high in favour with Nero, even Lesbia received him with marked courtesy. The conversation always turned, when the ladies were present, upon general topics﻿—the gossip of society in Rome, news from the provinces, and other similar matters, for Beric begged them not to speak of the serious events of the day.

“I am one of Nero’s guards, and I do not want to have to hate my work, or to wish well to those from whom I am bound to protect him. To me he is kind and friendly. At times when I am with him in the garden or alone in his room he talks to me as an equal, of books and art, the condition of the people, and other topics.

===Chinese===

六个月过去。贝里克在尼禄跟前圣眷正隆。有两三回，皇帝试他卫队的警醒，摇了铃；每一回，全副武装的身影即刻闪入内室，见他挥手便退；于是睡在另一头门边的奴隶进来时，只见尼禄一人——端上一盏冷饮，或做一点别的小差遣，权当召他的由头。皇帝深知：禁卫军若听说恺撒身边另有护卫，会妒火中烧。

皇帝出行，贝里克常随在行列里；他得宠的风声一传开，宫中那些人争相结纳，有求于皇帝的也来钻他的门路。有人出大钱托他通关节，他一概谢绝，分文不受；别人的事一概不沾——只偶尔有真正叫官吏赃官作践惨了的，才肯出一言。

首饰卖了一大笔钱，存在诺巴努斯处；尼禄给这职位定的用度，又远过他的所需。诺巴努斯家他永远是受欢迎的客；如今他是尼禄跟前的红人，连莱斯比亚待他也礼数俨然。有女眷在座时，话题总是一般的闲谈——罗马社交的新闻、外省的消息，等等；严肃时政，贝里克求他们一概不谈。

“我是尼禄的护卫；我不想恨自己的差事，也不想咒那些我职责所在、必须护他防着的人。他待我，和气而友善。有时在园中，有时在他独处的房里，他拿我当平辈谈天——谈书，谈艺术，谈民情，谈别的。”

===Original===

“It seems to me that there are two Neros: the one a man such as he was when he ascended the throne﻿—gentle; inclined to clemency; desirous of the good of his people, and of popularity; a lover of beautiful things; passionately devoted to art in all its branches; taking far greater pleasure in the society of a few intimate friends than in state pageants and ceremonies. There is another Nero; of him I will not talk. I desire, above all things, not to know of him. I believe that he has been driven to this war upon many of the best and worthiest in Rome, by timidity. He is suspicious. Possibly he has reason for his suspicions; possibly they are unfounded. I do not wish to defend him. All this is a matter for you Romans, and not for me. I wish to know nothing about it; to leave all public matters to those they may concern; to shut my eyes and my ears as much as I can to all that goes on around me. It is for that reason that I go so little to other houses save this. I meet those about the court at the baths, the gymnasium, and in the streets. But at these places men speak not of public affairs, they know not who may be listening; and certainly they would not speak before me. Happily, as I am known to stand high in Caesar’s favour, I am the last person to whom they would say aught in his blame. Thus it is that, though sometimes I come, from chance words let fall, to know that proscriptions, accusations, confiscations, and executions take place; that the Christians are still exposed to horrible persecutions and tortures; that a gloom hangs over society, and that no man of wealth and high station can regard himself as safe, it is only a vague rumour of these things that I hear; and by keeping my ears sealed and refusing to learn particulars, to listen to private griefs and individual suffering, I am still able to feel that I can do my duty to Caesar.”

===Chinese===

“依我看，尼禄有两个：一个是登基之初的那个人——温良，近于宽厚，求百姓之福，求人望，爱一切美的物事，痴心于各门艺术；同三两知己清谈之乐，远过朝廷的仪典排场。还有一个尼禄：那个我不谈。我头一件求的，就是不知道他。我相信，他向罗马多少最优秀的人开的这场战，是被疑心逼出来的。他多疑。他的疑，或有影子，或竟没有；我不想替他辩。那是你们罗马人的事，不是我的。我什么也不想知道；公事，尽归该管的人管；身边的种种，我尽力闭眼塞耳。所以我除了这一家，谁家都少去。宫里那一班人，我在浴场、操场、街面上见面；那些地方没人谈国事——谁知道墙外哪只耳朵在听；当着我，更没有人谈。也好：人尽知我圣眷正隆，要说恺撒坏话，找谁也轮不到找我。这样，虽然偶尔从失言的零句里，我知道有抄杀、告讦、籍没、处决在行；知道基督徒仍在受骇人的酷刑迫害；知道阴云罩着整个上流社会，凡有财有位者人人自危——我听见的，终究只是含糊的风闻。两耳一封，不问细节，不听私家的悲苦，我便还能觉得：我对恺撒，尽得了职分。”

===Original===

Norbanus and Lesbia alike agreed with Beric’s reasoning; the former, indeed, himself took but comparatively little interest in what passed around him. The latter was, on the other hand, absorbed in the politics of the hour. She was connected with many noble families, and knew that a member of these might fall at any moment under Nero’s displeasure. To have a friend, then, high in the favour of Nero was a matter of great importance; and she therefore impressed upon all her intimates that when they found Beric at her house they should scrupulously avoid all discussion of public affairs.

===Chinese===

诺巴努斯同莱斯比亚都服贝里克这套道理。前者本来就不大留心身外之事；后者正相反，时局沉在心尖上。她连着好几个世家，深知哪一族的哪一个人，随时可能跌进尼禄的疑忌。有一个位居尼禄宠中的朋友，便是天大的事；于是她叮咛遍至亲至交：凡在自家宅里遇上贝里克，务必一字不谈公务。

## XVII / 十七
===Original===

Betrothal

Nero had, within a short time of Beric’s establishment in the palace, spoken to him of his apprehension of the increasing power of the party who, having reverted to the opinions of the Stoic philosophers, were ever denouncing the luxury and extravagance of modern ways, and endeavouring, both by example and precept, to reintroduce the simplicity and severity of former times.

“All this,” Nero said angrily, “is of course but a cloak under which to attack me. Piso and Plautus, Seneca and Lucan, do but assume this severity of manners. They have plotted and intrigued against me. I shall never be safe while they live.”

“Caesar,” Beric said gravely, “I am but a soldier, but born a free Briton and a chief. I cannot sell my service, but must give it loyally and heartily. You honour me with your favour and confidence; I believe that I am worthy of it. I do not serve you for money. Already I have begged you not to heap presents upon me. Wealth would be useless to me did I desire it. Not only have you offered to bestow estates upon me, but I have learned already that there are many others who, seeing that I am favoured by you, would purchase my friendship or my advocacy by large sums. I should despise myself if I cared for money. You would, I know honour me not only with your trust that I can be relied upon to do my duty as your guard, but by treating me as one in your confidence in other matters. At the risk, then, of exciting your displeasure and forfeiting your favour, I must again pray you not to burden me with state matters. Of these I know nothing, and wish to know nothing. Save that of Seneca, I scarce know the names of the others of whom you have spoken. I am wholly ignorant of the intrigues of court life, and I seek to know nothing of them, and am therefore in no position to give any opinion on these matters; and did I speak from only partial knowledge I should do these men great wrong.

“In the next place, Caesar, I am not one who has a double face, and if you ask my opinion of a matter in which I thought that others had ill advised you, I should frankly say that I thought you were wrong; and the truth is never palatable to the great. I try, therefore, to shut my ears to everything that is going on around me, for did I take note of rumours my loyalty to you might be shaken.”

===Chinese===

订婚

贝里克进宫不久，尼禄便向他吐露过心病：如今有一党人重新尊奉斯多噶哲人的道理，日日指斥时下的奢靡，想以身教言教重振古昔的简朴克己，其势日张。

“这一切，”尼禄怒道，“当然不过是攻讦我的幌子。皮索、普劳图斯、塞内加、卢卡努斯，那一脸的道学全是装的。他们阴谋算计我。这班人不死，我一日不得安生。”

“恺撒，”贝里克正色说，“我一介武夫，却是生而自由的不列颠人、是个首领。我的效命卖不得钱，只能诚心诚意地给。您以恩宠信义待我，我自信当得起。我侍奉您，不为钱财；早求过您，赏赐不要再堆给我。财富于我无用，我纵想要也无用。您不但许过我田产；我还知道，许多人见我得您青眼，要出重金买我的交情、买我的进言。我在乎钱，就自己看轻自己。我知道，您敬我的，不止是我护卫尽职这一层信义，还有把我当心腹之意。所以，哪怕触怒失宠，我仍要求您：国务，莫再相累。那些我一无所知，也不想知。方才那几位，除了塞内加，我连名字都叫不全。宫廷的阴谋我全然不懂，也不想懂，因此没有置喙的资格；凭一知半解说长道短，反倒冤屈了人家。

“再者，恺撒，我不是两面人。您若问到我以为旁人进言进错了的事，我会直说您错了；而真话，从来不入贵人之耳。所以我尽力塞住耳朵不听四下的事——风声听多了，我对您的忠心怕要动摇。”

===Original===

“Perhaps you are right,” Nero said, after a long pause. “But tell me, once and for all, what you do think on general matters. It is good to have the opinion of one whom I know to be honest.”

“On one subject only are my convictions strong, Caesar. I think that the terrible persecution of the Christians is in itself horrible, and contrary to all the traditions of Rome. These are harmless people. They make no disturbances; they do injury to no one; they are guilty of no act that would justify in any way the tortures inflicted upon them. I am not a Christian, I know nothing of their doctrines; but I am unable to understand how one naturally clement and kind hearted as you are can give way to the clamour of the populace against these people. As to those of whom you speak, and others, I have no opinions; but were I Caesar, strong in the support of the Praetorian guards, and in the affection of the people at large, I would simply despise plotters. The people may vaguely admire the doctrines of the Stoics, but they themselves love pleasure and amusements and spectacles, and live upon your bounty and generosity. There can then be nothing to fear from open force. Should there be conspirators who would attempt to compass their ends by assassination, you have your guards to protect you. You have myself and my little band of countrymen ready to watch over you unceasingly.”

“No care and caution will avail against the knife of the assassin,” Nero said gloomily. “It is only by striking down conspirators and assassins that one can guard one’s self against their weapons. Julius Caesar was killed when surrounded by men whom he deemed his friends.”

===Chinese===

“也许你说得对，”半晌，尼禄说。“可告诉我——就这一回，总论大事，你到底是什么见识。听听一个我明知诚实的人的见解，有好处。”

“只有一件事，恺撒，我的主意拿得死。我以为，那样酷烈地迫害基督徒，本身就是骇人的，且违了罗马全部的传统。这些人无害：不滋事，不伤人，没有一行当得起加给他们的那些酷刑。我不是基督徒，他们的教义我一无所知；可我不明白，您这样天生宽仁心软的人，怎会顺从民众的叫嚣来对付这些人。至于方才说的那几位等等，我没有见解；可我若做了恺撒——有禁卫军撑腰，有万民爱戴——阴谋家，一笑置之罢了。斯多噶的道理，民众不过远远慕其名；他们爱的是行乐、是热闹、是场面，靠您的慷慨活着。明枪，无可惧。真有图谋行刺的，自有护卫；还有我，同我那一小队族人，昼夜不眨眼地守着您。”

“防得再严，防不住刺客的刀，”尼禄阴郁地说。“只有先发制人、砍倒阴谋家，才防得住他们的凶器。尤利乌斯·恺撒，就是死在他当作朋友的那些人围拢之中。”

===Original===

Beric could not deny the truth of Nero’s words. “That is true, Caesar, and therefore I do not presume to criticise or even to have an opinion upon acts of state policy. These are matters utterly beyond me. I know nothing of the history of the families of Rome. I know not who may, with or without reason, deem that they have cause of complaint against you, or who may be hostile to you either from private grievances or personal ambitions, and knowing nothing I wish to know nothing. I desire, as I said when you first spoke to me, to be regarded as a watchdog, to be attached to you by personal kindness, and to guard you night and day against conspirators and assassins. I beseech you not to expect more from me, or to deem it possible that a Briton can be qualified to give any opinion whatever as to a matter so alien to him as the intrigues and conspiracies of an imperial city. Did I agree with you, you would soon doubt my honesty; did I differ from you, I should incur your displeasure.”

Nero looked up at the frank countenance of the young Briton.

“Enough,” he said smiling, “you shall be my watchdog and nothing more.”

As time went on Nero’s confidence in his British guard steadily increased. He had his spies, and knew how entirely Beric kept himself aloof from intimate acquaintanceship with any save the family of Norbanus, and learned, too, that he had refused many large bribes from suitors. For a time, although he knew it not, Beric was constantly watched. His footsteps were followed when he went abroad, his conversations with others in the baths, which formed the great centres of meeting, and stood to the Romans in the place of modern clubs, were listened to and noted. It was observed that he seldom went to convivial gatherings, and that at any place when the conversation turned on public affairs he speedily withdrew; that he avoided all display of wealth, dressed as quietly as it was possible for one in the court circle to do, and bore himself as simply as when he had been training in the ludus of Scopus. There he still went very frequently, practising constantly in arms with his former companions, preferring this to the more formal exercises of the gymnasium. Thus, after a time, Nero became confirmed in his opinion of Beric’s straightforward honesty, and felt that there was no fear of his being tampered with by his enemies.

===Chinese===

尼禄这话，贝里克驳不得。“是实情，恺撒；所以我不敢妄议国政，连见解也不敢有。那些事全然在我之外。罗马各家的历史恩怨我一概不知：谁有真委屈、谁无理取闹，谁怀私怨、谁存野心，我一概不知；既不知，便不想知。初蒙垂询时我便求过：把我当一条看家犬，以私恩系于您身，昼夜替您防阴谋、防刺客。求您莫再指望别的，也莫指望一个不列颠人配对帝都的阴谋诡计置一词——那于他隔如天壤。我顺着您说，您早晚疑我不诚；我逆着您说，又开罪于您。”

尼禄抬眼看着这年轻不列颠人坦诚的脸。

“够了，”他微笑道，“你就做我的看家犬，别的什么都不做。”

日子越久，尼禄对这不列颠护卫的信义越深。他有他的暗探，深知贝里克除了诺巴努斯一家，不同任何人深交；又知他谢绝过请托人的多笔重贿。有一阵子——贝里克自己并不知道——他的一举一动都有人盯：出门有人跟；在浴场——罗马人聚会的中心，抵得后世的俱乐部——同人交谈，有人听、有人记。众人报上去的是：他极少赴宴；席间一谈到公务他便抽身；不摆阔，衣着素净到宫廷圈子里能容的极限；起居做派，同在斯科帕斯学堂受训时一样简朴。学堂他仍常去，同旧日同伴天天过手，宁舍公共操练场那份正经操练。这样过了些时日，尼禄对贝里克的坦直诚实再无怀疑，深信敌人的手伸不进他。

===Original===

One result of this increased confidence was that Beric’s hours of leisure became much restricted, for Nero came to require his attendance whenever he appeared in public. With Beric and Boduoc among the group of courtiers that followed him, the emperor felt assured there was no occasion to fear the knife of the assassin; and it was only when he was at the baths, where only his most chosen friends were admitted, or during the long carousals that followed the suppers, that Beric was at liberty, and in the latter case Boduoc was always near at hand in case of need.

Nero’s precautions were redoubled after the detection of the conspiracy of Piso. That this plot was a real one, and not a mere invention of Nero to justify his designs upon those he hated and feared, is undoubted. The hour for the attempt at assassination had been fixed, the chief actor was prepared and the knife sharpened. But the executions that followed embraced many who had no knowledge whatever of the plot. Seneca was among the victims against whom there was no shadow of proof.

After the discovery of this plot Beric found his position more and more irksome in spite of the favour Nero showed him. Do what he would he could not close his ears to what was public talk in Rome. The fabulous extravagances of Nero, the public and unbounded profligacy of himself and his court, the open defiance of decency, the stupendous waste of public money on the new and most sumptuous palace into which he had now removed, were matters that scandalized even the population of Rome. Senators, patricians, grave councillors, noble matrons were alike willingly or unwillingly obliged to join in the saturnalia that prevailed. The provinces were ruined to minister to the luxury of Rome. The wealth of the noblest families was sequestrated to the state. All law, order, and decency were set at defiance.

===Chinese===

信任加深的一个结果，是贝里克的闲暇大减：尼禄凡公开露面，都要他在侧。随行的廷臣堆里有贝里克同波杜克两条大汉，皇帝便觉刺客的刀近不了身；只有入浴时——只准入至交——和晚宴后的长夜纵饮，贝里克才得脱身；纵饮的场合，波杜克也总在近处备着。

皮索的阴谋败露后，尼禄的戒备加倍。那桩阴谋是真有其事，不是尼禄为除眼中钉编造的：行刺的时辰已定，下手的人已备，刀已磨快。可随后的大狱，牵连了许多同阴谋毫不相干的人；塞内加也在被杀之列，对他其实连一点影子的罪证也无。

这桩阴谋之后，纵然恩宠日隆，贝里克的处境却越发煎熬。罗马街谈巷议的事，他再掩耳也掩不住。尼禄穷奢极欲到神话般的地步；他同他宫廷的荒淫，公开而不加节制；廉耻公然被践踏；公帑像水一样泼进那座新落成的穷奢极侈的宫殿——他如今已搬了进去——连罗马百姓也为之咋舌。元老、贵族、肃穆的顾问、贵妇，或甘或不愿，都得加入这场狂欢。外省刮穷了，供罗马的挥霍；名门的家产抄没入官；法律、秩序、廉耻，一概被踩在脚下。

===Original===

To the Britons, simple in their tastes and habits, this profusion of luxury, this universal profligacy seemed absolutely monstrous. When they met together and talked of their former life in their rude huts, it seemed that the vengeance of the gods must surely fall upon a people who seemed to have lost all sense of virtue, all respect for things human and divine. To Beric the only bearable portions of his existence were the mornings he spent in reading, and in the study of Greek with Chiton, and in the house of Norbanus. Of Lesbia he saw little. She spent her life in a whirl of dissipation and gaiety, accompanying members of her family to all the fêtes in defiance of the wishes of Norbanus, whose authority in this matter she absolutely set at naught.

“The emperor’s invitations override the authority of one who makes himself absurd by his presumption of philosophy. I live as do other Roman ladies of good family. Divorce me if you like; I have the fortune I brought you, and should prefer vastly to go my own way.”

This step Norbanus would have taken but for the sake of Aemilia. By his orders the latter never went abroad with her mother or attended any of the public entertainments, but lived in the quiet society of the personal friends of Norbanus. Lesbia had yielded the point, for she did not care to be accompanied by a daughter of marriageable age, as by dint of cosmetics and paint she posed as still a young woman. Aemilia had long since recovered her spirits, and was again the merry girl Beric had known at Massilia.

One day when Beric called he saw that Norbanus, who was seldom put out by any passing circumstance, was disturbed in mind.

“I am troubled indeed,” he said, in answer to Beric’s inquiry. “Lesbia has been proposing to me the marriage of Rufinus Sulla, a connection of hers, and, as you know, one of Nero’s intimates, with Aemilia.”

Beric uttered an exclamation of anger.

“He is one of the worst of profligates,” he exclaimed. “I would slay him with my own hand rather than that Aemilia should be sacrificed to him.”

===Chinese===

在不列颠人看来——他们口味习性两样简朴——这样的穷奢极欲、这样普遍的荒淫，简直是怪物。同伴相聚，谈起从前粗屋陋舍的日子，都觉得：一个人人丧尽德性、不知敬神人为何物的族类，神的刑罚必定要落下来。在贝里克，日子里熬得下去的，只有读书的早晨、同基顿习希腊文的早晨，和在诺巴努斯家的时光。莱斯比亚他见得少了：她泡在纸醉金迷的旋涡里，拖着娘家的人赴遍大小宴会，全不把诺巴努斯的意愿放在眼里——他在这一头的主权，被她踩得一钱不值。

“皇帝的召请，压倒一个拿哲学装门面、自命不凡得可笑的丈夫的权威。我照罗马世家出身的太太们那样过日子。要休我便休；我的陪嫁我自己带走，正巴不得各行其路。”

若不为埃米利娅，诺巴努斯早走了这一步。他下令：埃米利娅不得随母出门，不得赴一切公开宴乐，只在他至交的安静交际里过活。莱斯比亚竟也让了这一条——她本就不愿带着一个待嫁之年的女儿在身边：靠脂粉铅华，她还充着年轻夫人呢。埃米利娅早已恢复生气，又是马西利亚时贝里克识得的那位快活姑娘了。

一天贝里克登门，见素来泰山崩于前不改色的诺巴努斯，满面愁容。

“实在心烦，”贝里克动问，他答道。“莱斯比亚向我提亲：把埃米利娅许给鲁菲努斯·苏拉——她的亲戚，你知道，也是尼禄的狎客之一。”

贝里克怒喝一声。

“那是头一等的无耻之徒！”他喊。“叫我亲手杀了他，也强似把埃米利娅牺牲给他！”

===Original===

“And I would slay her first,” Norbanus said calmly; “but, as Lesbia threatened when I indignantly refused the proposal, Rufinus has but to ask Nero’s approval, and before his orders my authority as a father goes for nothing. I see but one way. It has seemed to me for a long time, Beric, that you yourself felt more warmly towards Aemilia than a mere friend. Putting aside our obligations to you for having risked your life in defence of Ennia, there is no one to whom I would more willingly give her. Have I been mistaken in your thoughts of her?”

“By no means,” Beric said. “I love your daughter Aemilia, but I have never spoken of it to you for two reasons. In the first place I shall not be for some years of the age at which we Britons marry, and in the second I am but a captive. At present I stand high in the favour of Nero, but that favour may fail me at any day, and my life at the palace is becoming unbearable; but besides, it is impossible that this orgy of crime and debauchery can continue. The vengeance of heaven cannot be much longer delayed. The legions in the provinces are utterly discontented and well nigh mutinous, and even if Rome continues to support Nero the time cannot be far off when the legions proclaim either Galba, or Vespasian, or some other general, as emperor, and then the downfall of Nero must come. How then could I ask you for the hand of Aemilia, a maiden of noble family, when the future is all so dark and troubled and my own lot so uncertain?

===Chinese===

“那我就先杀了埃米利娅，”诺巴努斯平静地说。“可莱斯比亚放话了——我愤然回绝时——鲁菲努斯只消求尼禄一纸允准，他的谕令面前，我这点父权一钱不值。我只有一条路。贝里克，许久以来我看在眼里：你对埃米利娅，情分早不止于朋友。且不说你为救恩尼娅舍命的恩，天底下我也最愿把她许给你。你的心思，我可看错了？”

“没看错，”贝里克说。“我爱你的女儿埃米利娅；一直没提，有两层缘故。一，照我们不列颠人的规矩，我离娶亲的年纪还差着几年；二，我终究是个俘虏。如今我在尼禄跟前得宠，可这份宠随时会塌，宫里的日子已熬不住了；再说，这样一场罪恶荒淫的狂欢，断没有长不了的理。天罚拖不了多久了。各行省的军团怨气冲天，几乎要哗变；就算罗马城还撑尼禄，军团宣布加尔巴、韦帕芗或哪位将军为帝的日子也不远了——那时尼禄必倒。前路这样黑、这样乱，我自己的命数这样悬，怎么开得了口，向你要一位名门的闺女？”

===Original===

“I cannot raise my sword against Caesar, for, however foul his crimes, he has treated me well. Had it not been for that I would have made for Praeneste, when the gladiators rose there the other day, and for the same reason I can do nothing to prepare the way for a rising here. I know the ludus of Scopus would join to a man. There is great discontent among the other schools, for the people have become so accustomed to bloodshed that they seem steeled to all pity, and invariably give the signal for the despatch of the conquered. As to your offer, Norbanus, I thank you with all my heart; but were it not for this danger that threatens from Rufinus, I would say that at the present time I dare not link her lot to mine. The danger is too great, the future too dark. It seems to me that the city and all in it are seized with madness, and above all, at the present time, I would not for worlds take her to the palace of Nero. But if Aemilia will consent to a betrothal to me, putting off the period of marriage until the times are changed, I will, with delight, accept the offer of her hand, if she too is willing, for in Briton, as in Gaul, our maidens have a voice in their own disposal.”

Norbanus smiled. “Methinks, Beric, you need not fear on that score. Since the day when you fought the lion in the arena you have been her hero and the lord of her heart. Even I, although but short sighted as to matters unconnected with my work, could mark that, and I believe it is because her mother sees and fears it that she has determined to marry her to Rufinus. I will call her down to find out whether she is ready to obey my wishes.”

In a minute or two Aemilia came down from the women’s apartments above.

“My child,” Norbanus said, “I have offered you in marriage to Beric. He has accepted, saving only that you must come to him not in obedience to my orders but of your own free will, since it is the custom of his country that both parties should be equally free of choice. What do you say, my child?”

Aemilia had flushed with a sudden glow of colour as her father began, and stood with downcast eyes until he had finished.

“One moment before you decide, Aemilia,” Beric said. “You know how I am situated, and that at any moment I may be involved in peril or death; that life with me can scarcely be one of ease or luxury, and that even at the best you may be an exile forever from Rome.”

===Chinese===

“我不能举剑反恺撒——他罪再大，待我厚。不为这个，前几天普雷内斯特角斗士起事，我就投奔去了；也是为这个，我不能为罗马这里的起事铺一砖一瓦。我知道斯科帕斯学堂会倾馆相从；别家学堂怨气也大——如今看客见血见惯了，心肠硬得没了怜悯，败者几乎一律处死。至于你的美意，诺巴努斯，我全心感谢；可若不是鲁菲努斯这头逼来，眼下的时世，我不敢把她的命数拴上我的。险太大，前路太黑。我看这座城、城里的人全疯了；尤其眼下，万万不能带她进尼禄的宫。可倘若埃米利娅肯同我订下婚约，把成婚的日子推到时局翻覆之后——她若也情愿，我便欢欢喜喜接受她——在我们不列颠，同高卢一样，闺女自己的婚事，她自己有一票。”

诺巴努斯笑了。“这一票，贝里克，我看你不必担心。自你场内斗狮那天起，你就是她的英雄、她心上的主人。连我这样于本业之外近乎半盲的人也看得出；我信，正因她母亲看出了、怕了，才急急要她嫁鲁菲努斯。我叫她下来，看看她可肯依我。”

一两分钟后，埃米利娅从楼上内宅下来。

“孩子，”诺巴努斯说，“我把你许给贝里克了。他应下了——只是有个讲究：你须不是奉父命嫁他，而是出于自己的心愿；他们国里的规矩，两家择配，一样地自由自主。你怎么说，孩子？”

父亲开口时，埃米利娅脸上早已飞红，垂着眼站到他讲完。

“你允之前，且停一停，埃米利娅，”贝里克说。“你知道我的处境：随时可能陷身祸患死地；跟我，日子断不是安富尊荣；最好处，你也可能永远流放，不得回罗马。”

===Original===

She looked up now. “I love you, Beric,” she said. “I would rather live in a cottage with you for my lord and master than in a palace with any other. I would die with you were there need. Your wishes shall always be my law.”

“That is not the way in Britain,” Beric said, as he drew her to him and kissed her. “The husband is not the lord of his wife, they are friends and equals, and such will we be. There is honour and respect on both sides.”

“It will be but your betrothal at present,” Norbanus said. “Neither Beric nor I would like to see you in the palace of Caesar; but the sponsalia shall take place today, and then he can claim you when he will. Come again this evening, Beric. I will have the conditions drawn up, and some friends shall be here to witness the form of betrothal. This haste, child, is in order to give Beric power to protect you. Were you free, Rufinus might obtain an order from Nero for me to give you to him, but once the conditions are signed they cannot be broken save by your mutual consent; and moreover, Beric can use his influence with the emperor on behalf of his betrothed wife, while so long as you remain under my authority he could scarcely interfere did Nero give his promise to Rufinus.”

“Will my mother be here?”

“She will not, nor do I desire her presence,” Norbanus said decidedly. “She has defied my authority and has gone her own path, and it is only for your sake that I have not divorced her. She comes and she goes as she chooses, but her home is with her family, not here. She has no right by law to a voice in your marriage. You are under my authority and mine alone. It is but right that a good mother should have an influence and a voice as to her daughter’s marriage; but a woman who frequents the saturnalia of Nero has forfeited her mother’s rights. It will be time enough for her to hear of it when it is too late for her to cause trouble. Now do you two go into the garden together, for I have arrangements to make.”

At six o’clock Beric returned to the house. In the atrium were gathered a number of guests; some were members of the family of Norbanus, others were his colleagues in office﻿—all were men of standing and family. Beric was already known to most of them, having met them at suppers at the house. When all were assembled Norbanus left the room, and presently returned leading Aemilia by the hand.

===Chinese===

她抬起头来。“我爱你，贝里克，”她说。“同你守一间茅屋，胜过同别人守一座宫殿。必要的时候，我同你死。你的意愿，永远是我的法。”

“在不列颠不是这个规矩，”贝里克把她揽近，吻她。“丈夫不是妻子的主子；是朋友，是对等的人。我们也这样：两下里全是敬重。”

“眼下只算订婚，”诺巴努斯说。“贝里克同我都不愿见你进恺撒的宫。可婚约（sponsalia）今日便立；立了，他何时要你，何时便可迎娶。今晚再来一趟，贝里克。条款我着人起草，再请几位朋友到场做证。这样急，孩子，是为叫贝里克有权护你。你一日未订，鲁菲努斯便可能求尼禄下谕，叫我把你给他；条款一签，非你两家自愿，谁也毁它不动；而且贝里克可以未婚夫的名分向皇帝进言；你在我名下一天，尼禄若许了鲁菲努斯，他反倒不便插手。”

“母亲在场么？”

“不在，我也不要她在，”诺巴努斯断然说。“她践踏我的权威、自行其是已久；我不休她，全为着你。她来去随她，她的家在她娘家，不在这里。论法，你的婚事她没有发言权；你在我的权威之下，只在我一人之下。好母亲原该在女儿婚事上有她的分量和声音；可一个常年出入尼禄狂欢宴的女人，做母亲的资格早自行丧失尽了。等她知道，已晚到闹不动的地步，正合适。你们两个园子里去吧，我还有安排。”

六点钟，贝里克回到宅中。前厅已聚了一群宾客：有诺巴努斯的族人，有他衙门同僚——都是有身家有门第的人。多数贝里克本就识得，在这家宴上会过。人到齐后，诺巴努斯出去，片刻携着埃米利娅的手回来。

===Original===

“My friends,” he said, “you already know why you are assembled here, namely to be witnesses to the betrothal of my daughter to Beric the Briton. Vitrio, the notary, will read the conditions under which they are betrothed.”

The document was a formal one, and stated that Norbanus gave up his potestas or authority over his daughter Aemilia to Beric, and that he bound himself to complete the further ceremony of marriage either by the religious or civil form as Beric might select whenever the latter should demand it, and that further he agreed to give her on her marriage the sum of three thousand denarii, and to leave the whole of his property to her at his death; while Beric on his part bound himself to complete the ceremonies of marriage whenever called upon by Norbanus to do so, and to pay him at the present time one thousand denarii on the consideration of his signing the present agreement, and on his delivering up to him his authority over his daughter.

“You have heard this document read, Norbanus,” the notary said, when he had concluded the reading. “Do you assent to it? And are you ready to affix your signature to the contract?”

“I am ready,” Norbanus said.

“And you, Beric?”

“I am also ready,” Beric replied.

“Then do you both write your signatures here.”

Both signed, and four of the guests affixed their signatures as witnesses. Norbanus then placed Aemilia’s hand in Beric’s. “You are now betrothed man and wife,” he said. “I transfer to you, Beric, my authority over my daughter; henceforth she is your property to claim as you will.”

A minute later there was a sudden movement at the door, and Lesbia entered in haste. “News has just been brought to me of your intention, Norbanus, and I am here to say that I will not permit this betrothal.”

“You have no voice or authority in the matter,” Norbanus said calmly. “Legal right to interfere you never had. Your moral right you have forfeited. The conditions have been signed. Aemilia is betrothed to Beric.”

Lesbia broke out into passionate reproaches and threats, but Norbanus advanced a step or two towards her, and said with quiet dignity, “I have borne with you for her sake, Lesbia. Now that she belongs to Beric and not to me, I need not restrain my just indignation longer. I return your property to your hands.”

===Chinese===

“诸位朋友，”他说，“今日相请，诸位已知：为小女与不列颠人贝里克订婚作证。公证人维特里奥（Vitrio）宣读订婚条款。”

那是一纸正式文书：诺巴努斯将他对女儿埃米利娅的家主权（potestas）让与贝里克；并承诺待贝里克何时索取，即以宗教或世俗任一形式完成婚礼；许女儿出嫁时予她三千第纳里，自己身后全部财产归她。贝里克一方，则承诺凡诺巴努斯提出，即完成婚礼；并于签署本约、受让其对女儿之权威的此时，付予一千第纳里。

“文书你已听毕，诺巴努斯，”读毕，公证人说。“你可应允？可愿在婚书上署名？”

“愿意。”诺巴努斯说。

“你呢，贝里克？”

“我也愿意。”贝里克答。

“那么两位在此签名。”

两人签罢，四位宾客具名作证。诺巴努斯把埃米利娅的手放进贝里克手中。“你们如今是订了婚的夫妻，”他说。“贝里克，我对女儿的权威交割与你；从今以后，她是你的，随你何时迎娶。”

片刻之后，门口一阵急响，莱斯比亚匆匆闯入。“刚得了信，知道你们要干什么，诺巴努斯；我来说：这门亲事，我不准。”

“此事你既无发言之权，也无置喙之份，”诺巴努斯平静地说。“法律上的干预权，你从来没有；道义上的，你早丧失了。条款已签：埃米利娅同贝里克订了婚。”

莱斯比亚破口大骂，语多恫吓；诺巴努斯朝她迈进一两步，安详而威严：“莱斯比亚，为她，我容了你这些年。如今她归了贝里克，不归我，我这口正当的义愤，也无需再忍。你的财物，我奉还你手。”

===Original===

Lesbia stepped back as if struck. The words were the well known formula by which a Roman divorced his wife. She had not dreamed that Norbanus would summon up resolution to put this disgrace upon her, and to bring upon himself the hostility of her family. Her pride quickly came to her aid.

“Thanks for the release,” she said sarcastically; “far too much of my life has already been wasted on a dotard, and my family will see that the restitution of my property is full and complete: but beware, Norbanus, I am not to be outraged with impunity, and you will learn to your cost that a woman of my family knows how to revenge herself.”

Then turning she passed out of the door, entered her lectica and was carried away.

“I must apologize to you, my friends,” Norbanus said calmly, “for having brought you to be present at an unpleasant family scene, but I had not expected it, and know not through whom Lesbia obtained the news of what was doing here. I suppose one of the slaves carried it to her. But these things trouble not a philosopher; for myself I marvel at my long patience, and feel rejoiced that at last I shall be free to live my own life.”

“You have done well, Norbanus,” one of his colleagues said, “though I know not what Nero will say when he hears of it, for severity among husbands is not popular at present in Rome.”

“I can open my veins as Seneca did,” Norbanus said calmly; “neither death nor exile have any terrors for me. Rome has gone mad, and life for a reasoning being is worthless here.”

“I shall represent the matter to Nero,” Beric said, “and as it is seldom that I ask aught of him, I doubt not he will listen to me. When he is not personally concerned, Nero desires to act justly, and moreover, I think that he can weigh the advantages of the friendship of a faithful guard against that of his boon companions. I will speak to him the first thing in the morning. He frequently comes into the library and reads for an hour. At any rate there is no chance of Lesbia being beforehand with me. It is too late for her to see Rufinus and get him to approach Nero tonight.”

“Let us talk of other matters,” Norbanus said, “all these things are but transitory.” He then began to talk on his favourite topic﻿—the religions of the world, while Beric drew Aemilia, who had been weeping since the scene between her parents, into the tablinum.

===Chinese===

莱斯比亚踉跄后退，像挨了一击。这句话，是罗马人休妻的老例成文。她做梦没想到诺巴努斯能下这个狠心——把这桩羞辱加给她，把她娘家的敌意揽到自己身上。她的傲气立刻回来救驾。

“多谢开释，”她讥诮道。“我这半生，耗在一个老糊涂身上，早耗得太多了；我娘家自会看着，叫我名下的财产一物不少地归还。可你小心，诺巴努斯：我不是白白糟践的；我这样人家的女人会报仇，你早晚要领教。”

说罢转身出门，上轿而去。

“诸位，要抱歉，”诺巴努斯平静地说，“请各位看了一场家务丑戏；实出意外，也不知莱斯比亚从哪里得的信——多半是哪个奴隶传的。这些扰不动哲人；我只惊异自己忍了这样久，且欢喜：到底能过自己的日子了。”

“做得对，诺巴努斯，”一位同僚说，“只是尼禄听了如何，天知道——做丈夫的这样严厉，眼下在罗马可不兴。”

“我可以学塞内加割脉，”诺巴努斯平静地说。“死与流放，于我皆无可惧。罗马疯了；会思考的人，在这里活着不值一文。”

“此事我去面陈尼禄，”贝里克说。“我难得求他一事；他必听我。不关他自身的时候，尼禄原想行事公正；况且，一个忠心护卫的交情同一个酒友的交情孰轻孰重，他掂得出。明早我头一件事便说。他常进书房读一个钟头的书。无论如何，莱斯比亚抢不到我前头——天已这般晚，她见不着鲁菲努斯，更来不及今晚求尼禄。”

“谈点别的吧，”诺巴努斯说，“这些都不过浮云。”他随即谈起他心爱的题目——天下各教；贝里克则把双亲吵过之后一直在垂泪的埃米利娅领进书斋。

===Original===

“It is unlucky to weep on the day of your betrothal, Aemilia.”

“Who could help it, Beric? Besides, as it is not for my own troubles the omen will have no avail. But it is all so strange and so rapid. This morning I was in trouble, alarmed at what my mother told me of her intentions, fearful that my father, who has so long yielded to her, would permit her to have her own way in this also. Then came the great joy when he told me that he would give me to you﻿—that you, who of all men I thought most of, was henceforth to be my lord. Then, just when my happiness was complete, and I was formally bound to you, came my mother. Ennia and I always loved our father most, he was ever thoughtful and kind to us, while even as children our mother did not care for us. As we grew up she cared still less, thinking only of her own pleasures and friends, and leaving us almost wholly in charge of the slaves; but it was not until Ennia was seized as a Christian that I knew how little she loved us. Then she raved and stormed, lamented and wept, not because of the fate of Ennia, not because of the terrible death that awaited her, but because of the disgrace it brought upon herself. Even after she was brought here she scarce came in to see her, and loudly said that it would be best for her to die. Lately, as you know, I have seen little of her; she spends all her time abroad, has defied my father’s authority; and brought grief and trouble upon him. Still, to a daughter it is terrible that her mother should be divorced.”

“Let us not think of it now, Aemilia. Your father has acted, as he always does, rightly and well. I know much more of what is going on than you do, and I can tell you that Lesbia, who was so jealous of the honour of her name when Ennia was concerned, is bringing far greater dishonour upon her name by her own actions. And now let us talk of ourselves. The act you have just done, dear, may bring all sorts of sacrifices upon you. At any moment I may be a fugitive, and, as you know, the families of those who incur Nero’s wrath share in their disgrace; and if I am forced to fly, you too may be obliged to become a fugitive.”

She looked up brightly. “I shall not mind any hardships I suffer for your sake, Beric. Rome is hateful to me since Ennia stood in the arena. I would rather share a hut with you among the savage mountains of the north than a palace here.”

===Chinese===

“订婚的日子哭，不吉利，埃米利娅。”

“谁忍得住呢，贝里克？再说，哭的又不是我自己的祸事，这凶兆落不到我头上。可这一切太快、太奇了。今早我还在愁：听母亲讲她的打算，心里害怕，怕父亲——他让了她那样多年——这一次也拗不过她。随后大喜从天而降：他说把我许给你——我平生想得最多的你，从此做了我的主。随后，我的圆满刚成礼，母亲就闯了进来。恩尼娅同我从来最爱父亲：他待我们事事上心、样样慈爱；母亲呢，我们做孩子时她就不疼我们，长大了她更不放在心上，只想着自己的行乐朋友，把我们几乎全丢给奴隶。可直到恩尼娅以基督徒被拿，我才明白她爱我们爱得多薄。那时她跳着骂着、哭着嚎着——不为恩尼娅的命，不为等着她的惨死，只为加在她自己头上的羞辱。恩尼娅抬回来后，她都极少进房看她，还高声说这孩子死了最好。近来你也知道，我几乎见不着她：她终日在外，践踏父亲的权威，带给他无穷烦恼。话虽如此，做女儿的，眼看母亲被休，终究可怕。”

“现在别想它了，埃米利娅。你父亲行事，一向又对又好。眼下这些事，我知道的比你多：莱斯比亚当日那样看重娘家的体面，如今她自己的行止，正把大得多的羞辱往这体面上堆。说说我们吧。亲爱的，你方才这一诺，说不定要你受种种牺牲。我随时可能亡命；你也知道，凡获罪于尼禄的人，一族都要连坐。我若被迫逃亡，你怕也得跟着逃。”

她仰起脸，亮亮的。“为你受什么苦我都甘心，贝里克。自恩尼娅立在场上那天起，罗马于我就是可恨的。同你在北边荒山里守一间茅屋，强似在这里守一座宫殿。”

===Original===

“I trust that trouble is still far distant, but I shall, as soon as I can, find a retreat where, in case I fall under Nero’s displeasure, you can lie hid until I can send for you.”

“I have such a retreat, Beric. Since Ennia’s death I have seen a good deal of the Christians. Lycoris, you know, was captured at the same time as Ennia, and was put to death by fire; but her daughter, married to a freedman who had purchased her liberty from my father, managed to escape with her husband when the place was surrounded. I have met her several times since. She and her husband are living hidden in the catacombs, where she tells me many of their sect have taken refuge from the persecutions.

“The last time I saw her she said to me, ‘No one’s life is safe in this terrible city, and none, however high in station, can say that they may not require refuge. Should you need an asylum, Aemilia, go to the house of a freedman, one Mincius, living in the third house on the right of a street known as the Narrow one, close behind the amphitheatre at the foot of the Palatine Hill, and knock thrice at the door. When they open, say, ‘In the name of Christ,’ then they will take you in. Tell them that you desire to see me, and that you are the sister of Ennia, the daughter of Norbanus, and they will lead you to us. There is an entrance to the catacombs under the house. As the sister of Ennia you will be warmly received by all there, even although you yourself may not belong to us. The galleries and passages are of a vast extent and known only to us. There is no fear of pursuit there.’ ”

“That is good news, Aemilia; it is sad that, but an hour betrothed, we are forced to think of refuges, but it will be happiness to me to know that if danger threatens, you have a place of retreat. You see this ring; Nero himself gave it me; mark it well, so that you may know it again. It is a figure of Mercury carved on an amethyst. When you receive it, by night or day, tarry not a moment, but wrap yourself in a sombre mantle like that of a slave, and hie you to this refuge you speak of; but first see your father, tell him where you are going and why, so that he may fly too, if he choose.”

“He will not do that,” Aemilia said, “and how can I leave him?”

===Chinese===

“但愿祸事还远。可我要尽快备下一处退身之地：万一我失宠于尼禄，你可以先藏在那里，等我接你。”

“这样的地方我有，贝里克。恩尼娅死后，我同基督徒往来不少。你知道，吕科里斯同恩尼娅一同被拿，受了火刑；她的女儿——嫁了我父亲手下一个赎身获释奴的——围屋那夜，同丈夫逃了出来。后来我见过她几面。夫妻俩藏在地下墓窟里；她说，他们一宗避难的，那里收了许多。

“上一回见她，她对我说：‘这座可怕的城里，谁的命都不稳；位分再高，也不敢说自己用不着藏身。你若要避难，埃米利娅，去一个获释奴明基乌斯（Mincius）家——帕拉蒂尼山脚、大剧场背后那条叫窄街的街，右手第三家；门上叩三下。开门后说：“奉基督的名。”他们便收你。就说你要见我，说你是恩尼娅的姐姐、诺巴努斯的女儿，他们会领你到我这里。那宅子底下就有地下墓窟的入口。你是恩尼娅的姐姐，那里人人会热切迎你，哪怕你自己不入我们。廊道地洞宽大无边，只有我们识得路径。那里不怕追捕。’”

“这是天大的好消息，埃米利娅。订婚才一个钟头，就得商量避难所，令人心酸；可知道危难临头你有去处，于我就是安乐。你看这枚戒指：尼禄亲手赐的；记熟它。紫水晶上刻着墨丘利像。这戒指到你手上——不分昼夜——一刻也不停留：裹上一件奴仆样的深色斗篷，直奔你说的那处避难地；只是先见父亲一面，告诉他你去哪里、为何去，他若肯，也一并走。”

“他不会走的，”埃米利娅说，“我又怎么丢得下他？”

===Original===

“You must leave him because you belong to me, Aemilia, and because you are acting on my orders. The danger to you is far greater than to him. You are my wife, he only my father in law, and they would strike at me first through you. Besides, there are other reasons. Your father is a Roman of the old type, and like Seneca and Plautus, and others of the same school, will deem it no loss when the time comes to quit life. However, you will tell him of the danger, and he must make his own choice. I shall beg him to hand to you at once the money which I placed in his care now a year ago. Do you hand it over to the woman you speak of, and ask her to hide it away in the caves till you ask for it again; these Christians are to be trusted. I have much money besides, for Nero is lavishly generous, and it would anger him to refuse his bounty. This money I have placed in several hands, some in Rome, some elsewhere, so that if forced to fly I can at any rate obtain some of my store without having to run into danger.”

“One more question, Beric. Should I ever have to take refuge among the Christians, and like Ennia come to love their doctrines, would you be angered if I joined their sect? If you would I will not listen to them, but will tell them that I cannot talk or think of these things without my husband’s consent.”

“You are free to do as you like, Aemilia. Since Ennia died I have resolved upon the first opportunity to study the doctrines of these people, for truly it must be a wonderful religion that enables those who profess it to meet a cruel death not only without fear but with joy. You know Ennia said we should meet again, and I think she meant that I, too, should become a Christian. Ask the woman if I also, as a last resource, may take refuge among them.”

“I will ask her, Beric; but I am sure they will gladly receive you. Have you not already risked your life to save a Christian?”

The other guests having now left, Norbanus joined them, and Beric told him of the arrangements they had made in case of danger. He warmly approved of them.

===Chinese===

“你必须丢下他，因为你属于我，埃米利娅，因为这是奉我的令。你的险比他大得多：你是我的妻，他不过我的岳丈；他们要打击我，先从你下手。还有别的缘故：你父亲是旧式的罗马人，同塞内加、普劳图斯那一路人一样，时辰一到，离世不以为失。可这危险总要告诉他，去留由他自择。我要请他即刻把一年前存他那里的钱交割给你；你转交你说的那位妇人，请她藏进洞里，等你再取。这些基督徒靠得住。此外我另有许多钱——尼禄赏起来没有节制，辞他的赏反惹他恼——分散存在几处，罗马有，别处也有；真要亡命，至少有一份不用冒险就取得着。”

“再问一句，贝里克。倘若我真要在基督徒中避难，也像恩尼娅那样爱上他们的道理，我入了他们的教，你恼不恼？你若恼，我就不听他们讲，告诉他们：没有丈夫的允许，这些事我不能谈、不能想。”

“你随意，埃米利娅。自恩尼娅死后，我打定主意，一有机会便研读这些人的道理：那必是一门奇妙的教，否则信它的人迎着酷死，不但无惧，而且欢喜。你知道恩尼娅说我们要重逢；我想她的意思是：我也要成为一个基督徒。问问那位妇人，这末路一条，可也容我避进去。”

“我问，贝里克；可我笃定他们巴不得收你。你不早为救一个基督徒舍过命了？”

宾客们已散，诺巴努斯过来；贝里克把危急时的这些安排告诉他，他极口赞成。

===Original===

“It will be a relief to me as to you, Beric, to know that Aemilia’s safety is provided for. As for myself, fate has no terrors for me; but for you and her it is different. She is yours now, for although but betrothed she is virtually your wife. You have but to take her by the hand and to declare her your wife in the presence of witnesses, and all is done. There is, it is true, a religious ceremony in use only among the wealthier classes, but this is rather an occasion for pomp and feasting, and is by no means needful, especially as you have no faith in the Roman gods. What are the rites among your own people, Beric?”

“We simply take a woman by the hand and declare her our wife. Then there is feasting, and the bride is carried home, and there is the semblance of a fight, the members of her family making a show of preventing us; but this is no part of the actual rite, which is merely public assent on both sides. And now I must be going. Nero will be feasting for a long time yet; but Boduoc has been on guard for many hours and I must relieve him. Farewell, Norbanus; we have been preparing for the worst, but I trust we shall escape misfortune. Farewell, my Aemilia!” and kissing her tenderly Beric strode away to the palace of Nero.

He had not seen Boduoc since early morning, and the latter, standing on guard outside the private entrance to Nero’s apartments, greeted his arrival, “Why, Beric, I began to fear that some harm had befallen you. I came in this morning after the bath and found you had gone out. I returned again at six and found your chamber again empty, but saw that you had returned during my absence; I went on guard, and here have I been for four hours listening to all that foolish singing and laughter inside. How Caesar, who has the world at his command, can spend his time with actors and buffoons, is more than I can understand. But what has kept you?”

As there was no fear of his voice being heard through the heavy hangings, Beric, to Boduoc’s intense surprise, related the events of the day.

===Chinese===

“埃米利娅的退路有了着落，于你于我都是一宽，贝里克。至于我自己，命运吓不着我；你同她就不同了。她如今是你的了——虽说只是订婚，实际上已是你的妻：你只须携她的手、当众宣布她是你的妻，礼就成了。富家另有一套宗教婚礼，那不过是铺张宴乐的由头，全非必需——何况你本不信罗马诸神。你们本族的婚仪是怎样的，贝里克？”

“我们携起女子的手，宣布她是我们的妻。然后饮宴，新娘被抱回家，还有一场假打的过场——她家里人装模作样拦我们；那不算礼的本体，礼不过是两下当众的允诺。我得走了。尼禄的宴还长；波杜克守了几个钟头，我得换他。再会，诺巴努斯；我们备了最坏的，可我信灾祸会绕过我们。再会，我的埃米利娅！”贝里克深深吻了她，大步回尼禄宫去。

他从清晨就没见着波杜克。立在尼禄私室门外守卫的波杜克迎他：“咦，贝里克，我都开始怕你出了事。今早浴后进来，见你出去了；六点再回来，屋里又空着，却看出你中间回来过；我上了岗，一站四个钟头，光听里头那些蠢唱傻笑。恺撒坐拥天下，偏把光阴耗在戏子小丑身上，我到死不懂。什么事绊住你了？”

隔着厚重的帷幔不怕人听见，贝里克便把这一天的事讲了，波杜克听得目瞪口呆。

===Original===

“So you have married a Roman girl, Beric! Well, I suspected what would come of it when you spent half your time at the house of Norbanus. I would rather that you had married one of our own maidens; but as I see no chance of our return to Britain for years, if ever, one could hardly expect you to wait for that. At any rate she is the best of the Roman maidens I have seen. She neither dyes her hair nor paints her face, and although she lacks stature, she is comely, and is always bright and pleasant when I have accompanied you there. I am inclined to feel half jealous that you have another to love you besides myself, but I will try and not grudge her a share of your affection.”

“Well, hand me your sword, Boduoc, and betake yourself to your bed. I will remain on guard for the next four hours, or until the feasting is over. Nero often opens the hangings the last thing to see if we are watchful, and he likes to see me at my post. I wish to find him in a good temper in the morning.”

The next morning, to Beric’s satisfaction, Nero came into the library early. Chiton, as was his custom, retired at once.

“I was inspired last night, Beric,” the emperor said. “Listen to these verses I composed at the table;” and he recited some stanzas in praise of wine.

“I am no great judge of these matters, Caesar,” Beric said; “but they seem to me to be admirable indeed. How could it be otherwise, when even the Greeks awarded you the crown for your recitations at their contests? Yesterday was a fortunate day for me, also, Caesar, for Norbanus betrothed his daughter to me.”

The emperor’s face clouded, and Beric hastened to say:

“There is no talk of marriage at present, Caesar, for marriage would interfere with my duties to you. Therefore it is only when you have no longer an occasion for my services that the betrothal will be converted into marriage. My first duty is to you, and I shall allow nothing to interfere with that.”

===Chinese===

“你竟娶了个罗马姑娘，贝里克！唔，你在诺巴努斯家泡掉一半光阴时，我就猜到有这一日。我原愿你娶咱们自家的姑娘；可看这光景，回国不知要等多少年，兴许永世回不去，总不能叫你等那个。好在罗马姑娘里，她是顶好的：头发不染，脸不搽粉；个子是小了些，人却齐整；我随你去时，她总是又爽快又和气。你除了我又多一个人爱，我都快嫉妒了；不过我尽力，不去分她的醋。”

“好，把剑交我，波杜克，睡觉去。接下来四个钟头我来守，守到宴散。尼禄临睡常要掀开帷幔看我们醒不醒；他爱看我立在岗上。明早我要见他一副好脾气。”

次晨，如贝里克所愿，尼禄早早进了书房。基顿照例即刻退出。

“昨夜我诗兴大发，贝里克，”皇帝说。“听听这几节，席上成的；”他吟了几节咏酒的诗。

“于诗我一知半解，恺撒，”贝里克说；“依我看，确实绝妙。还能怎样呢——希腊人赛会上都把朗诵的桂冠给了您。昨天于我也是吉日，恺撒：诺巴努斯把女儿许给我了。”

皇帝的脸沉了下来；贝里克忙说：

“眼下不议婚娶，恺撒——成了婚，碍我为您当差。所以只有到您用不着我的一天，婚约才成婚礼。我头一样职分在您；这一点，什么也搅不了。”

===Original===

Nero’s face cleared. “That is right,” he said graciously. “You might have married better, seeing that you enjoy my favour; but perhaps it is as well as it is. Norbanus is a worthy man and a good official, although his ideas are old fashioned; but it is reported of him that he thinks of nothing but his work, and mixes himself up in no way in politics, living the life almost of a recluse. It was one of his daughters you championed in the arena. She died soon afterwards, I heard. Has he other children?”

“Only the maiden I am betrothed to, Caesar. He is now alone, for his wife has long been altogether separated from him, being devoted to gaiety and belonging to a family richer and more powerful than his, and looking down upon her husband as a mere bookworm. He has borne with her neglect and disobedience to his wishes for a long time, and has shown, as it seemed to me, far too great a weakness in exerting his authority; but his patience has at last failed, and when yesterday, in defiance of him, she would have interfered to prevent my betrothal to his daughter, he divorced her.”

“Divorce is the fashion,” Nero said carelessly. “I know his wife Lesbia, she has frequently been present with members of her family at my entertainments. She is a fine woman, and I wonder not that she and the recluse her husband did not get on well together. She will soon be consoled.”

“I have mentioned it to you, Caesar, because she is a revengeful woman, and might cause rumours unfavourable to her husband to be reported to you. He is the most simple and single minded of men, and his thoughts are entirely occupied, as you say, with the duties of his office and with the learned book upon which he has long been engaged; but although a philosopher in his habits he holds aloof from all parties, and even in his own family never discusses public affairs. Had it been otherwise, you may be sure that I, your majesty’s attendant and guard, should have abstained from visiting his house.”

“I know this to be the case, Beric. Naturally, when I first placed you near my person, I was interested in knowing who were your intimates, and caused strict inquiries to be made as to the household of Norbanus and his associates; all that I heard was favourable to him, and convinced me that he was in no way a dangerous person.”

===Chinese===

尼禄的脸色放晴了。“这就对了，”他优容地说。“凭你的宠，原可攀得更高的亲；不过这样也许正好。诺巴努斯是端方人、好官，虽然见解陈旧；据报，此人心无旁骛、只知公事，政事一概不沾，日子过得同隐士差不多。你在场上护过的，就是他的一个女儿吧？听说不久就死了。他还有儿女么？”

“只有同我订婚的这一位，恺撒。他如今孤身：妻子早已同他分居——她专务行乐，娘家又比他豪富有势，只把丈夫当一个书蠹看。她的怠慢违忤，他容了许多年，我看他把权威使得太软了；可他的耐性到底尽了。昨天她竟敢违着他、要拦我们订婚，他把她休了。”

“休妻是风气，”尼禄随口说。“他那位莱斯比亚我认得，常随她家人赴我的宴。是位体面的夫人；她同她那位隐士丈夫过不到一处，我毫不奇怪。她不久就会有人安慰的。”

“我提这一句，恺撒，因为这女人记仇，可能造出对她丈夫不利的言语，传到您耳中。他是最简朴最专一的人，如您所说，一心在公务、在他著了多年的那部书上；他虽是哲人做派，却远着一切党派，连自己家里也从不谈公务。不然，您陛下的近侍护卫贝里克，必不敢登他的门。”

“情形我尽知，贝里克。初把你放在身边时，我自然要查你的至交是谁，曾严令细查诺巴努斯的家门和交往；所得于他全然有利，我确知他绝非危险人物。”

===Original===

Nero left the room, and returned shortly bearing a casket. “Give these jewels to your betrothed, Beric, as a present from Caesar to the wife of his faithful guard.”

Beric thanked the emperor in becoming terms, and in the afternoon carried the jewels, which were of great value, to Aemilia.

“They are a fortune in themselves,” he said; “in case of danger, take them from the casket and conceal them in your garments. No one could have been more cordial than Nero was this morning; but he is fickle as the wind, and when Rufinus and others of his boon companions obtain his ear his mood may change altogether.”

===Chinese===

尼禄出去，片刻捧回一只小匣。“把这几件首饰送给你的未婚妻，贝里克——恺撒送给他忠心护卫之妻的礼。”

贝里克得体地谢了恩。下午，他把这匣价值不菲的首饰送到埃米利娅手上。

“这一匣本身就是一份家私，”他说。“危急时，把它们从匣里取出，藏进衣裳。今早的尼禄，再和气不过；可他像风一样无常；哪天鲁菲努斯同那班酒友凑上他的耳朵，他的脸色就会全变。”

## XVIII / 十八
===Original===

The Outbreak

It was not long, indeed, before Beric found that hostile influences were at work. Nero was not less friendly in his manner, but he more than once spoke to him about Aemilia.

“I hear,” he said one day, “that your betrothed is very beautiful Beric.”

“She is very fair, Caesar,” Beric replied coldly.

“I know not how it is that I have not seen her at court,” Nero continued.

“Her tastes are like those of her father,” Beric said. “She goes but seldom abroad, and has long had the principal care of her father’s household.”

“But you should bring her now,” Nero persisted. “The wife of one of the officials of the palace should have a place at our entertainments.”

“She is not at present my wife, Caesar, she is but my betrothed; and as you have yourself excused me from attendance at all entertainments, it would be unseemly for her, a Roman maiden, though betrothed to me, to appear there.”

“There are plenty of other Roman maidens who appear there,” Nero said pettishly. Beric made no reply, and the subject was not again alluded to at that time; but the emperor returned to it on other occasions, and Beric at last was driven to refuse point blank.

===Chinese===

祸起

果然没过多久，贝里克便察觉有人在与他为敌。尼禄待他的态度并不稍减亲热，却不止一次同他谈起埃米利娅。

“听说你的未婚妻生得很美，贝里克。”一天他说。

“她确实美丽，恺撒。”贝里克冷冷地答。

“不知怎么，我竟没在宫里见过她。”尼禄又说。

“她的性情随她父亲，”贝里克说，“轻易不出门，许久以来都是她一人操持父亲的家务。”

“可你如今该带她来了，”尼禄执意道，“宫中官员之妻，在我们的宴会上理应有她一席。”

“她眼下还不是我的妻子，恺撒，不过是未婚妻；陛下既已免了我出席一切宴会，她虽与我订了婚，究竟是个罗马闺秀，在那里露面总不相宜。”

“在那里露面的罗马闺秀多着呢。”尼禄怏怏地说。贝里克不再作声，那一回话头也就撂下了；可皇帝后来又提过几回，贝里克末了只得一口回绝。

===Original===

“I am your majesty’s guard,” he said. “I watch you at night as well as by day, and, as I have told your majesty, I cannot perform my duties properly if I have to be present at your entertainments. I should not permit my wife or my betrothed to be present in public unless I were by her side. Your majesty took me for what I was, a simple Briton, who could be relied upon as a guard, because I had neither friends nor family in Rome, and was content to live a simple and quiet life. I am willing to abstain from marriage in order that I may still do my service as heretofore; but if I have to attend entertainments, you cannot rely upon my constant vigilance. It is for you to choose, Caesar, whether you most require vigilant guards, who could be trusted as standing aloof from all, or the addition of two persons to the crowds you entertain. I am sure, Caesar,” he went on as the emperor made no reply, “it is not yourself who is now speaking to me; it is Rufinus, formerly a suitor for the hand of the daughter of Norbanus, who has been whispering into your ear and abusing the favour you show him. He dare not show his animosity to me openly, for one who has conquered a lion would make but short work of him. Your majesty, I pray you, let not the word of men like this come between yourself and one you know to be faithful to you.”

“You are right, Beric,” Nero said. “I will press you no farther; it was but a passing thought. I had heard of the beauty of your betrothed, and thought I would see if she were as fair as report makes her; but since you do not wish it to be so, it shall not be spoken of again.”

===Chinese===

“我是陛下的护卫，”他说，“昼夜守着您。我早已奏明陛下，若还要出席宴会，便当不好差。我的妻子、未婚妻，断不能在公开场合露面，除非我亲自在她身边。陛下收留我时，看我本是个淳朴的不列颠人，在罗马无亲无故，甘愿过简朴安静的日子，靠得住，才用我做护卫。为了照旧当差，我情愿终身不娶；可若要我赴宴，我便不能保证时时警醒。恺撒，何去何从，凭您抉择：您是要可靠的护卫——一个与众人绝不相涉、可以信托的护卫，还是要给宴席再添两个人。我敢说，恺撒，”他见皇帝不答，又接下去，“此刻同我说话的，不是您本人，是鲁菲努斯——从前向诺巴努斯家女儿求过婚的那个人。他在您耳边进谗，滥用您给他的恩宠。他不敢明着同我为敌，因为打死过狮子的人，收拾他不过一举手。陛下，我求您，别让这等小人的话，隔开了您和您明知忠心于您的人。”

“你说得对，贝里克，”尼禄说，“我不再逼你，不过一时起念罢了。我听说你未婚妻貌美，想看看她是否真如传闻那般标致；既然你不愿如此，此后便不再提了。”

===Original===

But Beric knew enough of Nero to be aware that, like most weak men, he was obstinate, and that Rufinus and his friends would not allow the matter to drop. Every preparation was therefore made for sudden flight. Aemilia was warned on no account to trust any message she might receive purporting to be from him, and the Britons in the palace, who were heartily sick of their monotonous duty, were told to hold themselves in readiness for action. Beric knew that he could depend on the slave who had been assigned to him as an attendant. He was not the man who had at first served him, and who, as Beric doubted not, had acted as a spy upon him. When it was found that there was nothing to discover this man had been removed for other work, and a slave boy of some seventeen years old had taken his place. To him Beric had behaved with great kindness, and the lad was deeply attached to him. He had several times taken notes and messages to the house of Norbanus, and Beric told Aemilia that when it became necessary to send her the ring, he should probably entrust it to him.

A week later Boduoc was on guard at ten in the evening. In the distant banqueting hall he could hear sounds of laughter and revelry, and knowing the nature of these feasts he muttered angrily to himself that he, a Briton, should be standing there while such things were being done within. Suddenly he heard a step approaching the hangings. They were drawn back, and one of the court attendants said, “Caesar requires the attendance of Beric the Briton in the banqueting hall.”

“I will tell him,” Boduoc said. “He will come directly.” Beric was sitting reading when Boduoc entered and gave the message.

“This means mischief, Boduoc,” he said. “I have never been sent for before to one of these foul carousals. Philo, come hither!”

===Chinese===

然而贝里克深知尼禄的为人：他同多数懦弱的人一样，偏偏执拗；鲁菲努斯一伙也断不会让这事就此罢休。于是出逃的准备一一停当。贝里克告诫埃米利娅，无论收到什么口信，凡托他名义的，一概不可轻信；宫里的不列颠人早对这单调的差事腻透了，都得了话，随时准备动手。贝里克知道派来伺候他的那个奴隶靠得住。起初伺候他的并不是这个，那人，贝里克毫不怀疑，是奉命监视他的；后来见实在查不出什么，便被调去做别的活计，换了个十七岁上下的奴隶小厮菲洛（Philo）。贝里克待这孩子极好，孩子也死心塌地贴着他。他已几回替贝里克往诺巴努斯府上递话送信；贝里克告诉埃米利娅，一旦需要把戒指送去，多半就托付给他。

一星期后的一晚，十点钟，轮到博多克值夜。远处宴厅里传来欢笑喧闹之声。他深知那类宴席的底细，一面暗自恼恨：自己一个不列颠人，竟站在那里，听着里头干这等勾当。忽然他听见有脚步向帷幔这边走来。帷幔一掀，一名宫廷侍从说：“恺撒要不列颠人贝里克即刻赴宴。”

“我去传话，”博多克说，“他即刻就来。”贝里克正坐着读书，博多克进来传了话。

“这是要出事了，博多克，”他说，“从没叫我去过那种污糟的酒宴。菲洛，过来！”

===Original===

The lad, who was lying on a mat by the door, rose. “Philo, take this ring. Follow me to the door of the banqueting room, and stand behind the hangings. If I say ‘Run, Philo!’ carry out the orders that I have before given you. Speed first to the room where the Britons sleep, and tell them to arm and come up by the private stairs to my room instantly. They know the way. They are then to pass on through the passage and the next room and wait behind the hangings, when Boduoc will give them orders. Directly you have given my message speed to the house of Norbanus, and demand in my name to see the lady Aemilia. If she has retired to her room she must be roused. If the slaves make difficulty, appeal to Norbanus himself. He will fetch her down to you. Give her this ring, and say the time has come.”

“I will do it, my lord. Where am I to join you afterwards?”

“I shall take the road to the Alban Hills first; I think that if you are speedy, you may be on the Alban road before me. Now follow me. Boduoc, do you come as far as the hangings of the banqueting room, and stand there with Philo. You will be able to hear what passes within. Do not enter unless I call you. Bring my sword with you.”

Beric passed through two or three large apartments and then entered the banqueting room. It was ablaze with lights. A dozen men and as many women, in the scantiest costumes, lay on couches along each side of the table. All were crowned with chaplets of flowers, and were half covered with roses, of which showers had fallen from above upon them. Nero lay on a couch at the end of the table; his features were flushed with wine. Beric repressed the exclamation of indignant disgust that rose to his lips, and walking calmly up to Nero said coldly, “I am told that you want me, Caesar.”

“I do, my fighter of lions,” Nero said unsteadily. “I would see this paragon of whom Rufinus tells me, whom you guard so jealously from my eyes. Send and fetch her hither. She will be a worthy queen of our revels.”
